- `collect_index_chain` (`cps.rs`) — the array-side chain-walker, still needed for the `a[i][j]`/two-bracket-pairs *array* case (`StmtKind::Assign`'s own multi-index `Store` collapsing, and the array-read `PrimOp::Load` path) — now walks whole *groups* rather than single indices, reversing group order (not each group's own internal order) before flattening; the write-side collapsing this exists for is a real correctness requirement, not just an optimization (see the module's own "Arrays" doc comment), so it's kept exactly as conservative as before rather than risking the never-actually-exercised "partial `Load` producing a real intermediate array value, read further" path.
- `monomorphize.rs::derive_impl_instantiation` — refactored from `arg_ids: &[NodeId]` to `arg_tys: &[Ty]` directly (it only ever did `node_types.get(id)` per id; the synthesized idx-array has no real `NodeId` of its own to look up) — a small, safe simplification, the same `NodeId`→primitive-value pattern already used this session for `resolve_call`/`infer_algebra_call`.

**A real, separate subtlety, found by direct testing while writing the negative test**: `dispatch_algebra_call`/`matching_impls`'s own target-matching tuple only ever includes an algebra's *type* generics (`Container`, `Elem`) — a *const* generic like `K` is deliberately excluded (`infer_algebra_call`'s own `resolved_generics` loop only pushes `GenericParam::Type` entries), bound separately via ordinary `unify_at` against the impl's declared parameter type instead. So ordinary immediate dispatch accepts `m[0,0,0]` (3 indices on a `Matrix`) freely — nothing about `K` blocks it there — and the real rejection only surfaces one pass later, at monomorphization (`derive_impl_instantiation`'s own *full* signature match, which does see `Matrix`'s own literal `[i32;2]` and fails to unify it against a `[i32;3]` query). `cleave::dump::dump_program` (ordinary inference alone) wrongly reports such a program as accepted; `cleave::monomorphize::dump_monomorphized` (which `main.rs`'s own `check_type_errors` gate already uses) is what actually catches it — `over_indexing_a_matrix_is_rejected` uses the latter specifically for this reason.

Verified via: `cleave/tests/lower.rs` (the new one-node-per-bracket-group shape, plus a `separate_bracket_pairs_still_nest` regression confirming `a[i][j]` still desugars as before), `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs` (the matmul/`Ring<Matrix>`/rank-3-`Tensor` tests reworked to read back through real `mc[i,j]`/`t[i,j,k]` sugar instead of raw `mlir::tensor::extract`, plus `over_indexing_a_matrix_is_rejected` and `over_indexing_a_plain_array_is_rejected`), `cleave/tests/print.rs` (updated to the new direct-printing shape, `a[i, j]` rather than re-nested `a[i][j]`). Full `cargo test --workspace` green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected; `examples/tensor_demo.cleave` reworked to use the real sugar throughout, re-verified numerically end to end.

### `check_pending_constraints`'s output-only-generic gate, and `ExprKind::Index`'s own missing deferred-resolution path

Two stacked bugs, found and fixed together, both surfaced by trying to remove the explicit annotation workarounds the two items above had needed (`let mc: Matrix<f32,2,2> = matmul(...)`, `let x: f32 = v[i];`) — a bare `print(v[i])` or `mc[i,j]` right after `let mc = matmul(...)` used to panic in `cps.rs::resolve_call` ("could not resolve call") instead of compiling.

**Bug 1 — `check_pending_constraints` required *every* position in a deferred `Constraint` to already be concrete, including an algebra's *output-only* generic** (`Index<Container,Elem,K>`'s own `Elem`, `MatMul<A,B,C>`'s own `C`), which by design is never independently pinned by anything — the only place it's ever supposed to get bound is inside a successful *dispatch*, exactly the way the already-correct immediate-dispatch path (`infer_algebra_call`) already treats it (only its *gating*, input-appearing generics need to be concrete before `dispatch_algebra_call` — the committing match — even runs). `check_pending_constraints` had no equivalent split, so a deferred call's own output-only position could never be satisfied, and the whole constraint was silently dropped forever (`std::mem::take`), never erroring, never resolving.

**The fix**: `Constraint` gained a `gating_indices: Vec<usize>` field (`Constraint::all_gating` covers every existing single-target bound-check push site — `Num`/`Int`/`Float`/`Complex`/`for`-loop/`Index`'s-own-per-index-`Int`, all trivially "every position gates" — a pure rename, no behavior change) — `infer_algebra_call`'s own deferred pushes record the *real* gating subset alongside its existing `gating: Vec<Ty>` computation. `check_pending_constraints` now gates readiness on `gating_indices` only; once satisfied, if some non-gating position is *still* a bare `Ty::Var`, it calls `dispatch_algebra_call` (committing) instead of `has_matching_impl` (non-committing, existence-only — the actual root cause: it never bound anything) — otherwise (everything already concrete, the ordinary bound-check shape) the exact original path is unchanged, including the `Ty::Const`-width bridging and the `Complex`-widening special case.

**Bug 2 — `ExprKind::Index` had no deferred-resolution mechanism at all** for "I don't yet know if my base will end up an array or an `Index`-algebra target," found while testing bug 1's own fix: `matmul(ma,mb)`'s inferred type is a *bare, structurally unconstrained* `Ty::Var` (unlike `Vector(data:...)`'s own type, which is *always* `Ty::App("Vector",...)` from the moment it's constructed, even with an abstract inner element type) — until `MatMul`'s own dispatch actually runs. So `mc[0,0]`'s own `resolved_base` was `Ty::Var(_)`, and the *existing* `ExprKind::Index` code treated that identically to `is_placeholder` (a genuine dead end, e.g. `<unresolved-call:...>`): an immediate dead `Ty::Con("<not-yet-inferred>")`, with **no constraint or deferred work registered at all** — nothing ever revisited the node once `mc`'s type later resolved, since a `Ty::Con` is inert under `Subst::apply`, unlike a real `Ty::Var`.

**The fix mirrors `FieldAccess`'s own already-correct `pending_field_accesses`/`check_pending_field_accesses` mechanism exactly** (`Index`'s own old doc comments already said it *should*, but never actually did): a new `PendingIndex { base, base_span, index_tys, index_spans, result, span }` list — indices are still inferred (and `Int`-constrained) immediately either way, mirroring `PendingMethodCall`'s own "arguments never depend on the base" split; only "what does indexing mean" is deferred. `Ty::Var(_)` now mints a fresh real `Ty::Var` and queues a `PendingIndex` (returned immediately, so later unification can still pin it down) instead of the dead placeholder; `is_placeholder` keeps the old immediate-dead-end behavior, unchanged. `resolve_index` (extracted from the old inline match arms, same shared-helper precedent as `resolve_field_access`) is reused by both the immediate path and `check_pending_indices`.

**A third issue, found empirically while wiring the two together, not predicted in advance**: `check_pending_constraints`/`check_pending_indices` can depend on *each other's* output across a real chain — `print(matmul(ma,mb)[0,0])` needs `MatMul`'s own `C` bound (`check_pending_constraints`) *before* `mc[0,0]`'s own `Index` dispatch can resolve (`check_pending_indices`), which in turn needs to complete *before* `print`'s own deferred `Constraint` — queued right behind `MatMul`'s in the very same `Vec`, processed in one linear pass — can be satisfied. One back-to-back call of each wasn't enough: `check_pending_constraints`'s own single pass would reach `Print`'s entry, find it not yet ready, and — before this fix — permanently drop it, since neither method ever *requeued* an entry that wasn't ready, only ever a hard "give up now." Fixed by making both requeue (not drop) a not-yet-ready entry, and adding `check_pending_constraints_and_indices`, which loops both to a real fixpoint (terminates because each round either strictly shrinks the combined pending count or stalls) — replacing all three existing call sites (`infer.rs`'s own inherent-impl-group tail and `finish_fn`, plus `callgraph.rs`'s whole-program group pass, a call site the original two-part plan for this item had initially missed). On a genuine stall (nothing left in this function will ever pin a base down, e.g. `fn f(a) { a[0] }` with `a` never otherwise constrained), any leftover `PendingIndex` is collapsed to the same dead placeholder `check_pending_field_accesses`'s own "give up" case already uses — needed to avoid a real regression, found directly by this project's own pre-existing `indexing_an_unresolved_base_defers_as_not_yet_inferred` test: a bare requeue-forever would have let a genuinely-unresolvable function signature escape `check_no_placeholder`'s downstream safety net instead of being caught by it.

Verified via: two real end-to-end JIT tests in `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs` (`print_of_an_unannotated_index_result_no_longer_panics`, `print_of_an_unannotated_matmul_index_result_no_longer_panics` — both register `print_f32` for real and check the JIT-executed output, not just that the module verifies). Both the matmul JIT test and `examples/tensor_demo.cleave` had every workaround annotation removed as the actual end-to-end proof, re-verified numerically. Full `cargo test --workspace` green throughout, including the one pre-existing test (`indexing_an_unresolved_base_defers_as_not_yet_inferred`) that caught the stall-collapse regression before it shipped.

### `stdlib/nn` — activation functions and reductions (toward simple ML)

The smaller, incremental half of "toward simple ML" (the bigger half, auto-diff via the e-graph, stays a separate, unstarted item) — a single artificial neuron's forward pass (dot product, bias, nonlinearity) needed activation functions (`relu`/`sigmoid`/`tanh`) and a way to reduce a `Vector<T,N>` down to a scalar (`sum`/`mean`/`max_of`), neither of which existed yet. Both are ordinary stdlib additions, no new compiler mechanism — the same `algebra` + `mlir::...`-body shape every other primitive in this project already uses.

**Two patterns, already proven this session, cover everything needed**: elementwise ops applied *directly* to a tensor-backed `Vector` value already work exactly like `Ring<Vector<T,N>>::add`'s own `arith.addf` does — confirmed directly (not just assumed by analogy) that `math.exp`/`math.tanh`/`arith.maximumf` broadcast over a `tensor<Nxf32>` operand the identical way, via `relu_sigmoid_tanh_compute_correctly_on_vectors` (`cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`); reductions need no MLIR-level op at all — `sum`/`mean`/`max_of` are plain cleave source, an ordinary `for` loop plus `Vector`'s own real multi-index `Index` read (this session's own prior item).

**`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave` is the first stdlib crate to reference another stdlib crate's own types** (`use vector;`) — and this surfaced a real, pre-existing, already-self-documented limitation: `driver.rs`'s own `compile` doc comment already stated `use` resolution was "deliberately one level deep only... a loaded crate's own `use` statements are not themselves followed." Confirmed directly: `nn.cleave`'s own internal `use vector;` line was functionally inert — any program wanting `nn`'s `Vector`-based impls had to `use vector;` *itself*, alongside `use nn;` (`stdlib/convert/convert.cleave` had already independently discovered and documented the identical limitation, for the identical reason). Fixed in a same-session follow-up — see "Real transitive `use` resolution," below.

**Two real, recurring rough-edge bugs hit again, both already-documented instances of the same family, not new categories**:
- **A bare `mlir::...` call nested directly as *another* raw `mlir::...` call's own argument** (`mlir::math::exp(mlir::arith::negf(x))`) leaves the *inner* call's result a permanently-unresolved type variable reaching codegen — an explicit `let e: f32 = mlir::math::exp(...)` pins `exp`'s own result, but never reaches back into `negf`'s result nested *inside* it, since `exp` itself (a raw op call) imposes no type requirement on its own argument. One level deeper than the previously-documented shape ("an intermediate `let` feeding only into further `mlir::` calls needs an explicit annotation") — found directly, testing `sigmoid` in isolation, not predicted. Fixed the same way: every intermediate raw-op result gets its own explicit `let ...: T = ...` binding, none nested directly as another raw call's argument.
- **A bare const generic read as an ordinary body value, handed directly to a raw op whose operand type differs from its result type** (`mlir::arith::sitofp(N)`, `i32 -> T`) hits the exact same "bare-literal-operand-vs-`mlir::...`-result-type heuristic gap" already tracked below (`arith.index_cast` hit it three times before this) — a native LLVM cast-assertion crash, not a clean error. Same workaround as every previous instance: `mlir::arith::addi(N, 0)` first, to force a genuine computed value before handing it to `sitofp`.

**`mean`'s own division originally avoided `stdlib/convert/convert.cleave`'s existing `.to()` sugar**, a real, separate inference gap found while writing it: `Convert<From,To>`'s own `To` is output-only, gated purely on `From` (`i32`, always concrete) being ready — so its dispatch used to run *immediately*, even while type-checking `Mean`'s own still-generic impl body, *before* any real instantiation ever pins the enclosing `T` down. With only `Convert<i32,f64>` declared at the time, this happened to "work" by having exactly one candidate to (over-eagerly) commit to — but adding the natural `Convert<i32,f32>` companion impl would have made `N.to()` inside a still-generic body a genuine `AmbiguousDispatch` (two real candidates, each resolving the shared `T` differently), even though the *real* call site's own `T` is never actually ambiguous once truly instantiated. Flagged at the time, `mean` sidestepped it via a direct `mlir::arith::sitofp` call instead — fixed for real in a same-session follow-up, see "The `Convert`/output-only-generic premature-commit bug," below; `mean` now uses `.to()` again.

Verified via: three real end-to-end JIT tests in `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs` (`relu_sigmoid_tanh_compute_correctly_on_scalars`, `relu_sigmoid_tanh_compute_correctly_on_vectors`, `sum_mean_max_of_a_vector_compute_correctly`) plus a new example, `examples/neuron_demo.cleave` — a real single-neuron forward pass (`sum(weights * inputs) + bias`, then `sigmoid`), numerically verified against a hand-computed expected value (`≈0.450166`). Full `cargo test --workspace` green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep including the new example.

**Deliberately out of scope**: `Matrix`/`Tensor` reductions (need an axis argument, genuinely more design work than a flat `Vector` reduction); auto-diff, the actual bigger half of "toward simple ML," a separate, unstarted item.

### Real transitive `use` resolution (topological load order + cycle detection)

`driver.rs`'s own `compile` doc comment already named the fix needed precisely: "Real transitive resolution needs cycle detection... not fine as a permanent limitation." Two independent stdlib crates (`nn`, `convert`) had already hit and separately documented the one-level-deep gap this closes.

**The fix**: `compile`'s former single pass over the entry files' own `use` statements is now a proper DFS (`driver.rs::visit_crate`) that, after `load_crate_dir` returns a newly-loaded crate's own merged `Program`, scans *that* `Program`'s own `items` for further `ItemKind::Use` entries — already present and unfiltered post-merge (confirmed directly: `merge_programs` never special-cases `Use` items) — and recurses, exactly the same scan `compile` already ran once, just applied transitively. Real cycle detection rides along for free: the DFS's own current path (`stack: Vec<String>`, distinct from `loaded`, which only ever dedups) catches a name re-encountered while still on that path, reporting a real, located `Diagnostic` naming the actual cycle (`"circular crate dependency: a -> b -> a"`) rather than looping forever or silently truncating. Pushing each crate's own `Program` in DFS *post-order* (after every one of its own dependencies has already been visited) gives a real topological order for free — `merge_programs` itself never required this (a flat, order-independent union), but it keeps `FileId` assignment deterministic, the same concern `load_crate_dir`'s own path-sorting already existed for. `PRELUDE_CRATES` joins the identical worklist (via a `required: bool` flag threaded through `visit_crate`, `false` only for a prelude crate's own top-level entry point — a missing prelude crate stays silently fine, exactly as before, but its own further dependencies, once it *is* found, are `required: true` like any other).

**The real end-to-end proof, not just new synthetic tests**: the three `stdlib/nn` JIT tests (`cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`) had their own workaround `use vector;` removed — they now write only `use nn;`, relying entirely on `nn.cleave`'s own internal `use vector;` being followed transitively, and still pass.

Verified via: four new tests in `cleave/tests/use_resolution.rs` (a real 3-crate transitive chain, a crate reached via two different paths loading exactly once, a genuine `a`-uses-`b`-uses-`a` cycle rejected with a located, clearly-worded diagnostic — plus the existing dedup/shadowing/ordering tests in that file, all unaffected) using the file's own pre-existing `TempProject` fixture, no new test infrastructure needed. Full `cargo test --workspace` green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected.

### The `Convert`/output-only-generic premature-commit bug

`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s own `mean` (see above) avoided `.to()` because of a real inference bug, found directly while writing it: `infer_algebra_call`'s readiness check only ever required an algebra's *gating* (input-appearing) generics to be concrete before committing a dispatch immediately, mid-body. For `Convert<From,To>`, `To` is output-only — never gated — so a call like `N.to()` (`Convert<i32,T>`) inside a still-*generic* impl's own body-check dispatched **immediately** the moment `From` (`i32`) looked concrete, permanently binding `self.subst` — during mere *declaration*-checking of the generic impl, before any real instantiation ever ran. With one candidate this silently over-committed the enclosing `T` to whichever impl happened to match; with two (`Convert<i32,f64>` and the natural `Convert<i32,f32>` companion), it became an outright `AmbiguousDispatch` even though no real, eventual call site is ever actually ambiguous.

**Two independent bugs, not one — the first fix attempt only caught the narrower of the two**: keying the fix purely off `self.active_generics` (deferring whenever a still-open generic fn/impl's own declaration was being checked) fixed the *declaration-time* over-commit, but a second, more general problem surfaced empirically once a real two-candidate `Convert` case was tested end to end (`an_algebra_call_inside_a_generic_impl_body_resolves_independently_per_instantiation`, `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`): the very same premature-commit shape also hits **ordinary, non-generic top-level code** — `let f: f64 = n.to();` inside `main`, with two `Convert<i32,_>` candidates declared, hit the identical false `AmbiguousDispatch`, proving this was never really about "inside a generic body" at all. The real cause is more general: `To`'s own concrete value is typically decided not by anything in the call's own arguments, but by whatever the call *expression* is used for next (an enclosing `let`'s own annotation, a sibling operand in a binary op, ...) — and that unification always runs *strictly after* the call itself is type-checked, never before. Dispatching before it has run only "works" by accident, when exactly one candidate happens to exist.

**The fix, in `infer_algebra_call`, layers two checks together**:
- **Still-declaring a generic fn/impl** (`self.active_generics` non-empty) **and some position isn't concrete yet** → defer, unconditionally, even with only one matching candidate — this is what stops the *declaration-time* over-commit (`quantify_impl_generics`, called right after the body finishes, marks the impl's own generics quantified; `check_pending_constraints`'s existing leading guard then silently skips any constraint mentioning one of them, exactly like any other still-open generic bound, resolved later per real instantiation by `monomorphize.rs` instead).
- **Dispatch attempted (gating satisfied) but `dispatch_algebra_call` reports `AmbiguousDispatch`** → caught and deferred instead of propagated, rather than failing immediately. Sound by construction: two *concrete* target patterns can never both unify against an already-concrete query (`check_no_overlapping_impls` already rules out two impls agreeing on one shared shape), so any live disagreement necessarily means the disagreeing position was still a bare `Ty::Var` going in — "ambiguous right now" therefore never means "ambiguous forever." The identical guard was also needed in `check_pending_constraints`'s own committing branch (this session's own earlier fix, `self.active_generics` staying set through `finish_fn`) — without it the declaration-time half of the bug just moved one phase later instead of disappearing.

`check_pending_constraints` gets the **final** say, once the whole body (hence every local unification that could possibly disambiguate an open position) has already been walked — a still-genuinely-ambiguous constraint reaching it there fails for real, exactly as it always did; nothing here makes a truly ambiguous program silently succeed.

**Found and fixed empirically, not from theory alone**: an earlier version of this fix tried detecting "does this call's output-only position already share a `TyVar` with one of `self.active_generics`'s own values" directly — plausible-looking, but unsound, confirmed by direct testing: for `let w: T = n_i.to();`, the call's own readiness decision is made *before* the enclosing `let`'s own annotation ever unifies `To` with `T`, so at decision time nothing yet aliases anything, and the check let it through anyway, reproducing the exact bug it was meant to prevent. A second, broader attempt — defer whenever *any* position isn't concrete, unconditionally, dropping the gating-based immediate-dispatch path entirely — over-corrected the other way, breaking `MatMul<A,B,C>`'s own legitimate immediate-resolution case (`let c = matmul(a,b); c.values[0,0]`, an *unannotated* binding whose own `C` has no later annotation to ever pin it down, needing `C` resolved *inside* the same dispatch since nothing else ever will) — caught directly by the full `cargo test --workspace` sweep (`heterogeneous_algebra_matrix_multiplication`), not predicted in advance.

Verified via: `an_algebra_call_inside_a_generic_impl_body_resolves_independently_per_instantiation` and `turbofish_on_convert_disambiguates_between_two_real_competing_impls` (`cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`), plus a second, real `Convert<i32,f32>` impl added to `stdlib/convert/convert.cleave` specifically to prove the false-ambiguity half is really gone, not just the wrong-permanent-commit half. `stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s `mean` reworked back to `sum(v) / n_i.to()`, dropping the `sitofp` workaround. Full `cargo test --workspace` green (`--test-threads=1`), including every pre-existing algebra-dispatch-inside-a-generic-impl test (`Ring<Complex<T>>` and friends) and the `MatMul` regression above, confirming the final fix is truly additive; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep, including `examples/neuron_demo.cleave`.

### `CVal::Float` representable in the e-graph

Toward auto-diff (see the plan this was built against — `derivative(x,x) → 1.0` for an `f32`/`f64` function needs a real float leaf to produce) — `doc/backlog.md`'s own former "`CVal::Float` in the e-graph" item. Bare `f64` has neither `Ord` nor `Hash`, both required by `egg::define_language!`'s own derives — `Forward::cval_to_id` used to return `None` for `CVal::Float`, so a segment touching one simply stopped translating there, silently, rather than misbehaving (this module's own former doc comment claimed a "later translator stage should panic clearly" — not accurate: confirmed directly, the real prior behavior was a silent, ungraceful stop, not a panic).

**The fix**: `ordered-float` added as a dependency (small, well-known, exactly what the module's own doc comment already named as needed). `CleaveLang` gains `Float(ordered_float::OrderedFloat<f64>)`. `Forward::cval_to_id` and `rebuild`'s own leaf match both gain a `Float` arm, mirroring the existing `Int`/`Bool` arms exactly — a real float value now survives translation, extraction, and reconstruction back into CPS.

**Deliberately narrow**: `ConstantFold::Data` stays `Option<u64>` (Int-only) — a `Float` leaf is representable and passes through a segment untouched, but arithmetic over one (`2.0 * 3.0`) doesn't itself fold to `6.0` yet. Real, separate axiom-level gap found *while verifying this*, not fixed here: `axiom_to_rewrite`/`build_pattern` parse every axiom-body numeric literal as `u64` unconditionally (`ExprKind::NumberLit`'s own text parsed via `.parse::<u64>()`), so an identity axiom like `Ring<T>`'s own `add_zero(a): add(a, 0) == a;`, instantiated for `T = f32`, builds a rule whose own "zero" position is `CleaveLang::Int(0)` — this can never match a real `f32` zero flowing through as `CleaveLang::Float(0.0)` (a different `enode` variant entirely), so `add_zero`-shaped axioms are silently inert for any float-typed instantiation today. Flagged, not fixed — the fix (making `build_pattern` respect the reached type when parsing a literal) is real but separate scope from just making `Float` representable at all.

Verified via: two new unit tests directly in `egraph.rs` — `a_letprim_with_a_float_literal_argument_translates_instead_of_stopping` (proves `Forward::walk` no longer stops dead on a `CVal::Float` argument) and `a_float_leaf_round_trips_through_extraction_and_rebuild_unchanged` (proves `rebuild` reconstructs the exact original float value, no rewrite needing to actually fire — the same code path `optimize_program` uses whenever one does). Plus a real end-to-end JIT test in `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs` (`a_float_literal_inside_a_straight_line_segment_still_computes_correctly_through_the_full_pipeline`), run through the complete `--run` pipeline, confirming the whole thing still computes the right numeric answer, not just that construction alone doesn't panic. Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected.

### Auto-diff v1 — `fprime = derive(f);`

The bigger half of "toward simple ML" (`stdlib/nn`'s activation functions/reductions were the smaller half, done earlier this session) — a first, deliberately scoped increment: `fprime = derive(f);`, a new top-level item mechanically synthesizing `fprime`'s own body from an existing, non-generic, scalar-valued function `f`. Read as an assignment with `derive` applied to `f` ("`fprime` equals the derivative of `f`") — an earlier `derive fprime = f;` phrasing was considered and rejected in discussion: it reads as if `fprime` itself were being derived, backwards. `f` must have every parameter (and its own return type) the same concrete numeric type `T` — `N == 1` synthesizes an ordinary scalar derivative (`fn fprime(x: T) -> T`); `N > 1` synthesizes the gradient/Jacobian row as `[T; N]` (the array type that already exists — deliberately sidesteps any dependency on tuples, not implemented). A name-a-variable-by-name spelling (`derivative(f, x)`) was considered and rejected too, in favor of always differentiating with respect to *every* parameter — curry via an ordinary helper (`fn g(x) { f(x, 2.0) }`) for a single partial, no new mechanism needed.

**The actual mechanism, established via discussion before any code was written**: not a bespoke recursive differentiation engine — `derivative(expr, wrt)` is added as one more node (`Op("derivative", [expr_id, wrt_id])`) to the *same* e-graph a function's own body already translates into (`egraph.rs::Forward`), and a new rewrite-rule set (`derivative_rewrites`) progressively eliminates it during the *same* `egg::Runner::run`/saturation pass that already runs axioms and constant-folding — reusing nearly everything `egraph.rs` already had, rather than a second, parallel system.

**Prerequisite, done first in the same session**: `CVal::Float` representable in the e-graph (see above) — a real float leaf is needed to produce `derivative(x,x) → 1.0`.

**The rewrite rules (`derivative_rewrites`)**: `derivative(?x,?x) → 1` (an ordinary declarative pattern — egg's own semantics already require two occurrences of the same pattern var to match the same e-class). `derivative(?a,?x) → 0` for a *leaf* (`Free`/`Int`/`Float`/`Bool`) whose own e-class genuinely differs from `?x`'s — deliberately leaf-restricted (an eclass-inequality check alone, applied to an arbitrary compound subexpression, would be unsound: `derivative(x*y, x)`'s own `x*y` subexpression has a different eclass than `x` too, but obviously still depends on it) — needed a hand-built `ConditionalApplier`, the one genuinely new use of `egg`'s API in this codebase (a bare "not equal" wildcard has no declarative pattern form). Sum/sub/product rule, built only for whichever `Ring<ty>` op is actually present; a small, explicit elementary-function table (`tanh`, `d/du tanh(u) = 1 - tanh(u)²`), grown one entry at a time, not attempted generically.

**A dedicated cost function, `DerivativeFreeCost`, found necessary directly (not anticipated)**: plain `AstSize` is the *wrong* extraction metric here — a still-unreduced `derivative(mul(x,y), x)` (3 nodes) is structurally *smaller* than its own fully-reduced expansion (7+ nodes), so `AstSize` alone happily kept the tiny, unhelpful original, the exact opposite of the feature's own point. Any surviving `derivative` node is penalized enormously during extraction instead.

**Three real, load-bearing bugs found by direct testing while building this, not anticipated in the plan**:
- **`Forward::cval_to_id` never cached an *external* variable's own e-class** (only `env`, for `LetPrim`-bound vars) — the *same* external `CVar` (a function's own parameter) referenced more than once within a segment minted a *different*, non-congruent `Free` node each time. `x * x` used to become `mul(Free(fv0), Free(fv1))`, two unrelated values — `derivative(?x,?x) → 1` could then only ever match *one* of the two occurrences a real rewrite (the product rule's own RHS) introduces, silently computing the *wrong* derivative (`x` instead of `2x`) rather than erroring. Fixed with a new `Forward::external_vars: HashMap<CVar, egg::Id>` field, checked (and populated) by `cval_to_id` right alongside `env` — a real, pre-existing gap in this module's own design, not specific to auto-diff (nothing in the axiom/constant-fold rule set that existed before this needed "the same external var, referenced twice, really is one value" to hold).
- **A derivative rule's own RHS can reference a unit `f`'s own body never itself called** (the product rule always needs `Ring::add<ty>`, even differentiating a body that only ever multiplies) — `Forward::reached` alone (exactly what `f`'s own body happened to call) isn't enough to know such a reference is valid, or to let `rebuild` recognize it afterward. Fixed by checking the *whole-program* unit map (`collect_units` already builds a `ConcreteUnit` for every non-generic algebra-impl method unconditionally, regardless of whether anything calls it) rather than just the one segment, and merging the confirmed-existing units into both `axiom_rewrites`'/`derivative_rewrites`' own `reached` map and `rebuild`'s own `call_units` table.
- **`rebuild`'s own `memo` must be *fresh per parameter*, not shared across all of them** — each parameter's own derivative is extracted as an *independent* `RecExpr` (a separate `Extractor::find_best` call per e-class), and different `RecExpr`s' own internal ids are small integers starting fresh from 0 each time, unrelated to one another. A memo shared across more than one silently reused the *first* parameter's own cached reconstructions while rebuilding the *second* — the Jacobian test's own `[6.0, 3.0]` collapsed to `[6.0, 6.0]` (both slots holding `df/dx`) before this was caught.

**Never-reused parameters**: `fprime`'s own `CFunDef.params` are brand-new `CVar`s (`FreshVars`), never `f`'s own reused — two different top-level functions each declaring a formal parameter under the same numeric identity, while `f` itself may still be live elsewhere in the program, would violate `max_cvar_in_program`'s own documented uniqueness requirement. `OpTables` gained a `param_substitution: HashMap<CVar, CVar>` table (empty for every pre-existing caller), consulted by `rebuild`'s own `Free`-node handling before falling back to the original `CVal`.

**Deliberately out of scope for this first iteration**: heterogeneous parameter types on `f` (needs tuples); deriving a generic or already-derived function; second derivatives (plausible, not attempted — `fprime`'s own body, once real, is an ordinary unit like any other, so likely to "just work" once tried); float constant folding through arithmetic (`ConstantFold` stays `Int`-only, so `2.0 * 3.0` doesn't itself fold to `6.0` — a real, separate follow-up); quotient rule, and any elementary function beyond `tanh`, added on demand; `Ring<T>`'s own `add_zero` axiom silently never firing for a float-typed instantiation (a pre-existing, separate gap — its own literal `0` is parsed `Int`-shaped regardless of `T`, confirmed directly while testing this feature, not fixed here).

Verified via: `derivative_rewrites`'s own unit tests directly in `egraph.rs` (`derivative_of_a_variable_with_respect_to_itself_is_one`, `derivative_of_a_different_free_variable_is_zero`, `derivative_of_a_float_literal_is_zero`, the leaf-vs-compound distinction specifically, and the product rule eliminating every `derivative` marker) plus two real end-to-end JIT tests in `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs` (`derive_of_a_single_parameter_function_computes_the_scalar_derivative` — `d(x²)/dx` at `x=3` — and `derive_of_a_two_parameter_function_computes_the_jacobian_as_an_array` — `f(x,y)=xy+x`'s own Jacobian at `(3,5)`, `[6.0, 3.0]`, chosen so neither component is right by coincidence). Error paths (`derive`-ing a nonexistent, generic, or heterogeneously-typed function) covered in `cleave/tests/driver.rs`, each a real, located `Diagnostic`. `examples/derivative_demo.cleave` added (`grad = derive(loss);` for a toy squared-error loss, `[2x, 2y]`, numerically verified). Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep, including the new example.

### Auto-diff v1 → algebra-declared `derivative` rules

Follow-up to "Auto-diff v1" above, done in the same session, prompted directly: hard-coding the sum/product/chain rule in Rust (`egraph.rs::binary_linear_rule`/`product_rule`/`tanh_chain_rule`) meant `div`, `neg`, `sigmoid`, `exp`, or any user-declared algebra had *no* derivative rule at all, and a `derivative` node saturation couldn't eliminate used to *panic* inside `rebuild` ("extracted `Op` node `derivative` is in none of this module's own lookup tables") rather than fail cleanly. Two changes: a derivative rule becomes a real, declared cleave construct — mirroring `axiom` exactly — and an undifferentiable `derivative` node becomes a real, reported error instead of a panic.

**The declaration**, a new `algebra_item` alongside `axiom`:

```
algebra Ring<T> {
    fn add(a: T, b: T) -> T;
    fn mul(a: T, b: T) -> T;
    derivative add(a, b): add(d(a), d(b));
    derivative mul(a, b): add(mul(a, d(b)), mul(d(a), b));
}
```

`method` (`"mul"`) is the identity — no separate rule name, unlike `AxiomDecl` (`ast.rs::DerivativeRuleDecl`). `d(x)` is sugar meaningful only inside a `derivative` rule's own body — `egraph.rs::build_pattern` (shared with `axiom_to_rewrite`, which passes `d_var: None` and never takes the new branch) recognizes a `Call` whose path is literally `"d"` with one argument and compiles it to `Op("derivative", [inner, x])`, where `x` is one reserved pattern var (`?x`) shared by every `d(...)` in the same rule. The two base cases (`derivative(?x,?x) → 1`, the leaf-zero rule) stay hard-coded on purpose — they're facts about what a variable/a constant *is*, not an algebraic law belonging to any one algebra, so nothing about them should be declarable.

**Registry** (`registry.rs`): `AlgebraEntry` gains `derivative_rules: Vec<DerivativeRuleDecl>`, populated in `Registry::build`'s existing algebra loop exactly the way `axioms` already is; a new `Registry::derivative_rules(algebra)` accessor mirrors `axioms()`.

**`egraph.rs`**: `derivative_rule_to_rewrite`/`derivative_rule_rewrites` mirror `axiom_to_rewrite`/`axiom_rewrites` exactly (same `reached_types` grouping, same "only build what's reached" discipline) — `derivative_rewrites` (the public entry point) now returns `(Vec<Rewrite<...>>, HashSet<String>)`, the second element the union of every unit name any built rule's own RHS references, needed because a rule's RHS can reference a unit `f`'s own body never itself called (the product rule always needs `add`) — `synthesize_derivatives` merges these, filtered to whatever actually exists in the whole program (`collect_units` already builds a `ConcreteUnit` for every non-generic algebra-impl method unconditionally), into `call_units` so `rebuild` recognizes them.

**A real complication, found while migrating `stdlib/nn`'s own `tanh` (not anticipated in the plan)**: `1 - tanh(x)²` needs `Ring`'s own `sub`/`mul`, a *different* algebra than the one the rule is declared on (`Activation`) — `axiom_to_rewrite`'s own pre-existing, documented limitation ("assumes an axiom's own body only ever calls methods of the *same* algebra it's declared on") would have made this silently resolve to nonexistent `Activation::sub<f32>`/`Activation::mul<f32>` units. Fixed by giving `build_pattern` (shared by both `axiom_to_rewrite` and the new derivative-rule path) real cross-algebra resolution: check whether the *enclosing* algebra declares the called method first (preserves every existing single-algebra rule's own behavior unchanged), and only if it doesn't, fall back to a real `Registry::algebras_with_fn` search — ambiguous (more than one candidate) or simply unknown is a hard rejection, never a guess.

**The "cannot derive" error**: `synthesize_derivatives` now returns `Result<CpsProgram, Vec<String>>` — after extracting each parameter's own best derivative, a new check (`undifferentiable_unit`) scans for a surviving `Op("derivative", ...)` node and, if found, names the specific inner unit it's still wrapped around (`"cannot derive `fprime`: no derivative rule for `Foo::bar<f32>`"`), collecting every such failure rather than stopping at the first (this project's own "see every conflict" posture). `main.rs`'s six `build_cps_program` call sites (one per `--dump-*`/`--run` flag) each now `match` on the result, printing every message and setting `ExitCode::FAILURE` instead of the pipeline panicking.

**Two smaller, real fixes surfaced by testing this specific migration, not the redesign's own main point**: `build_pattern`'s `NumberLit` handling used to parse every literal as `u64` unconditionally — `Activation::tanh`'s own literal `1` would have built `CleaveLang::Int(1)`, never matching a real runtime `CleaveLang::Float(1.0)` (the *same* gap `Ring::add_zero` already has, `doc/backlog-done.md`'s own `CVal::Float` entry) — fixed by making it `Float`-typed whenever `ty` is `f32`/`f64`, same convention `derivative_rewrites`'s own base-case `(one, zero)` already used.

Verified via: `cleave/tests/parser.rs` (`derivative_rule_decl_parses`, and that it's a valid `algebra_item`); `cleave/tests/registry.rs` (retained correctly, empty-not-missing when absent, coexists with `axiom` on the same algebra); `egraph.rs`'s own product-rule tests reworked to go through the real declared path (`TestRing`, not `Ring` — the real prelude algebra, same precedent `a_real_axiom_builds_a_rewrite_that_actually_fires` already established) instead of a hand-built `reached` map with no registry behind it, plus a new test confirming the referenced-unit set names `add` even when only `mul` was reached; a new, real "cannot derive" test (a method with no rule at all, through the full `compile` → `collect_units` → `convert_program` pipeline, confirming a real `Err` naming the exact unit, not a panic); a new end-to-end JIT test (`derive_of_tanh_uses_stdlib_nns_own_declared_derivative_rule`) checking `stdlib/nn`'s own migrated `tanh` rule against an independently-computed `expected` value using the identical underlying units (bit-identical IEEE754 results, a plain `==`, no hand-computed literal to get subtly wrong) — the real proof both the cross-algebra fix and the `NumberLit`-type fix are load-bearing. `stdlib/num`'s own `Ring<T>` and `stdlib/nn`'s own `Activation<T>` migrated to declare their rules in cleave source; the three pre-existing scalar/Jacobian JIT tests re-verified numerically identical, now sourced from the declared path. Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep, including `examples/derivative_demo.cleave`, numerically unchanged.

### Unroll statically-bounded `for` loops in the e-graph forward translator

Motivated directly by wanting a first real ML example (linear regression via gradient descent, using `derive()`): a loss function summing squared error over even a handful of data points needs a `for` loop, and `Forward::walk` (`egraph.rs`) stopped dead at *any* `for`/`while`, unconditionally — `is_straight_line` hard-codes `CExpr::Fix { .. } => false`, and the `Fix` arm only ever folds a `Fix` into an opaque node when `recognize_real_call` matches it as an ordinary call (`carried_types: None`, single-param continuation); a `for` loop's own generated `Fix` (`cps.rs`'s own lowering: a self-recursive `CFunDef` carrying `[i_var, ...carried_params]`, `carried_types: Some(_)`) never matches that shape.

In this language, though, a loop bound is *always* concrete by the time CPS conversion runs on a given monomorphized instantiation — a const generic (`stdlib/nn`'s own `sum`/`mean`/`max_of`, `for i in 1..N`) resolves to a literal during monomorphization, before CPS conversion ever sees it. So unrolling a `for` loop with literal integer bounds is a real, well-scoped, purely-additive capability.

**`Forward::try_unroll_for_loop`** (`egraph.rs`), wired into `Forward::walk`'s `Fix` arm as the first attempt (falls back to today's `recognize_real_call` path unchanged when it returns `None`): recognizes the exact CPS shape (ground-truthed via `--dump-cps`, not guessed) — the outer `Fix`'s `CFunDef` (`carried_types: Some(_)`), its own body a nested `Fix` matching `recognize_real_call` for the `<`-comparison, whose `If`'s `then_branch` is an ordinary straight-line/real-call chain ending in a recursive tail `App`, `else_branch` the loop's own exit continuation. Only when both bounds are literal `CVal::Int`s within `MAX_UNROLL_ITERATIONS` (1024, a deliberately conservative, easy-to-raise cap — past it, unrolling would trade a clean "stop, unchanged" bail for an e-graph blow-up) does it mechanically unroll: each iteration is walked with `i_var` bound to that iteration's own literal e-class and the carried vars bound to whatever e-classes the *previous* iteration actually produced, splicing straight into the next iteration's own copy (or the exit continuation, on the last one) instead of stopping at the recursive call. Bails — falls through to today's "stop, unchanged" — the moment any expected piece doesn't hold; never partially unrolls.

Each iteration's own copy of `then_branch` is alpha-renamed first (`Forward::alpha_rename`, a new recursive pass giving every internally-bound `CVar` — a `LetPrim`'s own `var`, a nested `Fix`'s own `CFunDef::params` — a fresh id via a local `FreshVars` seeded above every `CVar` the loop's own body uses) rather than reusing the same ids across copies — the first version of this reused ids on the theory that `Forward::walk`'s own sequential, single-pass nature made it safe; empirically false (see the next section) once the derivative machinery started creating cross-iteration e-class references, so alpha-renaming replaced it outright, no longer optional.

**A real, empirically-found bug, unrelated to the feature itself, found and fixed alongside it**: `synthesize_derivatives`'s own `segment_root_var`/`env.get` lookups used to `continue` *silently* — no error — whenever a request's body didn't translate as one clean segment reaching its own return (any `for` loop, before this item; still true today for a too-large or branching loop). The requested function (`grad`, say) was simply never added to `new_funcs`, and the real crash surfaced three stages downstream, confusingly, in `mlir_lower.rs`: `panicked ... call to unknown top-level fn \`grad\``. Reproduced directly via a real `--run` *before* touching any code. Both sites now push a real, collected error (`"cannot derive \`{name}\`: function body is not fully representable ..."`) into the same `errors`/`Result` mechanism the "cannot derive" feature already established, instead of a silent, confusing crash.

**Deliberately out of scope, confirmed not assumed**: calling `sum`/`mean`/`max_of` (or any unit whose own body loops) from within a derived function — `Forward::walk`'s `Fix` arm only descends into a *callee's* body when `is_straight_line(&callee.def.body)`, which hard-codes `Fix => false` regardless of what unrolling can now do once it's *already* looking at the loop directly; needs the separate, already-tracked "Multi-level call transparency" item first (`doc/backlog.md`, cross-referenced there now, alongside a note that combining the two would unblock exactly this). A `for` loop containing an `if` — blocked by the separate "Representing an `If`/join point" item, `Forward::walk` never crosses *any* `If`. Non-literal bounds and `while` loops (never wired into CPS conversion at all) — bail, unchanged, not needed for any current use.

Verified via: `egraph.rs` unit tests mirroring the real dump shape by hand (`a_literal_bounded_for_loop_unrolls_and_carries_state_correctly` — confirms carried state actually threads iteration-to-iteration, not just restarts; `a_for_loop_with_a_non_literal_bound_is_not_unrolled`; `a_for_loop_exceeding_the_unroll_cap_is_not_unrolled` — both regression guards that today's conservative behavior is preserved exactly where unrolling doesn't apply); a real JIT test (`derive_of_a_function_containing_a_statically_bounded_for_loop_computes_the_correct_derivative`) differentiating a hand-written accumulation loop over a local array; a regression test for the silent-drop fix (`synthesize_derivatives_reports_a_clean_error_instead_of_panicking_when_no_rule_reaches_a_node` and its sibling for the oversized-loop case), asserting a real, located error instead of a panic.

### A sound, `Analysis`-based occurs-check for auto-diff

Found *while building the linear-regression example above* — the very first real, "deep enough" use of `derivative`'s own `?a`-doesn't-depend-on-`?x` base rule (`derivative-independent-zero`, added earlier this session to generalize the original leaf-only zero rule so it'd also cover a compound, `?x`-independent subexpression like an array `Load`). Two distinct, real bugs, found by direct empirical narrowing (`egg::Runner::explain_id_equivalence`/`get_flat_string` — egg's own explanation machinery — pointed at the *exact* rewrite chain each time, not guessed):

**Bug 1 — a live occurs-check treated a derivative marker's own syntax as a real dependency.** `derivative-independent-zero`'s first version was a real, recursive graph traversal (`depends_on_eclass`, since removed): does `target`'s e-class appear, transitively, among `from`'s own children. A *still-unreduced* `derivative(inner, x)` marker sitting in `from`'s own e-class (ordinary, expected mid-saturation state — the rule just hasn't fired on that occurrence *yet*) got walked exactly like any other node, and its own *second* child is, by construction, always `x` itself — so *any* e-class transitively containing *any* pending derivative marker anywhere looked like it "depends on" `x`, regardless of whether it actually did. Reproduced directly, minimally: `derivative(0.0, w)` unioned into `0.0`'s own e-class (exactly what mid-saturation looks like) made `depends_on_eclass` report `0.0` as `w`-dependent.

**Bug 2 — deeper, and the real reason the whole mechanism moved to a proper `Analysis`.** Even fixed for derivative markers specifically, a *live* traversal over an already-saturating e-graph can never be sound in general: equality saturation routinely unions a genuinely `w`-*independent* value into the same e-class as an expression that merely *mentions* `w` syntactically without depending on it (`w * 0.0`, still value-equal to plain `0.0` regardless of `w`) — and the *more* saturation progresses, the *more* such alternatives accumulate in *every* e-class, since each is a byproduct of the very rules (commutativity, the declared product rule) already firing for ordinary reasons. A live "does *any* representation mention `w`" search becomes wrong more and more often the longer saturation runs — reproduced directly with a two-parameter loss (`w * 1.0 + b`, no loop at all, 107 nodes) failing outright, and a two-parameter *looped* case blowing up to 343 saturation iterations / 5349 nodes / a 30-second timeout without ever converging (the false "depends" verdicts kept triggering more, unnecessary rule work in a feedback loop).

The fix: `ConstantFold`'s own `Analysis::Data` (previously just `Option<u64>`, the existing int constant-fold value) becomes `FoldData { const_int, free_deps: HashSet<Symbol> }` — `free_deps` is an *upper bound* on which `Free`-variable symbols an e-class's value can be shown to depend on, computed compositionally in `make` (a `Free` node contributes itself; an ordinary `Op` node's own bound is the union of its children's — including a `derivative(...)` node, no special-casing needed at this layer at all) and, critically, *narrowed by intersection* in `merge` whenever two representations of the same e-class combine: if even one representation proves independence from `w` (an empty bound), the whole e-class — being value-equal to *every* one of its own representations — provably is too, regardless of how many other, less-reduced representations still mention `w`. `derivative-independent-zero`'s own condition becomes a one-line lookup, `egraph[x_class].data.free_deps.is_disjoint(&egraph[a_class].data.free_deps)`, no live traversal at all — the same reason `const_int` itself never needed one.

Also raised `synthesize_derivatives`'s own `Runner`'s limits (`iter_limit`/`node_limit`/`time_limit`, all well past `Runner::default()`'s own tuning for `optimize_program`'s ordinary segments) — a declared rule only peels one level of nesting per saturation iteration, so a chain deep enough to need unrolling can legitimately need more than the default 30 rounds even once Bug 2 no longer causes runaway, unnecessary iteration.

Verified via: two new, minimal `egraph.rs` unit tests exercising the `Analysis` directly, no saturation/rules involved (`an_unreduced_derivative_markers_own_children_do_not_pollute_free_deps`, `a_value_equal_but_w_mentioning_representation_does_not_pollute_free_deps` — the second is the real proof Bug 2's own fix holds, hand-building exactly the `w * 0.0` pollution shape and asserting the merged e-class's own `free_deps` stays empty); every previously-failing repro (`w+data[i]` mixed add/load chains at various depths, two-parameter `w*1.0+b`, a two-parameter *looped* loss) now produces the numerically correct gradient; `examples/linear_regression.cleave` (new) — real gradient descent, `y = w*x + b` fit to four noise-free points via `derive()`, no hand-derived math, converging to `w ≈ 2.0`, `b ≈ 1.0` against the true `y = 2x + 1`. Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep including the new example.

### `div`/`neg` constant-folding, and a real division-by-zero diagnostic

`const_eval::eval_binop` gains `div` (`u64` truncating division, `None` on a zero divisor — its own existing "can't evaluate this" signal, same as an unrecognized operator, not a panic) and `neg` support, closing `doc/backlog.md`'s own former "More constant-folding operators" item.

**`neg` doesn't need `eval_binop`'s own signature touched at all.** It's inherently unary, and `eval_binop` stays strictly binary — instead, `ConstantFold::make`'s own `Op` arm (`egraph.rs`) recognizes a 1-child node whose `abstract_op_name` is `"neg"` and folds it via `eval_binop("sub", Int(0), a)` directly — exactly what `Ring<T>::neg`'s own *real* body already computes (`mlir::arith::subi(0, a)` for every integer width), so this is reusing an existing identity, not inventing a new one. Sound for the same reason `add`/`mul`/`sub` already are: two's-complement wrapping arithmetic gives the identical `u64` bit pattern regardless of whether the operand is "meant" to be signed or unsigned.

**`div` is real but deliberately *not* reused by `ConstantFold` for opportunistic runtime folding — a genuine correctness risk found directly, not a style choice.** `add`/`mul`/`sub`/`neg` are all representation-agnostic (wrapping two's-complement arithmetic is identical whether the `u64` bit pattern is "meant" as signed or unsigned) — division is not: a negative signed operand divides differently than the same bit pattern read as unsigned, and neither `ConstValue::Int` nor `CleaveLang::Int`/this analysis carries a width/signedness tag to tell which is meant. Folding a real, runtime `Ring::div<i32>` node here regardless would risk silently computing the *wrong* answer, not just missing an optimization — so `ConstantFold::make` explicitly excludes `"div"` from the operators it forwards to `eval_binop`, leaving it as an ordinary, unfolded `Op` node for MLIR to compute correctly at actual runtime (`arith.divsi`/`arith.divf`, already real and tested, independent of this item). `eval_binop`'s own `"div"` support stays real and used — just only by `infer.rs`'s const-generic evaluator, where an array-size operand is always non-negative, so the signed/unsigned ambiguity can't arise in the first place.

**A real division-by-zero diagnostic, not a confusing downstream panic — found directly while designing this, exactly the class of bug this whole session kept surfacing.** Before this, a const-generic `[T; N/0]` with both operands already concrete would make `infer.rs::const_value_from_expr`/`fold_const_expr` build a `Ty::ConstExpr("div", N, 0)` that can never resolve further (both operands are already concrete — nothing left to substitute), silently surviving all the way to `mlir_lower.rs`'s own codegen-time panic for an unresolved deferred const expression, a message that never mentions division or zero at all. Two real fixes, matched to the two genuinely different moments a divisor can be known concrete:

- **The immediate/literal case** (`[i32; 4/0]`, or an explicit turbofish-pinned divisor) — both operands are concrete right at `const_value_from_expr`'s own construction site, with a real source `Expr`/span in hand. A new `Infer::pending_div_by_zero_checks: Vec<(u64, Span)>` field, pushed to right there, mirrors `pending_type_name_checks`'s own already-established deferred-check shape exactly (same reasoning: dozens of infallible call sites, no natural `?`-propagation path) — drained by a new `check_pending_div_by_zero`, wired into the identical four call sites `check_pending_type_names` already uses (`finish_fn`, and `callgraph.rs`'s own two per-member checks), producing a real, located `TypeErrorKind::ConstDivByZero { dividend }`.
- **The deferred-generic case** (`fn f<const N, const M>(x: [T; N/M])`, only invalid at one specific, later instantiation) — genuinely harder: `Ty` itself carries no span at all (confirmed directly, not assumed), and threading one through `Subst::apply`/`substitute`/`fold_const_expr` and into `monomorphize.rs`'s own specialization would be real, invasive plumbing, not a small addition — deliberately not attempted here, flagged instead. `mlir_lower.rs`'s own pre-existing "unresolved ConstExpr reached codegen" panic gets a specific, recognized-shape variant (`op == "div"` with a resolved-zero second operand) with a message that actually says "division by zero" instead of pointing at an "invariant violation" that isn't really one — better than the generic message, still not a located compile error, an explicit, acknowledged scope boundary.

Verified via: `const_eval.rs`'s own `div`/`neg` unit tests (truncating division, zero-divisor non-panic); two new `ConstantFold`-level tests in `egraph.rs` (`neg_folds_a_single_int_literal`, `div_is_never_folded_by_constant_fold` — the second is the real proof the signedness-safety exclusion holds); two new `infer.rs` tests (`a_literal_division_by_zero_in_an_array_size_is_rejected` — a real, located `ConstDivByZero`, not a downstream panic; `a_literal_division_in_an_array_size_folds_correctly` — the non-zero counterpart, confirming `div` support itself works, `8/2` resolving to a real `[i32; 4]`); one pre-existing test (`an_unsupported_operator_in_shape_position_stays_permissively_unconstrained`) updated from `div` (now supported) to `mod` (still isn't — a deliberately named function, not an operator) to keep testing what it always meant to. Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected.

### `for x in array` — element-based array iteration

Only index-based iteration (`for i in start..end { ... }`, reading `arr[i]` manually) existed before this. An array is homogeneous — every element shares one type — so unlike tuple iteration (a genuinely harder, deliberately separate item, still not started: MLIR/LLVM needs one fixed type per compiled loop body, so a heterogeneous tuple can only ever be consumed via compile-time unrolling, never a real loop), this needed no new compilation strategy at all — a thin desugar reusing the *existing* `for`/indexing machinery end to end.

**Grammar/AST/lowering**: `for_expr`'s own range clause becomes optional (`(".." ~ additive)?`), and a new `ExprKind::ForIn { var, iter, body }` sits alongside the existing `ExprKind::For` — `lower_for_expr` disambiguates by checking whether the second child is another `additive` (range form) or the `block` directly (new form), rather than pushing the distinction into the grammar itself.

**Type-checking (`infer.rs`)**: `iter` unifies directly against a fresh `Ty::Array(elem, size)` — deliberately *not* routed through `resolve_index`'s own dual-path array/algebra-dispatch machinery (that exists for `arr[i]`'s own `Index<...>`-trait fallback on a non-array container, irrelevant here — this item is specifically about a real, homogeneous array). A non-array `iter` gets the ordinary `Mismatch` diagnostic for free, no new `TypeErrorKind` needed. `var` binds to the array's own element type in the inner scope, exactly like the range form's own index variable binds to the bound's type.

**CPS conversion (`cps.rs`) — the one real design decision**: the existing `ExprKind::For` arm already binds its own loop variable by inserting `body_env.insert(var, CVal::Var(i_var))` right before converting `body` — `body`'s own AST never needs to *see* the index directly, it just resolves `var` through the environment like any other local. The same trick applies here: bind `var` to a *loaded element* CVar instead of an index CVar, and `body` (the user's own AST, referencing `x`) needs zero rewriting — no synthetic `let x = arr[i];` AST node, no fabricated `NodeId`/`node_types` entry. The new `ExprKind::ForIn` arm reuses `ExprKind::For`'s own `Fix`-building shape almost verbatim (`mutated_free_vars`, the `lt`/`add` synthetic-binop resolution, the carried-vars machinery — copied, not reinvented), with `start`/`end` always `0`/the array's own size (read straight from `ctx.node_types[&iter.id]`'s resolved `Ty::Array(_, Ty::Const(_))` — guaranteed concrete here, monomorphization has already run) and `idx_ty` always plain `i32` (no user-written bound to recover a different width from), plus one extra `LetPrim { op: PrimOp::Load, .. }` right where the index gets bound, mirroring exactly how `ExprKind::Index`'s own existing conversion already builds a `Load`.

**A real, if narrow, gap found while implementing this**: `ExprKind` gained a new variant, and Rust's own exhaustiveness checking caught every structural-recursion site that needed a mirror `ForIn` arm (`callgraph.rs`, `monomorphize.rs` ×2, `print.rs`, `dump.rs`, plus `cps.rs`'s own `mutated_free_vars`/`lambda_free_vars` helpers) — *except one*: `cps.rs::convert_expr`'s own top-level match ends in a catch-all `other => panic!("CPS doesn't support {other:?} yet")`, which already silently absorbed `ExprKind::Lambda` (closure values are a separate, not-yet-built feature) and would have identically absorbed `ExprKind::ForIn` — compiling clean, then panicking at actual CPS-conversion time the first time anyone wrote `for x in arr`. Confirmed directly (not assumed) by checking `convert_expr`'s own true extent before assuming the compiler's silence meant full coverage — the real conversion arm above was added by hand, not found via a compile error.

Verified via: `for_in_array_parses` (`tests/parser.rs`); `for_range_still_lowers_to_for`/`for_in_array_lowers_to_for_in` (`tests/lower.rs` — the first a direct regression guard on the grammar's new optional group); three `infer.rs` tests (element-type binding, a real `Mismatch` for a non-array `iter`, no scope leakage); two real JIT tests in `tests/mlir_lower.rs` (`a_for_in_loop_over_an_array_sums_its_own_elements_not_its_indices` — `1+2+3+4=10`, not `10` via summing indices `0+1+2+3` either, the real discriminator between "iterating elements" and "iterating indices by coincidence"; `a_for_in_loop_carries_an_outer_mutated_variable_correctly` — a second, independently-typed carried variable alongside the loop's own accumulator, mirroring `a_loop_carrying_two_different_types_computes_the_right_value`'s own established precedent). Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected (a pure addition, no existing example needed rewriting).

### Qualified-call syntax (`Ring::mul(a, b)`)

Two algebras legitimately declaring the same method name+arity made every call to that name a hard, unconditional `AmbiguousOperator` rejection — no way to say which one was meant. `doc/grammar.md` already documented the intended fix and the grammar already parsed it structurally (`path = { ident ~ ("::" ~ ident)* }`) — `Ring::mul(a, b)` already produced a 2-segment `Path`, nothing downstream did anything useful with it.

**Confirmed by direct investigation, not assumed, that fixing type-checking alone would not be sufficient** — this is why the user was asked, and explicitly chose the full fix over the narrower one.

**`infer.rs::infer_call` — the type-checking half.** A new branch checked *before* the ordinary unqualified path: a 2-segment `path` whose first segment names a real, registry-declared algebra (`registry.has_algebra`) is unambiguously a qualified call, and dispatches straight to `infer_algebra_call` (the same function the *unqualified*, unambiguous-candidate path already used) — bypassing the ambiguity search entirely, by construction, since the algebra is already named. A pre-check (`registry.fn_sig(algebra, method)`, arity-matched) is needed before that dispatch: `infer_algebra_call` itself `unreachable!()`s on a missing signature, since its one prior caller always already confirmed the method exists — a miss here is a genuine user error, not an invariant violation, so it returns a new `TypeErrorKind::UnknownAlgebraMethod { algebra, method }` instead. Operator sugar (`a * b`) can never accidentally take this branch — `lower.rs`'s `fold_binary` always builds a single-segment path — and a 2-segment path whose first segment *isn't* a declared algebra falls through to the unqualified path unchanged, a real fallback path, not just an assumption (see the test below).

**`cps.rs`/`monomorphize.rs` — the real second half, found necessary by direct code reading, not guessed.** `cps.rs::build_call_index` keys purely on `(bare_method_name, arg_tys, ret_ty)` — no algebra at all. Two algebras both implementing the same method for the same concrete types (exactly the case qualified syntax exists to disambiguate) would let the second `HashMap::insert` silently overwrite the first, with zero collision detection — never previously observable, because `infer_call`'s own ambiguity check always rejected such a program before CPS conversion ever ran; fixing the type-checking half alone would have made this newly, silently reachable. Fixed by reusing the existing `ctx.call_names: HashMap<NodeId, String>` escape hatch (already used by `resolve_call` to bypass `call_index` for other "already resolved, no ambiguity possible" cases) — `monomorphize.rs::collect_instantiations_expr` now recognizes a qualified call structurally (`path.segments` is `[algebra, method]` and some template's own `algebra` field matches it — a safe proxy at this pipeline stage, since type-checking has already run and rejected any qualified call naming a nonexistent algebra or undeclared method) and writes `call_names` directly for it, skipping `call_index` entirely.

`monomorphize.rs::derive_impl_instantiation` — the existing "find the one `ImplTemplate` matching this method+arg-types" resolver — gained an `algebra: Option<&str>` filter parameter, `None` at both pre-existing call sites (byte-for-byte unchanged behavior there, confirmed via full regression run). One real behavioral fork was needed, not just a filter: a matched *non-generic* template previously always returned `NoCandidates` (correct for an ordinary call — `call_index` already resolves an unambiguous concrete impl fine — but wrong for a qualified one, exactly the case `call_index` can't be trusted for). A new `ImplMatch::FoundConcrete(usize)` variant carries that match through when `algebra` was given, reusing `display_impl_instantiation` with an empty substitution mapping (a correct no-op for an already-fully-concrete template) to produce the same unit-name format `cps.rs` expects elsewhere.

Verified via: four `infer.rs` tests (`qualified_call_resolves_to_the_named_algebra_even_when_two_algebras_collide` — the qualified form resolving what the unqualified form can't; `unqualified_call_between_colliding_algebras_still_rejects_as_ambiguous` — a direct regression guard, proving the unqualified form doesn't start silently picking one candidate; `qualified_call_naming_a_method_the_algebra_does_not_declare_is_rejected` — real `UnknownAlgebraMethod`; `a_two_segment_path_whose_first_segment_is_not_an_algebra_falls_through_unaffected` — the non-algebra fallback path actually taken, not just assumed). One real JIT test in `tests/mlir_lower.rs`, the actual proof and the reason this was scoped as a full fix rather than "just the typing": `qualified_calls_to_two_algebras_colliding_on_the_same_method_and_type_run_their_own_bodies` — two algebras, `Foo`/`Bar`, both declaring `foo(x: i32) -> i32` with genuinely different bodies (`x+1`/`x*2`), each called via explicit qualification in the same program, asserting both correct, *different* results — direct end-to-end evidence `build_call_index`'s own collision is actually avoided at the CPS/MLIR level, not just that the type error disappeared. Also manually verified end-to-end via `--run` before the formal tests were written. Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green (183/98 in the two touched suites, up from 179/97, every other suite's count unchanged); `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected (a pure addition).

### `DynArray<T>` — a real, growable collection

`doc/backlog.md`'s own former "No dynamic-size collection" item — the confirmed blocker for output-size-unknown-at-compile-time workloads like convex hull/triangulation (every array was fixed-size, known at compile time — `Ty::Array(elem, size)`, `mlir_lower.rs::alloc_array` allocated a statically-sized `memref`, no growable variant anywhere). The item's own open design fork — "true growable" vs. "fixed upper bound + runtime length" — is resolved in favor of a real, `realloc`-doubling growable collection, `DynArray<T>` (`stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`).

**Built entirely as an ordinary stdlib struct + algebra impls + a handful of new `cleave-rt` extern fns — zero changes to `Ty`, `ast.rs`, `grammar.pest`'s own type grammar, `cps.rs`, or `mlir_lower.rs`.** Not a simplification for its own sake — this is the discipline `stdlib/vector/vector.cleave`'s own top comment already documents: an earlier version of `Vector<T,N>` was a hardcoded `Ty::Vector` Rust variant, reverted for "closing the loop," the exact anti-pattern `doc/hld.md` warns against, in favor of an ordinary stdlib struct needing no `Ty`/grammar changes at all. This applies the identical discipline to a genuinely *dynamic*-size collection.

**`RawBuf` — a real, declared, zero-field `struct RawBuf {}`** (`grammar.pest::struct_decl`'s own field list is already optional, `"{" ~ field_list? ~ "}"` — no grammar change needed even for this) backs the raw heap buffer. This also directly closes `doc/backlog.md`'s own separate "No opaque/foreign-pointer type for FFI-style resources" item, raised by the project owner while reviewing this feature's plan — a zero-field struct is exactly the "declared, nominal, distinguishable-from-`i64`" opaque handle that item asked for; `mlir_lower.rs::ty_to_mlir`'s own existing struct fallback (any non-primitive `Ty::Con`/`Ty::App` is an opaque `!llvm.ptr`) is all it ever needs. One honest residual gap versus a "real" `extern type Handle;`: nothing stops accidentally writing `RawBuf()` (an empty struct literal, legal today, would silently heap-allocate a meaningless zero-byte block) — nothing in `dynarray.cleave` ever does, but a future FFI use of this same trick should keep it in mind.

**`cleave-rt/src/lib.rs`**: one shared, byte-count-based `cleave_realloc(ptr, old_size, new_size)` (every per-width `dynarray_grow_*`/`dynarray_alloc_*` delegates to it, converting its own element count to bytes — hardcoded per width, exactly how `print_i32`/`print_f64`/... already hardcode their own width, no generic `sizeof` mechanism needed anywhere), plus four tiny per-width functions (`alloc`/`grow`/`get`/`set`) generated via one `macro_rules!` invoked once per width — six primitive widths (`i8`/`i16`/`i32`/`i64`/`f32`/`f64`, matching `Print<T>`/`Ring<T>`'s own existing width list) plus one more, `ptr` (`*mut u8`-element storage) — the "any struct element" case, since every cleave struct value is already an opaque pointer of exactly this shape, so this one width's functions are reusable as-is by *any future* struct-element `DynArray<Struct>`, no new Rust code needed per struct type, only a new `impl RawBuffer<Struct>` on the cleave side. **Memory**: unlike `cleave_alloc` (deliberately leaked, no free), a real `cleave_realloc` grow does *not* leak — `std::alloc::realloc` either extends in place or moves the data and frees the old block itself. What's still true, unchanged from every other struct in this codebase: a `DynArray`'s own *final* buffer is never freed once the value itself is discarded — cleave has no `drop`/ownership story anywhere yet, not a new gap this introduces.

**`stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`**: `algebra RawBuffer<T> { alloc, grow, get, set }`, one `impl` per primitive width (mechanical, `Print<T>`'s own per-width shape), each `extern(dynarray_*)`-bound to its `cleave-rt` counterpart. `DynArray<T> { buf: RawBuf, len: i32, cap: i32 }`, with `dynarray_new<T>(initial_cap: i32)` (ordinary top-level generic constructor — an inherent method's own first parameter always fills from an existing value, so a zero-argument constructor can't be one) and `impl<T> struct DynArray<T> { push, get, set, len }` as ordinary generic *inherent* methods (`registry.inherent_method`, a wholly separate dispatch mechanism from `RawBuffer<T>`'s own algebra dispatch — no name collision despite both levels using `get`/`set`). `v[i]` read syntax reuses `vector`'s own already-built `Index<Container,Elem,K>` fallback dispatch directly (`use vector;` inside `dynarray.cleave`, a crate depending on another crate, the same pattern `nn.cleave` already uses for `vector`/`convert`) — `v[i] = x` write syntax is deliberately not attempted, `infer.rs`'s own `StmtKind::Assign` guard rejects a non-array `Index` assignment target outright today; `DynArray` mutation goes through `.set(i, x)` instead. No bounds checking anywhere, matching the codebase's already-established "no runtime memory-safety enforcement" posture (`vector`'s own dynamic-index `Index` impls have none either).

**A real, previously-latent compiler gap found directly while writing `push` — not a `DynArray`-specific problem, a general one**: `infer.rs::check_mutability` treated *every* function parameter as unconditionally immutable (hardcoded `false`, no exception), and `assign_target_root` walks a field-assignment target (`v.buf = x`) all the way down to its root variable and checks *that* variable's own declared mutability — meaning no function could ever mutate a field of a struct passed to it as a plain parameter, only through a local `let mut`. Every existing field-mutation example in this codebase happened to go through a local, never a parameter, so this had never surfaced. Confirmed with the project owner before fixing (this plan had originally promised zero compiler changes) — fixed at the root, not worked around: `fn f(mut v: T)` is now legal, mirroring `let mut`'s own identical marker exactly (`grammar.pest`'s `param` rule gained the same, already-existing `mut_kw?`; `ast::Param` gained a `mutable: bool` field; `lower.rs::lower_param` reads it the same way `lower_let_stmt` already does; `check_mutability`'s own scope-seeding uses `p.mutable` instead of a hardcoded `false`). The considered alternative — `push` returning a *new* `DynArray<T>`, requiring `v = v.push(x);` at every call site — was rejected: it would have silently broken aliasing (`v2 = v; v.push(x);` wouldn't be visible through `v2`), a real semantic inconsistency with every other struct in the language, which is always a shared, in-place-mutable heap reference.

**A second real, if much smaller, parser gap found the same way**: a statement-position `if` with no `else`, followed by more code in the same block, needs an explicit trailing `;` after its closing `}` to parse at all (`stmt = { let_stmt | assign_stmt | expr_stmt }`, and `expr_stmt` itself requires a trailing `;` — an `if_expr` with no semicolon can only ever be a block's own *tail* expression, i.e. the last thing in it). Not a new bug — an existing test (`a_compiled_program_actually_runs_and_returns_the_right_value`'s neighbor, the `saw_three` while-loop test) already relied on exactly this, `if i == 3 { saw_three = 1; };` — `push`'s own first draft simply omitted the semicolon.

**A third real, and the deepest, compiler gap — found only once a genuinely realistic multi-type program (`examples/convex_hull.cleave`) exercised `dynarray_new` for the first time, invisible in every earlier test:** `dynarray_new<T>()`'s own original, zero-value-parameter signature hit `callgraph.rs`'s own deliberate Monomorphism Restriction for nullary top-level fns (`f.params.is_empty()`, its own doc comment there — mirrors Haskell's, on the stated reasoning that a no-argument binding "can't accept any caller-supplied type information at its call sites") — its `T` was **never generalized at all** (confirmed directly via a temporary debug print: `scheme.vars=[]`), staying one single, shared, *monomorphic* type variable for the entire compiled program rather than a fresh one per call site. Every prior test happened to use `DynArray` at exactly one concrete `T` per compiled program, so the bug stayed invisible; the first program combining `DynArray<Point>` with *any* other float-fielded struct construction elsewhere in the same file broke immediately (`expected i32, found f64`-style errors, tracing back to unrelated struct-literal field defaults happening to alias the same shared variable). `callgraph.rs`'s own nullary gate checks *argument count*, not whether `T` actually appears in a parameter — so the real fix was giving `dynarray_new` a genuine parameter it needed anyway (`initial_cap: i32`, the caller's own starting capacity, no longer hardcoded to `4` internally), sidestepping the gate entirely with correct, real let-polymorphism, zero compiler changes. The rejected alternative — a dummy phantom parameter purely to satisfy the gate — was avoided since a real, useful parameter was available instead.

Verified via: manual end-to-end `--run` checks for both the scalar (`DynArray<i32>`, growth past initial capacity, `.get`/`v[i]`) and struct-element (`DynArray<Point>`) cases before any formal test was written. Three real JIT tests in `tests/mlir_lower.rs`: `a_dynarray_grows_past_its_initial_capacity_and_reads_back_correct_values` (pushes 6 elements past the initial capacity of 4, forcing a real grow, reads every element back via both `.get(i)` and `v[i]`, plus `.len()`), `a_dynarray_of_structs_grows_and_reads_back_correct_field_values` (`DynArray<Point>`, five pushes, reads field values back — the direct proof the `_ptr`-width raw storage round-trips a real heap-referenced struct, not just a scalar), and `dynarray_generalizes_correctly_across_two_different_concrete_types_in_one_program` (`DynArray<i32>` and `DynArray<Point>` constructed and used simultaneously — the direct regression proof for the Monomorphism Restriction bug above). A real, working demo, `examples/convex_hull.cleave` — gift wrapping (Jarvis march), a square with four interior points correctly excluded, `DynArray<Point>` as the hull's own growable output — which also surfaced a fourth, separate gap, a `while`-loop condition needing more than one chained real call, fixed separately (see "A `while`-loop condition needing more than one chained real call" below). Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green (101 in `mlir_lower`, up from 98, every other suite's count unchanged including `infer.rs`'s own 183 — the `mut`-parameter change caused zero regressions); `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep green including the new `convex_hull.cleave`.

### A `while`-loop condition needing more than one chained real call

`doc/backlog.md`'s own item, opened and closed in the same pass: `while i < hull.len() { ... }` (`examples/convex_hull.cleave`) panicked at MLIR-lowering time — `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop` assumed a loop's own condition always CPS-converts to *exactly one* real call (`Ord::lt<...>`, say) whose own continuation is immediately a bare `If`, correct for every condition shape exercised until now (`i < 5`, `not done`, ...) but not for `i < hull.len()`, which needs *two* sequential calls first (`DynArray::len<...>`, then `Ord::lt` against its result).
