**Fixed by walking the condition as a chain, not assuming a single call.** `lower_loop`'s own condition-parsing — previously a single `let-else` chain expecting exactly `Fix{defs:[cond_k], body: App{...}}` with `cond_k.body` immediately a bare `If` — became a small loop: repeatedly matches `Fix{defs:[k], body: App{Label(callee), args}}`, records `(callee, args, result_var)`, and follows `k.body` — either another such layer, or, terminally, the bare `If` that ends the chain. Mirrors the exact same "keep following the next `Fix`" shape ordinary straight-line lowering already uses (`lower_real_call`'s own recursive continuation-following) — just walked by hand in a loop here, since each call in the chain has to land in the loop's own "before" block, ending in `scf.condition`, not an ordinary recursive `lower_cexpr` step. The "before"-block-building code that used to emit one `func.call` now loops over the recorded chain instead, threading each call's own result into `before_env` (so a later call in the chain can reference an earlier one's result, exactly as `Ord::lt`'s second argument here references `hull.len()`'s) and using the *last* call's result as `scf.condition`'s own boolean operand. No change needed anywhere else — `cps.rs`'s own CPS conversion of `while`/`for` already produced this exact chained shape; `mlir_lower.rs` was the only piece not yet able to consume it.

`examples/convex_hull.cleave`'s own earlier workaround (caching `hull.len()` into a local before the loop) was removed — the natural `while i < hull.len() { ... }` now just works.

Verified via: a new JIT test in `tests/mlir_lower.rs`, `a_while_condition_needing_two_chained_calls_lowers_and_runs_correctly` — pushes 3 elements onto a `DynArray<i32>`, sums them while looping against the *live* `hull.len()` (not a value cached before the loop), asserting both the correct final index and the correct sum — a wrong loop bound would either skip elements or read out of bounds, not just fail to compile. Full `cargo test --workspace` (`--test-threads=1`) green (102 in `mlir_lower`, up from 101, every other suite's count unchanged); `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep green, including `convex_hull.cleave` with its workaround removed.

### `break`/`break value` in loops, plus a new `loop { }` construct

`doc/backlog.md`'s own former "`break value` in loops" item, closed by the full, planned scope in one pass, mirroring Rust's own split: plain `break;` now works inside `while`/`for`/`for-in` (their own type stays `()`, as always); a new, unconditional `loop { }` expression is the only construct that can produce a value via `break value;`, since only it has no other, ambiguous "natural" exit value to reconcile against. `break` targets the nearest enclosing loop and cannot escape a closure (`infer.rs`'s own `loop_stack` is temporarily emptied while checking a `Lambda`'s own body).

**The real difficulty, confirmed before starting**: `scf.while`/`scf.if` (what every cleave loop/if already lowers to) have no native early-exit primitive — every region must terminate in `scf.yield` with a fixed arity, every time. The standard technique (used by Wasm, SPIR-V, and others under the identical constraint) is a synthetic `running: bool` threaded through the loop's own carried state, ANDed into the real condition, set `false` by `break` — the loop then exits through its own already-correct natural-exit path one iteration later. This reuses the chained-call condition-walk just above directly (`running AND cond` is exactly a two-call chain). `Ctx` gained `break_targets: RefCell<Vec<BreakTarget>>` (`{ running: CVar, break_val: Option<(CVar, Ty)> }`), pushed/popped by each of the four loop-conversion sites — but only when a new `loop_contains_break` scan (mirrors `mutated_free_vars`'s own scoping-aware recursion, stopping at a nested loop's or lambda's own body) finds one, so an ordinary, break-free loop compiles to *exactly* the same MLIR as before — zero added carried state, zero cost — confirmed by a dedicated regression test, not just assumed.

**Three real design bugs found and fixed by direct testing, not assumed:**
- A first design had `break` tail-call straight to the loop's own label, bypassing the block's own remaining continuation entirely — correct when `break` sits directly in the loop body, but an MLIR verifier failure (arity mismatch) the moment it sat inside a nested `if`, since a tail-jump out of a deeper `scf.if` region still has to satisfy *that* region's own declared yield arity.
- A second attempt threaded `running`/`break_val` through every enclosing `if`'s own join (a real, necessary, and still-present piece of the final design) and had the join *itself* decide whether to jump onward — this ran and type-checked but produced a silently wrong number, since ordinary statements textually following a break-containing `if` kept executing with stale values instead of being skipped. The actual fix belongs at statement-sequencing time, not inside any branch: a new `continue_after` function (called from every one of `convert_stmts`'s own "now continue with the rest of the block" sites, in place of a direct recursive call) checks whether the statement just converted might have set `running := false` and, only then, wraps the remaining statements in a synthesized guard — a pure no-op pass-through for the overwhelming majority of statements that can't possibly break.
- `CVal::Unit`, used as `loop { }`'s own break-value slot's pre-any-break placeholder, reached `lower_cval` unfiltered and panicked (no arm handled it) — fixed with a new arm building `llvm.mlir.undef` (melior's `dialect::llvm::undef`), MLIR's own real "value of this type, deliberately unspecified" primitive, correctly typed against whatever the carried slot's own type is.

**A fourth, unrelated regression found only by the full examples sweep, after every unit test was already green**: `examples/tensor_demo.cleave` and `examples/mandelbrot.cleave` — both untouched by this feature, neither using `break`/`loop{}` at all — started crashing with a native stack overflow. Root-caused (not guessed) by bisecting the compile pipeline with temporary debug prints down to `cps.rs::convert_program`'s own per-unit conversion of `main`, then confirmed directly by reverting to the pre-feature commit (same file, same debug build: no crash) and by re-running under an artificially enlarged stack (succeeds at 16MB, fails under the platform default). CPS conversion recurses one Rust stack frame per AST node converted; `continue_after`'s own new indirection adds one more frame per statement, on every statement in the program, tipping an already large, unrelated example over the OS's default main-thread stack size — nothing to do with loops or breaks specifically. Fixed the same way `rustc`'s own driver handles the identical, well-known class of problem: `main()` (`cleave/src/main.rs`) now runs the whole compile pipeline on a worker thread with a fixed, generous stack (64MB) instead of the OS default, sidestepping the platform limit rather than trying to shrink CPS conversion's own inherent recursion depth.

Verified via: real JIT tests in `tests/mlir_lower.rs` covering a bare `break` inside `while`/`for`/`for-in`, a break several frames deep inside a nested `if`, a `loop { }` returning its break value, nested loops (confirming a break only affects the innermost one), and the zero-cost regression (`main`'s own body specifically checked for the *absence* of any `Logic::and` call when no loop in the program contains a break). `tests/infer.rs` covers `break` outside any loop, a mismatched `break value` type, a `loop`'s own inferred type from `break value`, disagreeing break values across two sites, and a break inside a lambda not escaping to an outer loop. Full `cargo test --workspace` green throughout; `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep green across all eleven examples (`linear_regression.cleave` excluded, its own separately-tracked compile-time slowness unrelated to this work), including `tensor_demo.cleave`/`mandelbrot.cleave` after the stack-size fix.

### A generic call's own explicit turbofish is never consulted for monomorphization

`doc/backlog.md`'s own former item: `monomorphize.rs`'s `derive_instantiation` (recovers a generic function's own concrete instantiation at a call site) worked purely by reverse-unifying the call's own argument/return *value* types against the function's declared shape — the call expression's own explicit turbofish (`ExprKind::Call`'s second field) was discarded outright (`(path, _, args, ..)`). Harmless for an ordinary generic parameter (always recoverable from some argument's own concrete type), but a real gap for a const generic that only ever appears *combined* with another one in a parameter's own type (`fn f<const N, M>(x: [T; N+M])`, no parameter mentioning `N`/`M` individually): reverse-unifying `scheme.ty`'s own still-symbolic `ConstExpr("add", Var(N), Var(M))` against one concrete array length is genuinely underdetermined, and `unify`'s own `ConstExpr`-against-`Const` arm has no rule for it by design (falls through to `Mismatch`) — confirmed directly, not assumed: without this fix, `combined::<3,4>(seven_element_array)` panics at MLIR-lowering time, "array size must be a resolved constant, got `add('t0, 't1)`", since `derive_instantiation` silently returned `None` and the call was never seeded for monomorphization at all.

**Fixed by unifying the turbofish first, before the ordinary reverse-unification runs — no arithmetic reasoning needed.** `derive_instantiation` gained an `explicit_generics: &[GenericArg]` parameter (threaded through from `collect_instantiations_expr`'s `ExprKind::Call` arm, which no longer discards it); each turbofish argument, converted to a `Ty` and unified directly against its own `scheme.vars[i]`, gets bound into the same `Subst` *before* `scheme.ty` is reverse-unified against the call's `arg_tys`/`ret_ty`. Once `N`/`M` are already bound, `Subst::apply`'s own constant-folding (`fold_const_expr`, already existing machinery) collapses `ConstExpr("add", Const(3), Const(4))` to `Const(7)` the moment `unify` applies the substitution to `scheme.ty`, so the ordinary `Array`-vs-`Array` arm just matches — the "combined, underdetermined" problem disappears once `N`/`M` are supplied *separately* by the turbofish, regardless of what arithmetic combines them in the signature.

**No `Infer`/`TyVarGen` instance needed here either, matching this module's own established posture (see its own doc comment)**: two new small, `Infer`-free functions, `concrete_ty_from_ast`/`concrete_const_from_expr`, mirror `infer.rs`'s own `generic_arg_to_ty`/`const_value_from_expr` but only handle the fully-literal case (a bare type name, an integer/bool literal, or an arithmetic combination of literals) — by this pipeline stage type-checking has already run, so an explicit turbofish argument is always one of those; a turbofish argument that instead references some *other*, still-open generic (not expected in practice at a call site whose own argument/return types are already fully concrete) falls through to `None`, exactly as if no turbofish had been given at all — `derive_instantiation`'s ordinary reverse-unification stays the fallback, unaffected either way.

Verified via: a new test in `tests/monomorphize.rs`, `an_explicit_turbofish_resolves_a_const_generic_that_only_appears_combined_with_another_one` — two calls to the same `combined<const N, const M>` at different turbofish (`::<3,4>` and `::<1,2>`, arrays of length 7 and 3 respectively), asserting two *distinct* specializations are produced, each with its own correctly and independently resolved `N`/`M` (not collapsed together, not swapped) reflected in its own array parameter type. Confirmed the bug was real, and the fix actually needed, by reverting the fix (`git stash`) and re-running the same repro directly (`--run`), reproducing the exact panic described above; restoring the fix resolves it. Full `cargo test --workspace` green (16 in `monomorphize`, up from 15, every other suite's count unchanged); `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected.

### Tuples, validated via multi-argument `print`

`doc/backlog.md`'s own two former items, "Tuples as a language feature" and "Multi-argument, heterogeneous `print`" (which depended on it): `(T1, T2, ...)` as a type, `(a, b, ...)` as a literal, and `t.0`/`t.1` field access (arity 2+ — a 0-tuple is already `()`, cleave's own unit spelling; a 1-tuple has no motivating use case yet, both easy to add later), validated end to end via `print(("x=", x, "y=", y))` — a genuinely heterogeneous call mixing two string literals, an `i32`, and an `f64`.

**Design: zero new compiler primitives, reusing the struct pipeline entirely** — the same discipline every other feature in this codebase already follows (`DynArray`, `RawBuf`, `Vector`/`Matrix`/`Tensor`, `Complex<T>`). `(T1, T2)`/`(a, b)` desugar, entirely at `lower.rs` time (pure syntax, no semantic knowledge needed), into the *exact* AST shapes an ordinary generic struct's own type/literal syntax already produces — `TypeKind::Path("__Tuple2", [T1, T2])` and `ExprKind::StructLit("__Tuple2", [], [("0",a), ("1",b)])` (`ast::tuple_struct_name`, `__`-prefixed matching this codebase's own existing convention for a reserved, unwritable-from-source name — `cps.rs`'s `"__loop_running"`/`"__break_value"` from the `break` feature). `t.0`/`t.1` need **no new AST shape at all** — they parse to the exact same `ExprKind::FieldAccess(t, "0")` an ordinary named struct field access already produces; nothing downstream of parsing can tell a tuple field access apart from an ordinary struct's own field literally named `"0"`. `driver.rs::synthesize_tuple_structs` injects the real `struct __Tuple2<T0,T1> { 0: T0, 1: T1 }`..`__Tuple4` declarations (`MAX_TUPLE_ARITY = 4`, trivially raised later) into every compiled program, unconditionally — the same posture the prelude (`num`/`logic`) already takes; monomorphization only ever specializes what's actually reachable, so an unused arity is inert. Once these are ordinary registered structs, `infer.rs`'s existing `StructLit`/`FieldAccess` inference, `cps.rs`'s `StructSchema`, and `mlir_lower.rs`'s existing heap-allocated struct construction/field access need **no changes at all** — confirmed directly: every scenario (construction, unannotated inference, nested tuples with chained field access, `t.0 = x;` mutation through `let mut` — falls out for free from reusing ordinary struct semantics, not specifically designed for — and a generic function over an explicitly-annotated tuple parameter) worked on the first try. This also means a tuple is a heap-allocated reference value like every other struct (not the `#[mlir_type(tensor)]` native-value path `Vector`/`Matrix`/`Tensor` use) — a deliberate, flagged simplification (one small heap allocation per construction, leaked forever, the same already-accepted "no dealloc anywhere" tradeoff every struct already has); a genuine value-type tuple is a natural future optimization, not attempted here. Variadic generics (which would let a single `Print<(...)>`-style impl cover any arity, instead of one hand-written impl per arity) were raised as a related idea and deliberately deferred to their own backlog item — orthogonal to tuples themselves.

`stdlib/io/io.cleave` gained `impl<A: Print, B: Print> Print<(A,B)>`/`<(A,B,C)>`/`<(A,B,C,D)>`, each printing every element in order and returning the tuple unchanged (matching every other `Print<T>` impl's identity contract) — one impl per arity, no variadic generics needed, exactly the same posture `Vector`/`Matrix`/`Tensor` already established for rank.

**Two real, pre-existing, previously-latent bugs found while validating via `print` — neither specific to tuples, both real regardless of this feature:**

- **A duplicate-specialization bug, found the moment a *second* generic impl of the same algebra/method (`Print`) existed anywhere in the same crate as `impl<const N: i32> Print<[i8; N]>`** (which, until this work, was `Print`'s only generic impl — every other one is a single concrete width). `cps.rs::collect_units`'s own `ItemKind::Impl` branch iterates every impl of an algebra, and for *each* one, re-queries `mono.specializations_of("Algebra::method")` — the *full*, shared list of every specialization under that origin name, not just the ones that specific impl produced — then derived `UnitBody::Real`/`UnitBody::Extern` from *that* impl's own `FnDecl`, not from the specialization actually being processed. With two generic `Print` impls sharing the method name `print`, this silently rebuilt the *other* impl's own already-correct specialization using the *wrong* impl's body/extern-ness; the resulting duplicate `ConcreteUnit` (identical name, wrong body) silently overwrote the correct one in `convert_program`'s own `by_name` map (a plain `HashMap` — last-inserted wins). Confirmed by direct testing: `print("x=")` alone (no tuple, no wrapper call) failed MLIR verification (`'func.return' op has 0 operands, but enclosing function ... returns 1`) the moment *any* second generic `Print<...>` impl existed anywhere in `stdlib/io/io.cleave` — reverting to a single generic impl, or moving the second one to a different file/crate, made it disappear, isolating the bug precisely to "two generic impls of the same algebra/method, declared in the same file." **Fixed at the root**: `Specialization`/`ImplTemplate` (`monomorphize.rs`) each gained `is_extern`/`extern_symbol` fields, populated once per template from its own correct `FnDecl` (`build_impl_templates`, which has the *right* `f` in scope, unlike `collect_units`'s own outer loop) and read back via new `MonomorphizedProgram::is_extern`/`extern_symbol` accessors — `collect_units` now asks the specialization itself, never whichever impl the outer loop currently happens to be iterating.
- **An array-in-struct-field representation mismatch, found printing a tuple's own string-typed field** (`x.0: [i8;N]`, the string literal `"x="`). A cleave array has two different MLIR representations depending on where it currently lives: a standalone array (local/parameter) is a real `memref`; the *same* array type read back out of a struct/tuple field (`lower_field_access`'s own `is_array_ty` branch) is already a bare `!llvm.ptr` into that struct's own inline storage, never a `memref` at all (`struct_llvm_type`'s own established design). `mlir_lower.rs::array_ptr_and_len` (used by every `extern fn`'s own array-typed argument, `Print<[i8;N]>::print` chief among them) unconditionally assumed the `memref` shape, calling `memref.extract_aligned_pointer_as_index` on whatever it was handed — a hard MLIR verification failure (`operand #0 must be ranked or unranked memref... but got '!llvm.ptr'`) the first time an array value reaching an extern boundary came from a struct field rather than a plain variable. **Fixed by branching on the lowered value's own actual MLIR type** (`Value::r#type().is_mem_ref()`, already the established pattern this exact "two representations" problem uses elsewhere in this file — `copy_array_into_llvm_field`'s own identical `src_is_mem_ref` check): the `memref` branch is unchanged; the already-`!llvm.ptr` branch skips the extraction entirely and uses the pointer directly, since it already *is* the value this function exists to produce.

Verified via: real JIT tests in `tests/mlir_lower.rs` — tuple construction/field read-back, a heterogeneous (`i32`, `f64`) tuple, a nested tuple through chained field access, `t.0 = x;` mutation, a function over an explicit tuple parameter type, and the full `print(("x=", x, "y=", y))` validation (registering `print_i32`/`print_f64`/`print_bytes` on `run_i32_with_dynarray_symbols`'s own `extra_symbols` slot, since the base `run_i32` helper registers no `print_*` symbols at all). New parser tests cover tuple type/literal syntax, `.0`/`.1` field access (including chained, `t.0.1` — confirmed directly this PEG grammar splits it into two separate `postfix_op`s, not misparsed as one merged token), and that a single parenthesized expression still takes the ordinary grouping path, not `tuple_lit`. New `tests/infer.rs` tests (via a new `inject_tuple_struct` helper, injecting the same synthesized struct `driver.rs` would, since this file's own `infer_fn_named` deliberately tests `Infer` in isolation, bypassing `driver::compile`) cover tuple-literal inference, field access, out-of-range field access (falls out of the ordinary `NoSuchField` error, no bespoke diagnostic needed), and a nested tuple. Both real bugs confirmed via direct isolation before fixing (minimal repros with the fix reverted, reproducing the exact failure; restored, resolving it) — not assumed from reading the code alone. Full `cargo test --workspace` green throughout (115 in `mlir_lower`, up from 109; 194 in `infer`, up from 188; 80 in `parser`, up from 72); `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep unaffected (no existing example used tuples; `mandelbrot.cleave`'s own heavy `print`/array usage re-verified unchanged after the two bug fixes).

### No memory deallocation anywhere — every heap allocation leaked forever, now fixed for the dominant (tensor-payload) case via a real region-local arena allocator

`cleave_alloc` (backing every struct construction) was deliberately leaked, no `free` — documented directly in its own `cleave-rt` doc comment every time it was touched. `DynArray<T>`'s own `cleave_realloc` (this file's own "growable collections" item) doesn't leak *during* growth (`std::alloc::realloc` frees the old block itself) but the *final* buffer was never freed once a `DynArray` value itself went out of scope — the same accepted gap every other struct already had. No `drop`/ownership/lifetime concept exists anywhere in the language, still.

**Raised directly by the project owner, with a real, promising angle: CPS's own explicit continuation-threading may make this more tractable than it would be in a typical direct-style/AST-based compiler.** In CPS, every value's remaining use is *explicit* — it's either passed as an argument into the current continuation (or returned) or it isn't; there's no implicit "still reachable via some later statement" the way ordinary direct-style control flow has. That's structurally close to a real liveness/last-use analysis already, for free, from the representation itself — the same property CPS-based compilers (and, in a different form, Tofte/Talpin-style region inference) have exploited for automatic deallocation before.

**Not free of real complications: cleave structs are *shared, mutable, heap references*** (`s.x = v` mutates in place, "never copied," established throughout this codebase's own `struct_llvm_type` doc comment and reused directly by `DynArray<T>::push`'s own `mut v` parameter), so more than one binding/parameter can legitimately alias the same underlying allocation (`let a = s; let b = s;` — `a`/`b` refer to the same object). A naive "not used again after this point" check on a single *name* isn't enough without also knowing whether some *other* still-live binding aliases the same object — real escape/alias analysis, not just a syntactic last-use scan.

**`doc/hld.md`'s own "Memory management: a region/stack discipline derived from CPS, not a GC" section worked this out into a real design.** Triggered directly by this exact gap becoming a hard crash, not just a theoretical concern: training a real, MNIST-scale network (`784->512->256->128->10`, 60,000 samples) hit a genuine `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` partway through one epoch, confirmed by bisection to be scale-dependent (a 100-sample slice of the identical program ran fine). The HLD section works out the escape-analysis-per-CPS-unit idea into a real design: a four-operation region interface (`region_enter`/`region_exit`/`alloc_local`/`alloc_escaping`) decoupling the (backend-agnostic) escape analysis from the (backend-specific) physical allocator.

**Phase 0, the struct/descriptor side, landed first — `cleave::refcount::insert_refcounting`** (`cleave/src/refcount.rs`, wired into `pipeline.rs::build_optimized_cps` and `main.rs`'s own `--run`/`--dump-cps-optimized`, right after the e-graph pass), on top of the already-shipped `cleave_alloc_rc`/`cleave_retain`/`cleave_release` runtime (`cleave-rt`). The rule: a function releases exactly the struct-typed values it itself freshly allocated (`PrimOp::Struct`), minus whichever one it returns; parameters are always borrowed, never released by the callee; storing an *existing* struct-typed value into another struct's own field *or* embedding it at construction time (`Line(a: p1, b: p2)`) retains it first. Ownership-based, not last-use — sound without any dataflow fixpoint over general control flow, by construction. Verified via 7 real, JIT-executing regression tests (`cleave/tests/refcount.rs`) and, decisively, via `examples/digits-interop`'s own real training run: 94.7% accuracy, the exact original baseline, now with refcounting genuinely active.

Getting there took three real, sequentially-discovered correctness bugs, each found only by real end-to-end execution: (1) a `PrimOp::Field` read off a *borrowed* base (e.g. `model.l1`, `model` a function parameter) is itself borrowed too — treating every struct-typed free variable a continuation still needs as *that continuation's own* release responsibility released a network's own weights out from under its caller; (2) a free variable needed by *both* an outer, recursively-re-entered loop and an inner one nested inside it can only be claimed by *one* of them — the shallowest; (3) a "trampoline" continuation (a loop's own back-edge resumption, whose entire body is just a bare tail-call to the loop itself) doesn't *directly* reference anything the loop needs, so a live-variable check has to be transitive through such a tail-call. Each manifested as a real crash only at real (digits-interop) scale — bisected down to a 5-line/2-epoch repro each time, then converted into permanent regression tests.

**MLIR's own `--buffer-deallocation-pipeline` was tried for the tensor-payload half and reverted — a real correctness regression, not just an incomplete win.** `ownership-based-buffer-deallocation`'s analysis had no visibility into cleave's own function-boundary ABI — a struct's `Tensor` field crossed a function boundary as raw decomposed scalars (pointer + shape), re-packed into a `memref` locally via a manually-built `unrealized_conversion_cast`, opaque to the pass — so it couldn't distinguish a *borrowed view* into caller-owned memory from a fresh function-local allocation, and freed the former after first use. `mnist-interop` crashed even at 100 samples/1 epoch; `digits-interop`, re-tested after the struct-side fixes above, no longer hard-crashed but silently trained to 9.9% accuracy (random-guess level) — the same premature-free bug, just not manifesting as an immediate crash at that smaller scale. Confirmed the whole pass assumes function-boundary types are real `memref`s, which cleave's weren't — not a narrow bug to patch. Reverted cleanly, full workspace suite green, `digits-interop` back to 94.7%.

**The real fix that landed: a general, compile-time-chosen, per-allocation-site region/arena allocator — not an MLIR pass, not per-type cascading refcount, one mechanism covering every LLVM-level allocation, struct or tensor payload alike.**

`cleave-rt` gained a real bump-arena: `cleave_region_enter(size) -> handle` (an `i64` cursor snapshot, not a pointer — supports real nesting via a `REGION_DEPTH` counter), `cleave_region_exit(handle)` (rewinds the cursor — bulk reclamation, not per-object `free`), `cleave_alloc_local(handle, size)` (bump-allocates from a lazily-`std::alloc::alloc`'d 256MB arena, 16-byte aligned, writes a real `RcHeader` matching `cleave_alloc_rc`'s own output shape so retain/release code stays identical regardless of which allocator backed a value), and `cleave_release`/`cleave_release_void` were taught to check `is_in_arena(header)` at refcount-zero and skip `dealloc` for an arena-backed value (bulk-reclaimed later by `region_exit`) while still returning `true` for correct cascading into nested refcounted fields.

**A design correction made and caught before shipping, not after**: an early version made `cleave_alloc_rc` itself region-aware via a single ambient "is any region open" flag — unsound, found via a concrete counterexample (`Optimizer::step` runs nested inside the same region `net_grad` needs open for its own result to survive until read, but `Optimizer::step`'s own escaping allocations must never be arena-backed; one ambient flag can't distinguish the two). Reverted: `cleave_alloc_rc` is unconditionally heap-backed (the safe default), and `cleave_alloc_local` is the *only* explicit, compile-time-chosen entry point into the arena.

**A genuine, general, CPS-level escape analysis decides, per top-level function, whether *every* one of its own allocation sites can be arena-backed** — `cleave/src/region_analysis.rs::find_region_local_functions`, run pre-`--inline` (deliberately: a struct value is a bare `!llvm.ptr` at the MLIR level, so nested fields reached via `llvm.store` rather than SSA flow are invisible to any later, post-inline backward walk). For each self-recursive loop, a function's result is marked region-local iff it has exactly one call site in the whole program *and* its result never reaches the loop's own carried/escaping state — tracing through `PrimOp::Field` projections, not just literal identity, and correctly treating a function called from more than one place as never region-local (a wrong classification here would only be caught by `assert_region_open`'s own crash, not silently — the analysis's whole job is to not be wrong in the first place). Wired into `mlir_lower.rs`: `alloc_llvm_value` is the *one* place the `cleave_alloc_rc`-vs-`cleave_alloc_local` choice is made, per allocation site, at compile time; `lower_loop` opens a region once per loop iteration (not per call — a region-local function's result must survive until read by a later, non-region-local call in the same iteration, e.g. `net_grad`'s result read by `Optimizer::step`) and closes it right before `scf.yield`.

**A further generalization — extending the same mechanism to the *construction* of a `Tensor` value itself (not just struct-field crossings) — was tried, measured, and reverted.** A hand-built memref (via `unrealized_conversion_cast` on a from-scratch descriptor, wrapped in `bufferization.to_tensor restrict`) JIT-executed correctly and passed every unit test, but a follow-up VTune profile of the real mnist kernel showed it reintroduced ~63s of real `memcpy` traffic: the hand-built buffer is opaque to One-Shot Bufferize's own alias analysis in a way `tensor.from_elements` isn't, so bufferization could no longer prove downstream reads were copy-free. Fully reverted (`lower_tagged_struct_construct` back to always building `{keyword}.from_elements` unconditionally) — the shipped fix is scoped to struct-boundary field crossings, not tensor construction sites themselves; extending further would need a technique that doesn't defeat bufferization's own alias analysis, not attempted.

**Verified, decisively, on the exact original motivating case**: `examples/mnist-interop` (784→512→256→128→10, the real MNIST dataset, 60,000 training samples, no `MNIST_DEBUG_CAP`) — previously `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` at ~5,000 samples — now trains the full 10 epochs without crashing: **1073.4s total (~107.3s/epoch, no perf drift across epochs — confirms the 256MB bump-arena's rewind-on-region-exit design holds under sustained load), 0.9782 test accuracy.** `examples/digits-interop` unaffected (the mechanism is general, not mnist-specific) — verified via the full JIT test suite plus real kernel dumps confirming zero real `@malloc`/`@free` calls remain post-`unify_alloc.rs` (only structural declarations, renamed in place to `cleave_alloc_rc`/`cleave_release_void` for whatever bufferization-inserted tensor allocation escapes a region, sound because by this pipeline stage cleave's own code never emits raw malloc/free itself).

**Still open, a separate and smaller gap, not touched by this fix**: `cleave_release`'s own non-cascading nature for ordinary (non-region-local) struct fields — see `doc/backlog.md`'s own entry for that.

### Produce a real executable — done, and had already been for a while when this entry was finally corrected

`doc/backlog.md`'s own entry for this had gone stale without anyone noticing: it still described `--emit-object`/`enable_object_dump`/a real `.exe`-linking step as "deliberately not started," a real design question. All of that shipped a while ago and has been exercised heavily since — `cleave::pipeline::emit_object`/`emit_exe` (`melior::ExecutionEngine::dump_to_object_file`, gated by `enable_object_dump: true`), `cleave-build`'s own `Build`/`compile_library` (the `build.rs` integration every real interop example, `examples/rust-interop-demo`/`digits-interop`/`mnist-interop`, already uses), and `--emit-exe` (a real, standalone `.exe`, `emit_exe`'s own generated Rust shim + a real `rustc`-driven link step, `cleave_rt` compiled as a genuine `staticlib`). This whole OpenMP-parallelization/codegen-options session used `--emit-exe`/`cleave-build` repeatedly and directly — `examples/mnist-interop`'s own real `.exe`, linked against `cleave_rt.lib` and (once OpenMP landed) `libomp.lib`, is exactly this mechanism, not JIT.

**What's still genuinely open, correctly separated out now**: the *deeper* design question this entry's own final paragraphs actually raised — a real, separately-compiled, linkable **cleave-to-cleave** library format, carrying enough metadata (declared signatures, generic templates, algebra impls) for a *downstream cleave program* to monomorphize against a precompiled dependency, the way Rust's own `.rlib`/`.rmeta` split does. `extern fn`/`export fn` already solves the *other* interop story fine (calling into/out of compiled cleave across a language boundary to Rust, real and tested) — this is specifically about compiling one cleave crate once and consuming it from *another* cleave program without recompiling from source, never attempted, a real, still-open, deliberately-deferred design question. Moved to `doc/backlog.md` under its own, more precisely-scoped title so it doesn't get lost as "not started" again.

### `derive()`'s own saturation grows combinatorially on realistic loss functions — largely stale, re-measured, and closed

**The original numbers in this entry no longer hold — re-measured directly, not assumed.** `examples/linear_regression.cleave` (the example that originally motivated this item, differentiating a two-parameter, four-point loss function unrolled over 4 iterations) used to grind for 117 iterations and 124,516 e-graph nodes before hitting its own 30-second wall-clock limit. Today, with every real correctness bug this session found and fixed *elsewhere* in the exact same derivative-synthesis pipeline (multi-level call transparency, the `?x` pattern-variable collision, the qualified-call monomorphization gap, the typed-zero/`Ring::zero()` work, ...), it compiles-and-runs in **2.6s total** (debug build) — no code in this specific item was touched to get there; the earlier explosion was very likely substantially *caused* by some combination of those now-fixed bugs (a stuck, unreducible `derivative` marker forcing the saturation to keep exploring alternate paths that could never actually close, say), not purely by axiom-commutativity duplication alone.

**Re-investigated directly, this time on the actual current hot example (`examples/xor.cleave`, not `linear_regression.cleave`)**, since that's what's slow *today*: capping `synthesize_derivatives`'s own `Runner` to a 1-second time limit (down from 30s) for `derive(loss)` — `xor.cleave`'s own heaviest differentiation — stops at `TimeLimit(1.0s)` with only **2,208 e-graph nodes and 200 iterations** (nowhere near the old 124K-node scale), and **the trained output is bit-for-bit identical** to running with the full 30-second budget. `optimize_program`'s own separate saturation pass (axiom simplification on the already-differentiated `grad` function) is not the bottleneck either — it fully saturates in 2 iterations, 479 nodes, effectively instant. **Conclusion: the e-graph itself is no longer where `xor.cleave`'s own compile time goes.**

**Where the time actually goes today, measured directly**: `--dump-mlir-lowered` alone (the full MLIR pass pipeline — elementwise-to-linalg, one-shot-bufferize, linalg-to-loops, scf-to-cf, to-llvm, reconcile-casts — with *no* JIT invocation at all) takes 8.9s on a debug build; the rebuilt `grad` function itself is a genuinely large ~700-operation CPS body (e-graph node count and rebuilt-CPS size diverge because a shared e-class gets duplicated once per reference when rebuilt, not kept shared). This is a real, separate, MLIR-pass-pipeline performance question — nothing to do with equality saturation — not investigated further this session (a plausible future item if larger networks are ever attempted, but not the same class of problem as this entry was originally about).

**The single biggest, free lever, found along the way and worth remembering going forward: debug vs. release build.** Every timing measurement across this *entire* session (this item and everything before it) was taken via `cargo run` — a debug build. A `cargo build --release` binary runs `examples/xor.cleave` in **3.5s total** (vs. ~15-20s debug) and `examples/linear_regression.cleave` in **0.475s** (vs. 2.6s debug) — roughly a 5x difference, zero code changes. Even the MLIR pass pipeline alone drops from 8.9s (debug) to 3.45s (release) — still the dominant cost of `xor.cleave`'s own compile time, but a very different, much less alarming number. **Any future performance investigation on this compiler should measure against a release build first** — a real, easy-to-forget confound this session's own numbers were quietly carrying throughout.

**The commutativity/associativity mechanism investigated conceptually (kept for reference, not acted on)**: discussed directly with the project owner while this item still looked like the active bottleneck — canonicalizing commutative operators (sorting operands by canonical e-class id at construction, eliminating `add_commutative`/`mul_commutative` as ordinary bidirectional rewrites) would remove the doubling, but a concrete, traced example (`a + 10 + b - 10 → a + b`, confirmed via `--dump-cps-equivalences` to genuinely need the sequence `add_commutative → add_sub_assoc → add_commutative → add_sub_assoc → constant-fold → add_zero`) showed this is *not* free: `add_sub_assoc` is declared in only one direction (`sub(add(a,b),c) == add(a,sub(b,c))`, never the mirror `== add(sub(a,c),b)`), relying on commutativity's own free bidirectional swapping to cover the other side. Naive canonicalization would silently lose that specific simplification whenever the canonical order happens to land on the "wrong" side — real, not hypothetical. The fix discussed (declare explicit mirror-image axioms for anything currently relying on commutativity for symmetric coverage) stays fully within the existing declarative `axiom` mechanism (no hardcoding, `MatMul` unaffected), but asking stdlib/user authors to reliably identify which axioms need a mirror is itself a real, easy-to-get-wrong burden — flagged, not resolved. **Not needed right now** given the re-measurement above; revisit if e-graph saturation (not MLIR lowering) becomes the dominant cost again on some future, larger example.

**Done**: `synthesize_derivatives`'s own two `Runner`s (Pass A/Pass B) and `optimize_program`'s own (deliberately kept "identical" to them, per its own doc comment) all lowered from `with_time_limit(30s)` to `with_time_limit(1s)`, matching the measurement above exactly. Verified again after the change, not just trusted from the earlier measurement: full workspace test suite green, and `examples/xor.cleave --run` still trains and predicts correctly (`0.0307`/`0.9665`/`0.9658`/`0.0427`, the expected near-0/1/1/0 XOR pattern).

### An inherent-impl method's own generics aren't picked up by inference at all — done, plus a second, deeper monomorphization gap found and fixed along the way

Found while trying to make `Dense::forward` (`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`) generic over a batch dimension `B` not among `Dense<T,In,Out>`'s own generics (`B` varies per call site, not per `Dense` value — a real, separate, per-method generic was needed). A minimal probe (`impl struct Foo { fn pick<X>(foo, a: X, b: X) -> X { a } }`, called as `f.pick(7, 9)`) failed type-checking outright: `X` never became a fresh instantiable type variable at the call site — the second argument was checked directly against the first's own already-concrete type instead of both unifying against a fresh `X` ("type mismatch: expected `i32`, found `X`"). A top-level generic `fn` (`dynarray_new<T>`, and every algebra-impl method, both already proven) had no such problem — this was specific to an inherent-impl method declaring generics *of its own*, beyond whatever the enclosing `impl<...>` block already binds.

**Root cause, confirmed by direct reading, not guessed**: every real entry point that builds an inherent method's own generics-name-to-fresh-var mapping (`infer_inherent_impl_fn_generic`/`infer_inherent_impl_fn_raw`/`infer_inherent_impl_block`, the declaration-checking paths; `ExprKind::MethodCall`'s own dispatch, the call-site path, `infer.rs`) only ever fed `entry.generics`/`impl_generics` (the *enclosing impl block's* own generics, `impl<T> Foo<T>`) into `fresh_generics_mapping` — a method's own `f.generics`/`entry.method.generics` (`fn pick<X>`) was never included at all. `ty_from_ast_mapped`'s ordinary by-name lookup then failed to find `X` in the mapping and fell through to treating it as a literal, concrete type named `"X"` — hence the "expected i32, found X" diagnostic.

**Fixed, in `infer.rs`**: a new `fresh_generics_mapping_for_method` (concatenates the impl and method generics lists into one combined list before delegating to the existing `fresh_generics_mapping` — same bounds/const-width handling for both, one pass, allows a method's own const generic to reference the impl's own type generic if it ever needs to) is used at the two single-method entry points (`infer_inherent_impl_fn_generic`'s own declaration check, and the `ExprKind::MethodCall` dispatch's own call-site check) — each independent, so re-freshing the whole combined list per call is correct and cheap. `infer_inherent_impl_block` (several methods sharing one `Infer`, real mutual recursion) needed a different shape: the impl-level `impl_mapping` must stay the *same* `T` across every sibling method (mutual recursion depends on this), so only each method's own extra generics are freshly minted per method and *layered on top of* the shared `impl_mapping`, not the whole thing re-derived per method — `quantify_impl_generics` (preventing a genuinely-unresolved generic from being silently defaulted) extended to run over every method's own combined mapping too, not just the block-level one. `infer_inherent_impl_fn_raw`'s own `seed_const_generics` call (binding a const generic's own name into `env` as an ordinary value, for a body that references it, e.g. `[0.0; K]`) extended to also seed `f.generics`, not just `impl_generics`.

**A second, deeper, real bug found immediately after the inference fix, via direct testing — not anticipated going in**: type-checking a call to `Foo::pick(7, 9)` (`Foo` a genuinely *non*-generic struct, `pick` a generic *method*) now succeeded, but MLIR lowering then crashed (`MLIR lowering doesn't support type 't0' yet`) — a real, separate gap one layer down. Root-caused via `--dump-inference-pass`: `monomorphize.rs::build_inherent_templates` and `cps.rs::collect_units`'s own `ItemKind::InherentImpl` branch selection both keyed their "does this impl need per-call-site monomorphization, or can its body just be compiled once and reused" decision purely on `d.generics.is_empty()` — the *impl block's* own genericness — never checking whether any individual *method* inside a non-generic block was itself generic. `Foo::pick<X>` fell into the "compile once" fast path meant for fully-concrete methods, so its body was type-checked exactly once, in isolation, with `X` necessarily left as a bare, permanently-unresolved free variable (nothing at declaration time pins a method-level generic to any concrete type) — that raw, unresolved variable is what then reached MLIR lowering and crashed it.

**Fixed, in both places, with the identical, mirrored condition** (`d.generics.is_empty() && d.fns.iter().all(|f| f.generics.is_empty())` gates the fast "compile once" path; anything else routes through the existing generic/templated machinery): `monomorphize.rs::build_inherent_templates` now builds a per-call-site `InherentTemplate` for every method of an impl block whenever *either* the impl block or any of its own methods has generics (previously: impl block alone); `cps.rs::collect_units`'s two `ItemKind::InherentImpl` match arms select the identical way. The actual instantiation/substitution machinery underneath (`derive_inherent_instantiation`'s own `unify` call, the `inherent_worklist` drain loop) needed *no* changes at all — both already operate generically over "any free `Ty::Var` the template happens to mention," with zero assumption baked in about whether a var came from the impl's own generics or a method's own; only the two branch-selection conditions deciding *whether* to route a method through that machinery in the first place were too narrow.

**A third, real bug found writing the two-call-site regression test, fixed the same way `display_impl_instantiation` already avoids it**: `display_inherent_instantiation` (the specialization's own display/cache-key string) used to be built from *only* the receiver's own substituted type (`param_patterns[0]`) — correct for an impl-level generic (`T` always shows up *inside* the receiver's own type, `Boxed<i32>` vs `Boxed<f64>`, so the receiver alone was already unique), but silently wrong for a method-level generic that never appears in the receiver's type at all: `Foo::pick::<i32>` and `Foo::pick::<f64>`, called from two different call sites in the same program, both substituted the receiver to the identical `Foo` and collided onto the exact same display string — the second call site's specialization was silently treated as "already built," reusing the first's (wrongly-typed) body and corrupting downstream MLIR lowering into a real native crash (an LLVM `isa<To>()` cast assertion, `STATUS_STACK_BUFFER_OVERRUN`). Fixed by building the display string from *every* substituted parameter plus the substituted return type (mirroring `derive_inherent_instantiation`'s own `unify` domain exactly — the same set of types that already determines whether two instantiations are really the same one), not the receiver alone; purely an internal cache-key/diagnostic string, no other code parses its shape, so widening it is a safe, non-breaking change.

**Verified**: three new tests in `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs` — `an_inherent_methods_own_generic_is_a_real_fresh_type_variable` (the original motivating probe, `Foo::pick`, non-generic impl), `an_inherent_methods_own_generic_stays_independent_of_the_impls_own` (the harder case, a method-level generic *inside* an already-generic impl block, `Boxed<T>::combine<X>`, proving `X` and `T` don't collide), and `an_inherent_methods_own_generic_is_specialized_independently_at_two_call_sites` (the display-collision regression, `Foo::pick` called once at `i32` and once at `f64` in the same program) — all pass. Full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` (default-members) clean, zero regressions.

### `DynArray<T>` needs a hand-written `impl RawBuffer<Struct>` per struct element type — done, via a compiler-synthesized marker algebra, not a new bound kind

Raised directly by the project owner, using `DynArray<Point>` for the first time in `examples/convex_hull.cleave`: genuinely hard to justify at the point of use. `stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`'s own `RawBuffer<T>` only shipped impls for the six primitive widths — a struct-typed element (`Point`, or any future one) needed its own 4-line `impl RawBuffer<Point> { extern(dynarray_alloc_ptr) ...; extern(dynarray_grow_ptr) ...; extern(dynarray_get_ptr) ...; extern(dynarray_set_ptr) ...; }`, written by hand in user code, every time — even though the four `extern(...)` symbol names are always these exact same four `_ptr`-suffixed `cleave-rt` functions, verbatim, for every struct type, with zero exceptions (every cleave struct value is already an opaque pointer of identical shape, `mlir_lower.rs::ty_to_mlir`'s own struct fallback), so there was no real per-type information in that impl block at all.

**The real fix, found by re-examining the two options this item's own text originally posed**: not a new *kind* of bound (`impl<S> RawBuffer<S> where S: struct`, needing genuinely new type-system machinery — a structural predicate no existing `Constraint`/dispatch code understands), and not a hand-maintained per-type codegen step either — a **compiler-synthesized, ordinary marker algebra**, the exact same "empty marker impl, real content lives entirely in the fact it exists" shape `stdlib/num/num.cleave`'s own `Int`/`Float` and `stdlib/linalg/tensor.cleave`'s own `NativeShape<T>` already establish by hand, just synthesized mechanically instead of hand-declared per concrete type. This needed **zero** new bound-checking machinery — `T: HeapStruct` reuses `Constraint`/`has_matching_impl`/`bounds_satisfied` completely unchanged, exactly like `T: Float` already does; the only new code is what *generates the impls themselves*.

**`driver::synthesize_heap_struct_marker_impls`** (new, mirrors `synthesize_tuple_structs`'s own "one synthesized declaration per real shape found in the program" precedent exactly, one level up — struct declarations instead of tuple arities, run immediately after it in `compile`'s own chain so a synthesized tuple struct gets marked too): injects one empty `impl HeapStruct<StructName<...>> {}` per plain struct declaration in the merged program, referencing that struct's own declared generics verbatim, in order (`GenericArg::Type(TypeKind::Path(...))` for each — confirmed, by reading `lower.rs::lower_generic_arg`, that a bare generic-name reference in argument position is *already* represented this exact way regardless of whether the generic is a type or a plain const generic, so no Type/Const distinction is even needed when building the synthesized target). `algebra HeapStruct<T> {}` (`stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`) is the new marker; `stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`'s own `impl<S: HeapStruct> RawBuffer<S> { extern(dynarray_alloc_ptr) ...; ... }` (one impl, written once) is the *only* stdlib change needed to make every struct usable with `DynArray` — no per-type impl anywhere, ever again.

**Two real exclusions, both for soundness, not convenience — deliberately narrower than "every struct"**:
- A struct tagged `#[mlir_type(...)]` anywhere in the program (`Tensor`, via `NativeShape<T>`) is skipped — detected by calling `cps::collect_mlir_types` directly (the identical scan `mlir_lower.rs` itself already relies on), not duplicated. Such a struct's real runtime representation is *not* the ordinary heap-allocated opaque pointer every other struct gets, so `RawBuffer<S>`'s pointer-based primitives would silently miscompile if applied to one.
- A struct declaring a pack generic of its own (`const Dims...: i32`) is skipped — referencing a pack by name in a synthesized impl target needs a different `GenericArg` shape (a pack reference, not the ordinary type-path one built here) this function doesn't build. No untagged stdlib struct declares one today (only `Tensor` does, already excluded by the tag check above), so this is a real but currently-inert scope limit, flagged in the function's own doc comment rather than silently mishandled should it ever matter.

**Verified**: `examples/convex_hull.cleave`'s own hand-written `impl RawBuffer<Point> { ... }` block removed entirely (the exact motivating boilerplate), confirmed the example still compiles correctly through the JIT pipeline (its own `--run` crash, found while checking this, is real but confirmed *unrelated* — see the backlog.md entry documenting it as a separate, still-open bug). `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`'s own two pre-existing `DynArray<Point>` tests had their hand-written boilerplate impls removed too, both still passing unchanged; a new test, `dynarrays_of_two_different_struct_types_both_dispatch_correctly_in_one_program`, proves two unrelated struct types (`Point`, `Pair`) each get independently-synthesized markers with no cross-contamination. Three new `cleave/tests/driver.rs` tests exercise `synthesize_heap_struct_marker_impls` directly at the Rust level via `print_program`: an ordinary struct (and a *generic* one, `Pair<A,B>`, proving the generics list threads through correctly) gets its marker; a `#[mlir_type(...)]`-tagged struct doesn't; a pack-generic struct doesn't. Full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` (default-members) clean — 27 `driver` tests (up from 24), 186 `mlir_lower` tests (up from 185), zero regressions.

### `region_analysis.rs`'s escape analysis wrongly scanned a loop's own exit branch, not just its repeating body — a real, silent-compiler-bug-turned-loud-crash, found and fixed

Investigating the `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run` crash flagged in the `DynArray<T>` item above (initially assumed unrelated to that session's own work, then investigated for real at the user's request): `STATUS_STACK_BUFFER_OVERRUN`, a non-unwinding Rust panic aborting the process, stack trace bottoming out in `cleave-rt`'s own `cleave_alloc_local`.

**Root cause, found precisely, not guessed — the panic message itself, initially hidden by only looking at the tail of `--run`'s own output**: capturing the *full* output (not just the crash backtrace) showed the real panic text first: `cleave_alloc_local called with no region open -- a real compiler bug, never a legitimate runtime condition` — `cleave-rt/src/lib.rs::assert_region_open`'s own deliberate, documented safety net (the same doc comment explicitly anticipates this exact failure mode: "`cleave-rt::cleave_alloc_local`'s own `assert_region_open` would catch a wrong classification loudly... if this analysis were ever wrong"). `STATUS_STACK_BUFFER_OVERRUN` here is a Windows quirk, not literal stack corruption — a panic inside an `extern "C"` fn can't unwind, so Rust aborts via a fast-fail path Windows happens to report under that status code.

Confirmed directly, via a temporary `eprintln!` of `find_region_local_functions`'s own output on `examples/convex_hull.cleave`: `dynarray_new<Point>` — called exactly **once**, textually *before* any loop in `main` even starts — was wrongly present in the region-local set. Traced to `region_analysis.rs::analyze_loop_body`, which scanned `loop_def.body` as a whole for calls to mark region-local. `loop_def.body` is *not* just the loop's own repeating iteration body — it's a `CExpr` with three parts (a leading condition-call chain, `then_branch`, `else_branch`), and `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`'s own doc comment is explicit that `else_branch` ("loop exit") "runs in the *outer* scope, ordinary flow" — lowered entirely *outside* the `scf.while` op it builds, with no `cleave_region_enter`/`cleave_region_exit` pair around it at all (those are only ever emitted inside `lower_loop`'s own per-iteration `then_branch` lowering). Since CPS conversion nests "whatever comes after a loop" as that loop's own `else_branch` (part of the *same* structural term, not a separate top-level continuation), any call textually positioned right after an *earlier* loop finishes gets swept into that earlier loop's own `else_branch` scan and misclassified as "runs once per iteration of that loop" — `dynarray_new`'s call, right after the leftmost-point-finding `for` loop and before the main gift-wrapping `loop{}`, hit exactly this. Its own single call site is therefore never wrapped in a region, so the very first construction inside its body (`DynArray(buf:...,len:...,cap:...)`) called `cleave_alloc_local` with no region ever opened — the exact crash.

**Fixed**: a new `loop_then_branch` helper walks `loop_def.body`'s own condition-chain-then-`If` shape (the identical structure `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop` itself parses — zero or more `Fix{[k], App(callee,args)}` layers, terminating in a bare `If`) and returns just `then_branch`; `analyze_loop_body` now runs `collect_escaping`/`collect_calls_and_derivations` on that alone, never on `else_branch`. A shape this helper doesn't recognize returns `None`, conservatively marking nothing region-local for that loop — `lower_loop` itself remains the sole place a genuinely malformed loop panics loudly; this analysis only ever gets *more* conservative by bailing out, never wrong. `find_loops_and_mark`'s own separate, outer walk (finding *nested* loop definitions anywhere, including inside a later `else_branch`) is untouched — a real, later, independent loop reached that way still gets its own correct, independent analysis; only the *specific loop's own* call/escape scan was too broad.

**Verified**: a new regression test, `a_call_after_an_earlier_loop_finishes_is_never_marked_local` (`cleave/tests/region_analysis.rs`) — a function called exactly once, structurally after an earlier `for` loop finishes, must never be marked region-local — confirmed to fail on the pre-fix code with exactly the predicted misclassification (`before_body` wrongly in the set), and to pass after the fix. All 5 `region_analysis` tests green. `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run` now produces the mathematically correct hull (`4` points: the square's own four corners, `(0,0) (3,0) (3,3) (0,3)`) on the specific class of failure this fix addresses — see the backlog.md entry for the *second*, separate, still-open non-deterministic bug this investigation also found along the way, which this fix does not address. Full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` (default-members) clean, zero regressions.

### `is_refcounted` didn't exclude an opaque-handle struct with no real construction site — a second real bug behind the `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run` corruption, found and fixed

Continuing the investigation right above: even after the `region_analysis.rs` fix, `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run` still crashed intermittently (`cleave_release: invalid layout: LayoutError`, `cleave-rt/src/lib.rs:231`) or, worse, silently printed corrupted output — roughly 1 run in 3, varying between otherwise-identical runs of the same binary. Minimized via a sequence of small, real repros (not guessed) down to the smallest possible trigger: `use dynarray; fn main() -> i32 { let h: DynArray<i32> = dynarray_new(4); print(h.len()); 0 }` — no `push`, no struct element type, no loop at all — crashed ~30% of the time on its own.

**Root cause, found precisely**: `refcount::is_refcounted` (`cleave/src/refcount.rs`) decided whether a struct type needs `Retain`/`Release` calls purely from its *type* — any declared, untagged struct name. `RawBuf {}` (`stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`) is exactly such a type — an ordinary, untagged, zero-field struct — but its own doc comment is explicit that it's "never constructed via `RawBuf(...)` anywhere in this module, only ever produced/consumed by the `RawBuffer<T>` impls below": every real `RawBuf` value comes from an `extern fn` (`dynarray_alloc_i32`/`dynarray_alloc_ptr`/...), a plain `realloc`-backed pointer from `cleave-rt`'s own internal allocator, with **no `RcHeader` in front of it at all**. `is_refcounted` didn't know this — a `RawBuf`-typed struct field (`DynArray.buf`) still got ordinary `Retain`/`Release` calls inserted around it, each one reading/writing an `RcHeader` (`refcount`/`data_size`) off whatever garbage bytes happened to sit just before that pointer in memory — real corruption, non-deterministic because the garbage byte pattern immediately preceding a fresh allocation is itself unspecified and varies run to run.

**Fixed, generally, not by hardcoding `RawBuf`'s own name**: a new whole-program scan, `collect_constructed_struct_names`, walks every top-level function's own CPS body (mirrors `region_analysis.rs`'s own established "plain recursive `CExpr` walk, no fixpoint needed" shape) collecting every struct name with at least one real `PrimOp::Struct` construction site anywhere in the compiled program. `is_refcounted` gained a third exclusion: a struct name absent from that set — genuinely never constructed via `cleave_alloc_rc` anywhere, only ever produced by an `extern fn` — is never retained/released, structurally, regardless of field count or name. Every genuinely refcounted struct in this codebase (`DynArray` itself included, via `dynarray_new`'s own real `DynArray(buf:...,len:...,cap:...)` construction) has a real construction site somewhere reachable, so this exclusion only ever fires for the `RawBuf`-shaped "opaque FFI handle" idiom.

**Verified**: two new tests in `cleave/tests/refcount.rs`. `an_opaque_handle_struct_with_no_real_construction_site_is_never_retained_or_released` asserts *structurally* (walking the post-`insert_refcounting` `CpsProgram` directly for any `Retain`/`Release` naming `RawBuf`) rather than relying on a single JIT run — deliberate, since the corruption itself is probabilistic and a single run passing proves nothing; also asserts `DynArray` itself *is* still retained/released, proving the fix isn't over-broad. `dynarray_of_primitives_still_computes_correct_values_after_the_rawbuf_fix` is a real, additional end-to-end JIT check (`60` from three pushed values summed back via `.get()`). Both green; full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` (default-members) clean, 9 `refcount` tests (up from 7). Re-measured `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run`'s own failure rate directly, before/after this fix specifically: 7/30 before, 2/25 after — a real, large reduction, but not zero at that point in the investigation; see the next entry for the third, final bug that closed it out completely.

### An array literal of struct-typed elements got no `Retain` at all — the third and final bug behind `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run`'s own corruption, found by directly reading the generated code, not more bisection

Continuing straight on from the two entries above: even with both prior fixes, `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run` still failed roughly 1 run in 12-15 (`cleave_release: invalid layout: LayoutError`, or silently-corrupt printed output). 18 minimal reproductions (`region_analysis.rs`'s own entry documents the full bisection), tested at 20-50 repetitions each, had narrowed the trigger to a precise but still-large combination — a leading `for` loop, feeding into a second loop pushing an array-aliased struct while running a nested `for` loop, followed by a separate read-back loop — without finding the actual root cause. Further shrinking the repro kept hitting diminishing returns.

**The user's own suggestion — read the generated code directly instead of bisecting further — found it immediately.** `--dump-cps-optimized` (`main.rs`, already existing — runs the real pipeline through `insert_refcounting` and prints the result, `cleave/src/main.rs:400-408`) on a 3-point version of the minimal repro showed the smoking gun in `main`'s own very first few lines:

```
(let-prim v555: Point = (struct.Point[x,y] 0 0)
(let-prim v556: Point = (struct.Point[x,y] 3 0)
(let-prim v557: Point = (struct.Point[x,y] 0 3)
(let-prim v558: [Point; 3] = (array v555 v556 v557)
(let-prim v626: () = (release v555)
(let-prim v625: () = (release v556)
(let-prim v624: () = (release v557)
```

`points: [Point; 3] = [Point(...), Point(...), Point(...)]` — the array literal *embedding* three freshly-constructed `Point`s — released all three **immediately after** building the array containing them, with **zero `Retain` calls anywhere in the whole 426-line dump**. Every one of the dozens of later `points[i]` reads, for the entire rest of `main`, read back through an already-released (and, once the arena/heap reused that memory for something else, genuinely dangling) pointer — real, silent corruption, non-deterministic purely because *what* garbage happens to occupy that freed memory by the time it's next read varies run to run.

**Root cause, in `refcount::rewrite_body`'s own `retain_targets` match**: `PrimOp::FieldStore`/`PrimOp::Store` (an existing value written into existing storage) and `PrimOp::Struct` (a struct literal's own field values) both correctly retain every aliased argument before the enclosing scope's own release-tracking can reclaim them — the module's own doc comment is explicit about `Struct` needing exactly this ("`Line(a: p1, b: p2)` embeds `p1`'s/`p2`'s own pointers directly, found by direct testing"). `PrimOp::Array` (`ExprKind::ArrayLit`, `[e1, e2, ...]`) is *structurally the identical shape* — it also embeds every argument's own pointer directly — but was simply never added to that match arm at all, falling through to the `_ => Vec::new()` catch-all. No prior example ever built an array literal of *refcounted struct* elements (every earlier array-of-struct case went through `DynArray`'s own `push`, one element at a time, or was a primitive-element array), so this gap had never been exercised until `examples/convex_hull.cleave`'s own `points: [Point; 8]`.

**Fixed**: `PrimOp::Array` added alongside `PrimOp::Struct` in `retain_targets`'s match — one line. `PrimOp::ArrayRepeat` (`[v; N]`) is a plausible, structurally similar risk, *not* confirmed by an actual failing case the way `Array` was — deliberately left untouched rather than guessed at; flagged for a future look if it's ever actually hit.

**Verified**: two new tests in `cleave/tests/refcount.rs`. `every_element_of_a_struct_array_literal_is_retained` asserts structurally (≥3 `Retain(Point)` calls for a 3-element array literal) — the same "don't rely on a single probabilistic JIT run" posture the `RawBuf` fix's own test already established. `a_struct_array_literals_elements_read_back_correctly_after_other_work` is a real, additional end-to-end JIT check (sums three `Point` fields read back through array indexing inside a `for` loop, `21.0`). Both green; 11 `refcount` tests total (up from 9).

**The real, final result, measured directly, not assumed**: `examples/convex_hull.cleave --run`, 50 consecutive runs, 0 failures — the exact minimal repro that used to fail 1 in ~13, also 40/40 clean. Every other `examples/*.cleave` re-swept (axiom_demo, complex, convert, derivative_demo, fibonacci, linear_regression, mandelbrot, matmul, neuron_demo, tensor_demo, vector, xor, xor_tensor — 3 runs each) and `rust-interop-demo` — all clean, unaffected by any of the three fixes in this whole investigation. Full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` (default-members) green throughout. `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` (excluded from `default-members`, real multi-minute training runs, and don't use `DynArray`/array-literals-of-structs at all) weren't re-run end to end this session — flagged as worth a spot-check, not expected to be affected.

### A pack-generic struct's own field, typed through *another* pack-generic type (`m: Tensor<T, Dims...>` on `AdamState<T, Dims...>`), panicked on field access

Found building `stdlib/optim/optim.cleave`'s new `AdamState<T, Dims...> { m: Tensor<T, Dims...>, v: Tensor<T, Dims...>, ... }` — the first struct in the codebase whose own pack generic (`Dims...`) is used, in a field's declared type, *nested inside* another generic type's own argument list, rather than either of the two shapes every earlier pack-generic struct used: a bare array size (`Tensor<T, Dims...>`'s own `data: [T; Dims...]`) or the pack as a field's entire type by itself (`print`'s own `Args...`). `state.m` (`state: AdamState<T, Dims...>`) panicked: `"type inference: pack reference \`Dims...\` has no matching declared generic in scope"`.

**Root cause, read directly in `infer.rs::resolve_field_access`/`ty_from_ast_mapped_with_pack`**: a pack-generic struct's field-access path (`Ty::App(struct_name, type_args)`, the `struct_generics.last().is_some_and(is_variadic)` branch) correctly splits `type_args` into `non_pack`/`pack_tys` and calls `ty_from_ast_mapped_with_pack` — but that method only ever special-cased two shapes (a field typed as a bare array with the pack as its size, or a field typed as the bare pack itself); anything else (the `_` arm) fell through to plain `ty_from_ast_mapped`, called with only `mapping` (the struct's *non-pack* generics, `T`) and no `pack_tys` at all — the moment plain `ty_from_ast_mapped` recursed into the field type's own nested `Dims...` reference (`m`'s declared type, `Tensor<T, Dims...>`), `Dims` genuinely wasn't in `mapping`, and panicked.

**Fixed**: a new `TypeKind::Path` arm in `ty_from_ast_mapped_with_pack`, mirroring `ty_from_ast_mapped`'s own `Path` arm positionally, except a generic argument that's exactly the pack reference (`Dims...`) splices *every* element of `pack_tys` into that argument-list position (one struct type-argument slot expanding into several `Ty::App` slots — `Tensor<T,Dims...>`'s own concrete dimensions laid out flat, the same shape a direct `Tensor<f32,2,2>` reference already has), rather than collapsing to a single tuple type the way the bare-`PackRef`-as-whole-field-type arm does; every other argument recurses through this same pack-aware method, so deeper nesting resolves correctly too.

**Verified**: `examples/xor.cleave --run` (the real end-to-end trigger, `Optimizer<Opt,Model,State>`'s own `Dense`-level composition reading `state.m`-shaped fields through this exact path) and a battery of minimal standalone probes (`Tensor<T,Dims...>`-typed fields on a fresh pack-generic struct, read back at multiple ranks) — all correct, no regressions in the existing pack-generics test suite (`cleave/tests/infer.rs`, `cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`).

### `Optimizer<Opt, Model, State>`: a composing algebra impl generic over `Opt` can now dispatch a nested call to a *different*, still-generic impl, even with several leaf `Opt` impls and several levels of struct nesting — a real, previously-deep compiler limitation, fixed for good

`Optimizer<Opt, Model, State>` itself (`stdlib/optim/optim.cleave`) is the real answer to "l'API de l'optimiseur est zarbi... pas de support de plusieurs simplement (adam, adamw etc.)" — `State` as its own independent target, not `Model` reused, the same heterogeneous-algebra shape `MatMul<A,B,C>` already established, so `Adam` (needing two accumulators per tensor) can exist at all. The design itself was never the problem — *composing* it was: any impl generic over `Opt` and forwarding into a nested `Optimizer::init_state`/`step` call on a sub-field (`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s own `DenseState<StateW,StateB>`-level impl, a user's own `Network`-level one, ...) used to fail — sometimes a clean compile error, sometimes a raw MLIR-lowering panic on a leftover free type variable, once even a *silently wrong* answer (a bogus `-> Sgd`/`-> f32` return type on an otherwise-correct call) — the moment **more than one leaf `Opt` impl existed** (`Sgd` and `Momentum` both declared, say). With only one leaf impl, or with `State = Model` (no independent third target), the identical composing shape always worked, which is exactly why this never surfaced until this design existed.

**Root cause, in three real, separate, precisely-diagnosed layers — reading the actual code, not guessing at any of them:**

1. **A still-generic impl's own body can't structurally commit a nested dispatch when more than one leaf candidate exists.** A composing impl's own body (`WrapState(a: Optimizer::init_state(opt, model.a), ...)`) is checked once, generically (`infer_impl_fn_generic_with_env`, `self.active_generics` non-empty). Committing a nested dispatch mid-declaration is only judged sound by `infer.rs::unambiguous_and_preserves_openness` when *exactly one* impl of the algebra structurally matches — with `Sgd` *and* `Momentum` both targeting `Tensor<T,Dims...>`, and `Opt` still unconstrained at this point, both match, genuinely ambiguous from this declaration's own point of view. Dispatch correctly defers — but the deferred constraint's eventual resolution was never propagated back into `monomorphize.rs`'s own `ImplTemplate`, a *frozen* snapshot of that one generic-body-check, only ever *substituted* (never re-inferred) at each real, concrete specialization. The nested call's return type stayed a genuinely disconnected fresh variable forever.

2. **Even once re-inferred concretely, one composing method can't learn what a *sibling* method (same `impl` block) already resolved.** `step`'s own signature has `State` as a *parameter*, not just the return — gating, unlike `init_state`'s genuinely output-only `State` — so `step`'s own concrete `State` isn't derivable from its own arguments at all; it can only come from `init_state`'s own resolution of the identical `(Opt, Model)` pair. A first attempt at a cross-specialization table keyed by raw `TyVar` failed outright: `monomorphize.rs::build_impl_templates` gives *every method of the same `impl` block its own fresh `Infer::new(registry)` instance*, each numbering its own `TyVar`s from `0` independently — `StateA`/`StateB` in `init_state`'s own captured template and in `step`'s own are semantically the same declared generics but literally different, unrelated ids.

3. **`TyVar` numbers colliding across independent `Infer` sessions can silently alias unrelated bindings, not just fail to match.** Fixing (2) properly required a fresh re-inference session, in `monomorphize.rs`'s own `impl_worklist` drain loop, to be able to consult *and* contribute to a shared cache — and running two independently-numbered `Infer` sessions' own outputs through `unify` against each other is exactly how a real, silently-wrong answer got produced once (confirmed directly, then fixed) before this was understood.

**The real fix, three matching pieces:**

- **`Infer::new_with_vars`/`current_vars`** (`infer.rs`): an `Infer` session can now be seeded with an existing `TyVarGen` instead of always starting at `0`, and hand a live snapshot back out. `monomorphize.rs` threads one `shared_vars: TyVarGen` through *every* `Infer` session `build_impl_templates` and the `impl_worklist` loop's own re-inference fallback mint, one after another — the whole reason `TyVar` numbers can never again collide across these specific sessions.
- **`Infer::infer_impl_fn_with_concrete_targets`** (`infer.rs`): mirrors the existing `fn_worklist`/`detect_duck_typed_fns`/`infer_fn_with_concrete_params` fallback for ordinary generic functions — a real, fresh re-inference of a composing impl method's body once its own `target_tys`/`param_types` are as concrete as `monomorphize.rs` can make them, refreshing any still-open leaf to a fresh, session-local variable rather than threading a stale one through literally. `monomorphize.rs`'s own `impl_worklist` loop invokes it whenever ordinary substitution leaves a free `Ty::Var` anywhere in `param_types`/`result`/`node_types`.
- **`resolved_target_sigs: HashMap<(String, Vec<String>), Vec<Ty>>`** (`monomorphize.rs`), keyed *structurally* — `(algebra name, every target position's own stringified type, "?" for whichever are still open)` — not by `TyVar`, closing gap (2) for real: `step` finds what `init_state` already learned for the identical concrete `(Opt, Model)` purely by matching shape, no shared variable identity required. Grown every time a specialization's `target_tys` end up fully concrete (gated on full concreteness before ever unifying against a frozen template's own patterns — the fix for gap (3)). A bounded retry loop (`deferred_impl`, retried once per outer fixed-point pass, given up on only once a whole pass adds nothing new to the table) lets sibling resolutions interleave in either order. `collect_instantiations` deliberately still runs even on an incomplete specialization (discovering a nested, independently-resolvable template — `Wrap` inside `Net` — is what lets the outer one's own retry ever have something new to find) — found necessary directly: skipping it starves a genuine two-level composition permanently, the inner template never gets queued at all.
- **`Infer::external_state_hint`/`apply_external_state_hint`** (`infer.rs`): the piece that actually closes the loop. `monomorphize.rs`'s own re-inference session is opted in (`with_external_state_hint(&resolved_target_sigs)`) so that the moment `infer_algebra_call`'s dispatch commits for real inside a composing body, it also unifies the just-matched candidate's own still-open leaves against `resolved_target_sigs`'s own answer if one exists — closing what ordinary dispatch alone structurally cannot (dispatch only ever proves the *shape*, `WrapState<'a,'b>`, never the concrete contents). Safe regardless of which session a `TyVar` originated from: the hint's own value is always fully concrete by construction, and unifying anything against an already-concrete value can never alias two unrelated variables to each other.
- **`monomorphize.rs::patch_ordinary_fn_node_types`**: the one remaining piece, found by testing `main()` itself calling `Optimizer::step` more than once with `state` threaded through (`let r = ...step(...); let r2 = ...step(..., r.1, ...);`, or a `for` loop doing the identical thing — `examples/xor.cleave`'s own real training loop). An *ordinary*, non-generic top-level `fn` is never re-inferred by anything above (its own body is assumed already fully concrete, true for every ordinary program but this one shape) — `program_inference.node_types` was built once, by an already-finished, separate whole-program pass with no way to know what `resolved_target_sigs` learned afterward. Patches it in place, at the very end, a few rounds (a chained call needs the first patched before the second's own arg types become concrete enough) — safe for the identical "unify only ever binds an open var to something already concrete" reason.

**Two more, smaller, genuinely separate bugs found and fixed along the way**, both in code paths this work newly exercised for the first time:

- **`resolve_struct_field_ty_with_pack`** (`mlir_lower.rs`) — the MLIR-codegen-side counterpart of the pack-generic-field-access fix above, with the identical gap: `AdamState<T, Dims...>`'s own field `m: Tensor<T, Dims...>` (a pack reference nested inside *another* generic type's own argument list, as opposed to a bare array-size slot or a field's whole type) panicked lowering the struct's own layout — `"unresolved pack reference \`Dims...\` reached a struct field's own type"`. Fixed with the identical technique: a new `TypeKind::Path` arm splicing `pack_tys` into the matching argument-list slot, recursing pack-aware for deeper nesting.
- **Constructing a pack-generic struct via a turbofish still needs one even when field values alone look pack-shaped** (`AdamState::<T, Dims...>(m: ..., v: ..., ...)`, `stdlib/optim/optim.cleave`) — `infer.rs::infer_struct_lit_with_pack`'s own existing, already-documented restriction (inferring a pack's own arity from field values isn't supported yet); `optim.cleave`'s own `Adam` impl just hadn't hit it before this session.
