**Verified end to end, not just "compiles"**: standalone probes at increasing depth (a single leaf, `Sgd`+`Momentum` composed one level through a `Wrap`-shaped struct, two levels through a `Net`-of-`Wrap`-of-`Tensor` shape, loop-carried state across five iterations matching `examples/xor.cleave`'s own training-loop shape exactly) — all producing hand-verified-correct numeric output, `Momentum`'s own real velocity accumulation included. `examples/xor.cleave` itself, now using the full `Optimizer<Opt,Model,State>` design with real `DenseState`/`NetworkState` composition through two full levels of nesting inside a real 4000-step training loop, produces **byte-identical** output to its own pre-this-session, simpler `State = Model` version. `Adam` trains the same real network (compiles, runs, moves the loss) — convergence quality with the particular hyperparameters tried is a separate, ordinary ML question, not a compiler-correctness one; a real, separate, narrower bug around `Adam` specifically and literally-constructed (not `Init::xavier()`-built) tensors is documented in `doc/backlog.md`, not blocking here. Two pre-existing tests (`cleave/tests/mlir_lower.rs`, `optimizer_composes_correctly_one_level_up_through_dense`/`optimizer_momentum_trains_a_real_network_to_convergence`) needed updating to the new `Optimizer<Opt,Model,State>` shape (they predated it); both green now, the latter a real, independent, full 4000-step convergence check. Full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` green throughout, a full `examples/*.cleave --run` sweep clean.

**A fourth bug, found only after all of the above — a real, non-deterministic correctness bug, not a missing-resolution one, and the reason this whole fix took a second pass.** The composition above initially shipped with a rare (roughly 1 run in 4–6, `HashMap`-iteration-order-dependent — the same source producing different outcomes on different runs, nothing about the program itself changing) failure: `Optimizer::step` on a `Network`-shaped model would sometimes fail to specialize at all, and — worse, caught directly before it could ship — could sometimes *silently* produce a wrong answer instead (`NetworkState<S1,S2>`'s own `S2` ending up structurally equal to `S1`, one layer's own optimizer state literally aliased to a different, unrelated layer's).

Root-caused precisely, with direct evidence (temporary `eprintln!` tracing through `derive_impl_instantiation`, not guessed): `shared_vars` (this entry's own earlier fix) made every `TyVar` `build_impl_templates` and the `impl_worklist` fallback mint mutually unique — but `callgraph::infer_program`, the separate, already-finished whole-program pass whose own `node_types` map is what real call sites (`main`'s own `Optimizer::step(...)` call) get their query types from, was never part of that shared counter at all. Both sessions independently started counting from `0` — so a template's own internal `TyVar` (e.g. `104`, minted deep inside `NetworkState<StateL1,StateL2>`'s own `StateL2` position while building the `step` template) could, purely by coincidence, share a raw number with `program_inference`'s own, completely unrelated `TyVar` for `state`'s own type at the call site. `unify` has no way to tell these apart — it silently treated them as the same variable, aliasing `StateL2`'s own field to whatever `StateL1` had already resolved to.

Fixed by extending the *existing* mechanism this codebase already used for the identical problem *within* `callgraph::infer_program` itself (`Infer::with_var_counter_starting_at`/`high_water_mark`, chaining `TyVar` numbering across mutually-recursive SCC groups, `next_var_id` in `callgraph.rs`) one step further: `ProgramInference` gained a new `next_var_id` field (`callgraph.rs`, the pass's own final counter value), and `monomorphize.rs`'s own `shared_vars` is now seeded from *that* (`TyVarGen::starting_at(program_inference.next_var_id)`) instead of `0` — the same chaining principle, one more link. No new mechanism, no scanning `node_types` for a maximum by hand (an earlier, rejected version of this fix did exactly that, redundant with the counter the pass already tracks internally).

### `cleave_release`'s own field-cascade — one stale backlog entry corrected, one real, different, deeper bug found investigating it and fixed

Raised directly by the user, asking to revisit `doc/backlog.md`'s own "`cleave_release` is non-cascading for ordinary (non-region-local) struct fields" entry.

**Correction first**: that entry's own original claim — that `cleave_release` never recurses into a struct's own nested struct-typed fields at all — was already wrong by the time it was written. `mlir_lower.rs::lower_release_cascade` (added the *same* commit, `c1b781f "fix mnist memory leak"`, that added the stale entry) already does exactly this: reads every field's own pointer before releasing anything, releases the container, and — only if that release's own returned `i1` confirms the refcount genuinely reached zero (gated in a real `scf.if`, load-bearing per that function's own doc comment: an unconditional cascade was tried first and caused a real `STATUS_HEAP_CORRUPTION` on `examples/digits-interop`, freeing a still-live second reference) — recurses into every tensor-typed and nested-struct-typed field, at arbitrary depth. `Network` containing `Dense` containing a `Tensor` is exactly the shape this already, correctly, fully releases. The entry was simply never moved here at the time.

**The real, different bug, found investigating whether the stale entry hid something real** — a genuine, code-inspection-confirmed corruption risk, unrelated to nesting *depth* at all: `lower_release_cascade`'s own "is this an ordinary nested struct field" check was `ctx.struct_schemas.contains_key(name)` alone — *any* declared struct type, with no check for whether that type is actually `cleave_alloc_rc`'d anywhere. `refcount::is_refcounted` already has the real check this needed (`struct_schemas.contains_key(name) && not tensor/vector-tagged && constructed.contains(name)`) — added once already, for the *original*, `is_refcounted`-level version of this exact class of bug (`stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`'s own `RawBuf {}`, an ordinary, untagged, zero-field struct declaration, *never* constructed via `RawBuf(...)` anywhere, only ever produced by an `extern fn` as a plain, non-headered pointer) — but that fix only ever reached `insert_refcounting`'s own *top-level* `Retain`/`Release` insertion, never `lower_release_cascade`'s own separate, cruder, MLIR-lowering-time field-walk. `DynArray<T> { buf: RawBuf, len: i32, cap: i32 }` is exactly the shape that exposes it: releasing *any* struct that embeds a `DynArray` (a real, ordinary pattern) cascades into `buf`, generating a real `cleave_release` call against `dynarray_alloc_*`'s own raw returned pointer — the identical corruption class `is_refcounted`'s own doc comment already documents as genuinely non-deterministic (roughly a third of the time a visible panic, the rest silent).

**Fixed**: `LowerCtx` gained a new field, `constructed_structs` (`refcount::collect_constructed_struct_names`, made `pub(crate)` alongside `is_refcounted` itself, computed once in `lower_program` the same way `region_local_fns` already is) — `lower_release_cascade`'s own field-walk now calls `refcount::is_refcounted` directly (the exact same check, not a re-derived parallel one) instead of the old bare `contains_key`. Confirmed directly, not just reasoned: built a minimal probe (`Wrapper { arr: DynArray<i32> }`, released inside a function that constructs but never returns it), dumped the real, fully-refcounted MLIR (`pipeline.rs`'s own real ordering, `insert_refcounting` then `lower_program` — neither `--dump-mlir` nor `--dump-mlir-lowered` run `insert_refcounting` at all, a separate, minor CLI-debugging gap noted in passing) — the buggy version generates a real `cleave_release` call targeting `buf`'s own raw pointer, nested inside `d`'s own release cascade; the fixed version omits it entirely, releasing only the genuinely `cleave_alloc_rc`'d/`cleave_alloc_local`'d containers. New regression test (`releasing_a_struct_that_embeds_a_dynarray_never_cascades_into_its_own_rawbuf_field`, `cleave/tests/refcount.rs`) asserts the real, fixed `cleave_release` call count structurally (execution alone can't reliably catch this — same non-determinism as the original bug) — confirmed to fail against the pre-fix code (temporarily reverted, re-tested, re-restored) and pass against the fix. Full workspace `cargo test` green throughout (12/12 in `refcount.rs`, including this new one); `mnist-interop`/`digits-interop` re-verified end to end, `test accuracy: 0.9342`/`0.94713414` unchanged (neither kernel happens to embed a `DynArray`, so this fix changes nothing observable for them — the exposure is real for any future struct that does, `DynArray`-based collections included).

**Verified the fix actually closes it, not just "seems to help"**: the exact non-deterministic failure was reproduced in isolation first (a small standalone `.cleave` file matching the failing test's own shape, run in a loop — ~9/15 failures before the fix), confirmed fixed (30/30 clean after), then the full `mlir_lower` test suite run 8 times consecutively single-threaded (`--test-threads=1`, ruling out a thread-scheduling explanation entirely) — 8/8 fully green, 189/189 tests every time. Full `cargo test --release --no-fail-fast` and `cargo build --workspace --release` (including `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop`, each needing their own `Network`/`NetworkState` update to the new shape too) both clean.


## Real profiler ground truth on the `~3.5×`-vs-PyTorch gap: OpenMP overhead triple-confirmed on *both* sides, then isolated away to find PyTorch still wins ~6.6× on raw single-thread codegen — two concrete, previously-undiscovered root causes found by disassembling the real hot addresses — both eventually resolved, see below and the following entry

Picked up directly off the accumulator-widening dead-end above, on the user's own explicit redirection: back to fundamentals — this machine's real AVX-512 peak (`2 FMA ports × 2 flops/FMA × 16 lanes × 4.5GHz × 8 cores ≈ 2.3 TFlop/s`, confirmed by the user's own back-of-envelope math) against the real FLOP count of one training batch (`~83M FLOPs` forward+backward, `2×M×K×N` per layer, PyTorch's own `dX` correctly skipped for the input layer — confirmed directly via a full-batch `MKL_VERBOSE=1` trace, no `N,T` call with `M=784` anywhere). Both cleave (**~3.6 GFlop/s, 0.16% of peak**) and PyTorch (**~14 GFlop/s, 0.6% of peak**) are nowhere near the compute roofline — by two orders of magnitude — which by itself already ruled out the whole accumulator-latency investigation above as the dominant explanation for the gap, regardless of its own real merits.

**OpenMP fork/join overhead, independently confirmed by three separate tools/methods, not just VTune again:**
- VTune `hotspots` on the real `mnist-interop.exe`: **86.3% of all CPU time in `_kmpc_barrier`+libomp's own spin loop**, only 12.2% in `train_and_evaluate` itself.
- The identical measurement on **PyTorch itself** (`vtune -collect hotspots` on `mnist_bench.py`, 200 epochs capped to a clean 15s window): **87.5% in `libiomp5md.dll`** (Intel's own OpenMP runtime, bundled with this PyTorch wheel, `torch/lib/libiomp5md.dll` — confirmed a genuinely different binary from cleave's LLVM `libomp.dll`, not just a hypothesis) — refuting the natural "Intel's own runtime must be more efficient" hypothesis directly: both runtimes waste essentially the same ~87% on barrier/spin for this workload's shape (tiny matmuls, one fork per BLAS call either way — real, confirmed by summing a steady-state `MKL_VERBOSE` batch: **PyTorch does ~150µs of real SGEMM compute per batch against a wall-clock of ~420µs**, the same ~65% waste ratio).
- **AMD uProf** (`AMDuProfCLI.exe collect --config tbp|hotspots [-g]`, this machine's *own* silicon vendor's profiler, real hardware PMU sampling — VTune had explicitly warned it was running in degraded software-only mode on this non-Intel CPU) gives a third, independent measurement on the real kernel: **`libomp.dll` 56.9% + `ntdll.dll` (kernel-level thread wait/wake) 15.5% = 72.4%** of all CPU time, `train_and_evaluate` itself only 26.0% — same story, different tool, different sampling mechanism entirely.
- A real, if inert, confound found and precisely characterized along the way: this machine reports `Virtual Machine: Yes, Hypervisor Vendor: Microsoft Hyper-V` to both profilers despite being genuine bare metal with no WSL2 — confirmed via `Get-CimInstance Win32_DeviceGuard`: `VirtualizationBasedSecurityStatus: 2`, `SecurityServicesRunning: {2}` (HVCI/"Memory Integrity" actively running) — a real Windows-11-default security feature that runs the whole OS as a Hyper-V guest partition even with no VM ever explicitly created. User's own call, not chased further: PyTorch runs under the identical HVCI either way, so it can't be the differentiator for the *relative* gap.
- **AMDuProfCLI's own CLI usability note, for next time**: `-h`/`--help` invoke an interactive pager (`Press any key...`) that hangs indefinitely against a non-interactive stdin, even with `< /dev/null` (silently swallows the *first* "page" without printing it) — real flags/configs found instead via `AMDuProfCLI.exe info --list collect-configs` (enumerates `tbp`/`hotspots`/`cpi`/`branch`/... directly) and the public AMD uProf user guide, not the CLI's own `-h` output. Also real and load-bearing: `collect --config hotspots` alone does *not* enable call-stack sampling (`Call Stack Sampling: False` in the resulting report) — needs the separate `-g` flag too.

**Isolating OpenMP away entirely — `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` on the real kernel — to find PyTorch still wins by ~6.6×, a real, separate, previously-unattributed gap.** With one thread, a fork/join still happens per region but there is no barrier wait (nothing to synchronize with) — confirmed directly: `libomp.dll` drops to **0.92s out of ~84s of total CPU-time (~1%)**, `train_and_evaluate` itself now **74.633s across 10 epochs = 7.46s/epoch**, essentially the *entire* budget. The equivalent PyTorch single-thread number (`OMP_NUM_THREADS=1 MKL_NUM_THREADS=1`, scaled linearly from a `1,000`-batch subset to the real `1,875`/epoch): **~1.13s/epoch of real GEMM-only compute**. **`7.46s` vs `1.13s` — cleave's own generated code is ~6.6× slower than MKL's, per unit of real, already-serial, non-parallel work, for the exact same FLOPs.** This reframes the whole gap: closing the OpenMP-overhead problem (a real, ~5.7× lever on its own — `train_and_evaluate`'s own 8-thread CPU-time-across-threads, `~10.36s`/epoch, divided by 8 real cores if perfectly parallelized ≈ `1.3s`/epoch, against today's real `7.4s`/epoch 8-thread wall-clock) would *still* leave a real, separate `~1.6×` gap against PyTorch's own 8-thread `0.79s`/epoch — both problems are real and distinct, neither alone closes the gap.

**Where those single-thread cycles actually go — found by direct disassembly of the real sampled hot addresses (`llvm-objdump` + AMD uProf's own DuckDB-format `cpu.db`, queried directly with Python's `duckdb` package for exact per-instruction sample weights), not inferred:**
- **36.7%** — the already-known, already-chased accumulator-latency chain (`vfmadd132ps` rotating through `zmm0`-`zmm3`, one dependency each) — real, but not the majority contributor, and both of this session's own two attempted MLIR-level fixes (`split_reduction`, `structured.split`) are confirmed dead ends (see above) — needs real new C++ or an `mlir_lower.rs`-level rewrite to progress further, not attempted.
- **21.1%, a real, previously-undiscovered finding**: a full, 32-register `vmovups %zmm0..%zmm31, ...` spill sequence (2KB of pure register-file save, zero useful arithmetic) sits immediately before a matmul loop whose own row stride is `0xc40` = `3136` bytes = **`784` floats × `4`** — almost certainly `l1`'s own `784 -> 512` matmul (the single largest-FLOP layer). A real register-pressure/over-unrolling artifact from whatever tile size that layer's own instantiation of the schedule picks, not a fundamental limit — a smaller tile size or explicit unroll factor for this specific layer is the natural next thing to try.
- **11%, a second real, previously-undiscovered finding**: the `128 -> 10` output layer's own matmul (`l4`, already known from earlier this session to be non-divisible and fall through to Stage 2's un-fused path) doesn't just fail to *fuse* — it falls all the way through to a **fully scalar** loop (`vmovss`/`vfmadd132ss`/`vfmadd213ss`, one float at a time, inner trip count `cmpq $0xa` = exactly `10`), not even packed-SIMD, let alone vectorized across the `10`-wide output. `N=10 < 16` (one AVX-512 lane count) is presumably why `create_named_contraction`+`outerproduct` never applies here at all — a real coverage gap in the schedule for any dimension narrower than one vector register, silently and expensively falling all the way back to the oldest, slowest lowering path rather than e.g. a masked/padded vectorization. Disproportionately expensive for the network's own smallest layer — a real, concrete, likely-tractable target (pad to 16 with a real/zero mask, or a dedicated narrow-N vectorization strategy in the schedule).
- The remaining `~17%` (AVX-512 mask-register setup, `kxnorb`/`vpsllq`/`vxorps`, and plain scalar bounds-check control flow) is very likely edge/remainder-handling satellite cost of these same three loops, not a fourth distinct root cause — not separately investigated.

**The `l4` scalar-fallback finding — fixed, landed.** `transform.structured.pad` (`LinalgTransformOps.td`, confirmed real via an isolated probe before touching the schedule) pads whichever dimension isn't already a multiple of `16` with a real, zero-filled `tensor.pad`, copying the unpadded region back out afterward (`copy_back_op = "linalg.copy"`) — letting the exact same `create_named_contraction`+outerproduct path already used everywhere else apply to the `128 -> 10` shape too. Confirmed directly, on an isolated probe, to produce genuine packed `vfmadd*ps` in place of the scalar fallback.

**A real, serious integration bug found applying this as a blanket fix to *every* matmul (the first attempt), not just the ones that actually need it** — padding an *already*-divisible matmul (`l1`'s own forward matmul, `784 -> 512`, both dims already multiples of `16`) still perturbs its own IR shape enough that `vectorize` silently falls through to `--convert-vector-to-scf`'s own generic masked-transfer lowering instead of outerproduct, allocating a real scratch buffer (`memref.alloca`, confirmed directly in the real lowered `llvm` dialect IR) once per iteration of the K-reduction loop (**784 times**, for this layer) — *inside* the single `stacksave`/`stackrestore` pair `--convert-scf-to-openmp` wraps around the whole per-thread loop body, not once per K-iteration. Real, measured consequence: a genuine stack overflow, crashing partway through `evaluate()`'s own repeated forward passes. Three isolated repros (a bare loop over the `128 -> 10` shape at 80,000 iterations; a full 4-layer forward pass at 10,000 iterations, matching `evaluate()`'s own shape exactly; a full training loop — `grad()`/SGD/backward — at 3,000 batches, exceeding the real `1,875`/epoch) all failed to reproduce the crash in isolation — it only manifests on the real kernel with real `extern fn` data loading, never root-caused beyond the mechanism above.

**The real fix**: never pad unconditionally. `structured.pad` only ever runs as a *retry*, on whatever `linalg.matmul` a stage's own direct `vectorize {create_named_contraction}` call still leaves standing (a real, silenceable failure, not a crash — confirmed already-established elsewhere in this file). A genuine wrinkle found getting this right: `vectorize` consumes its own operand handle whether it succeeds or fails, so re-matching `linalg.matmul` against that *same*, now-invalidated handle hard-errors ("uses a handle invalidated by a previously executed transform op") — the retry re-matches from the still-valid *ancestor* handle instead (the outer `tile_using_for`'s own loop handle, untouched by `vectorize`'s own consumption). Landed in all three places a matmul gets tiled+vectorized in this schedule: `@tile_and_vectorize` (Stage 2), Stage 1's own matmul section (`@fuse_and_vectorize` — needed here too, since `l4`'s own forward matmul in the *training*-loop context, output feeding `pred - y`, routes through Stage 1 instead of Stage 2), and each of Stage 0's four `failures(suppress)` blocks (needed for `%f6`, `dh3`, `l4->l3`'s own backward gradient, `K=10`). A first, broader design (a separate, brand-new "Stage 3" `foreach_match` pass matching plain `linalg.matmul` again after Stage 2) does *not* work and was caught before landing: by the time Stage 2's own tiling has run, a leftover unvectorized matmul is nested inside the `scf.forall`/`scf.for`/`scf.for` that tiling just built, so its parent is `scf.for`, not `func.func`/`scf.while` — `@match_matmul`'s own existing matcher never finds it again from the top.

**Verified**: real kernel, 10 epochs + `evaluate()`, no crash, `test accuracy: 0.9342` (unchanged, correctness never in question). Real compiled object disassembled directly (`llvm-objdump`) before and after: the dominant, hot-path scalar `vfmadd132ss`/`vfmadd213ss` occurrences are gone from both the training-loop and `evaluate()`-loop call sites (the handful still present are cold, one-time weight-init code — `Init::he`/`Init::xavier`'s own scalar formula — confirmed by their call-site context, indirect calls to `rand_normal_f32`/init helpers immediately before, not a loop). `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` single-thread timing (isolating the fix from any OpenMP-overhead noise, matching this entry's own earlier methodology): ~122.6s/10 epochs, down from ~131.8s-138.3s pre-fix — a real, if modest (consistent with the ~11% share of single-thread "effective" time this bucket was ever measured to occupy), improvement in the expected direction. Full `cargo test --workspace --release` clean throughout (189 `mlir_lower` tests + everything else).

**The `l1` register-spill finding — investigated, real dead end found, reverted, not landed.** The obvious first fix (a narrower `K` tile for the matmul's own `tile_using_for`, `16 -> 8`, tested directly on `@fuse_and_vectorize`'s own matmul section since that's the stage `l1`'s forward matmul routes through) does **not** work — confirmed directly by disassembling the real compiled object before and after: the exact same 32-register `vmovups %zmm0..%zmm31` spill, at the exact same `0xc40`-strided (`784` floats) call site, survives completely unchanged. Root cause, found by reading the surrounding disassembly directly rather than assumed: after MLIR's own tiling produces a real `98`-iteration `scf.for` (`784 / 8`), **LLVM's own backend `-O3` loop unroller fully unrolls it anyway** — the disassembly shows one unbroken, straight-line chain of `vfmadd132ps` steps at strides of `0xc40`, `0x1880`, `0x24c0`, ... with no loop back-edge at all, i.e. the *same* fully-flattened `784`-deep chain as before, just re-created one level lower in the pipeline. An isolated probe matching `l1`'s own exact shape (`32x784 @ 784x512`), pushed through the identical tile+vectorize+outerproduct schedule, does **not** reproduce any spill at all (`0` `vmovups %zmm` spills in the resulting asm) — meaning this is a genuine whole-function register-pressure effect from `train_and_evaluate` itself being one single, enormous (`~98KB`) compiled function with many other simultaneously-live values nearby, not something an isolated single-matmul probe can ever exercise. Tuning the *MLIR-level* tile size can't control this on its own while LLVM's own backend is free to re-unroll the result regardless — a real fix would need either suppressing LLVM's own loop-unroll decision for this specific loop (loop metadata, or a backend/`llc` flag — not investigated) or reducing whole-function register pressure some other way (e.g. splitting `train_and_evaluate` into smaller functions) — a bigger, separate undertaking, not attempted. Reverted cleanly (confirmed via `diff` against the pre-experiment file) — nothing from this specific attempt shipped.

**A second, more targeted attempt — real MLIR mechanism found and correctly wired, still doesn't work, also reverted.** Raised directly by the user: control the re-unrolling via loop annotations, rather than fighting it through tile size. Found real, by reading `SCFToControlFlow.cpp` directly: `propagateLoopAttrs` copies any attribute on an `scf.for` whose own *value* belongs to the LLVM dialect onto the `cf.cond_br`/`llvm.cond_br` its back-edge lowers to — exactly where `!llvm.loop.unroll.disable` metadata needs to live for LLVM's own backend to respect it. Wired via `transform.param.constant #llvm.loop_annotation<unroll = <disable = true>> -> !transform.any_param` + `transform.annotate %loops2 "llvm.loop_annotation" = %ann`, attached to the K-reduction loop specifically. Confirmed correct, on an isolated probe, all the way to a real `llvm.br {llvm.loop_annotation = ...}` surviving translation to real LLVM IR — but that same probe never actually got fully unrolled in the first place (no whole-function register pressure to trigger it), so it only validated the *plumbing*, not the *effect*. Tested directly on the real kernel (the only place the effect is even observable): **no change at all** — the identical, unbroken `32`-register-spill, fully-flattened `784`-deep chain, byte-for-byte the same shape as without the annotation. Real, plausible explanation, not confirmed further: LLVM's *full*-unroll decision for a small, statically-known trip count is a separate pass/heuristic from the general partial-unroller `loop.unroll.disable` targets, and may need a different metadata key (or combination) to suppress — not chased further, a genuine, deeper LLVM-backend-internals question past what this session's own remaining budget could responsibly cover. Reverted cleanly, confirmed via `diff`.

Both real attempts at this point converge on the same conclusion: this is a genuine LLVM backend behavior, not something either MLIR-level tiling or MLIR-level loop metadata alone can currently steer. The two remaining, unexplored real levers are: finding the *correct* metadata/pass-specific knob (would need reading LLVM's own `LoopUnrollPass`/`LoopFullUnrollPass` source directly, not attempted), or reducing whole-function register pressure structurally (splitting `train_and_evaluate` into smaller functions, a bigger, separate undertaking).

**The real trigger for the register spill, found by following the allocation angle instead — a genuine architectural gap, not an LLVM quirk at all.** Redirected by the user directly: this project already built a real arena (`cleave-rt`'s own `cleave_region_enter`/`cleave_alloc_local`/`cleave_region_exit`, a plain bump-pointer allocator) specifically to avoid exactly this kind of per-value heap traffic — so why does the spill happen at all? Traced precisely, not guessed:

- The `32`-register spill sits immediately before a real `llvm.call @cleave_alloc_rc(...)` — a genuine heap allocation (`std::alloc::alloc` plus a written refcount header, confirmed reading `cleave-rt/src/lib.rs` directly). Any function call forces the calling convention to spill every live vector register the callee might clobber — this is *why* the spill exists at all, not a side effect of loop unrolling.
- `doc/hld.md`'s own "Memory management" section states the arena's real, deliberate scope precisely: *"a refcounted object's own payload never lives here [the arena], precisely because the arena can't free one object out of order."* Every tensor in this codebase is, today, unconditionally refcounted (`unify_alloc.rs`'s own module doc: *"Gives cleave sole ownership of every tensor payload's own physical memory"* — a deliberate, blanket end-of-pipeline rename of every MLIR-bufferization-inserted `malloc`/`free` to `cleave_alloc_rc`/`cleave_release_void`, no per-site exception). Refcounted, by the arena's own documented rule, structurally excludes every tensor from ever using it.
- **A real, already-built, already-tested escape-analysis pass exists and is wired in, but doesn't cover tensors at all**: `cleave/src/region_analysis.rs` (`find_region_local_functions`, `8` passing tests, called unconditionally from `mlir_lower.rs`) determines which top-level `fn`s are safe to have *all* their internal allocations drawn from the currently-open region instead of the heap — a function is safe when it has exactly one call site in the whole program, inside a loop, whose result never escapes to that loop's own carried state. **Its own module doc comment names the real mnist-interop training loop as the motivating example, verbatim**: *"`net_grad` is safe to mark region-local; `Optimizer::step` is not."* Confirmed this verdict is real and correct — but its *effect* is scoped far narrower than its own analysis: `mlir_lower.rs::alloc_llvm_value` (`ctx.currently_region_local.get()`, gating `cleave_alloc_local` vs `cleave_alloc_rc`) is the *only* place this verdict changes anything, and that function only ever fires for **struct/array construction** (`Network`, `Dense`, a struct-leaf embedded array) — never for an ordinary tensor value. Every tensor intermediate (every layer's output, every `relu`, every one of `net_grad`'s own real internal gradients — `dz1`-`dz4`, `dh1`-`dh3`, `dW1`-`dW4`, `db1`-`db4`, dozens of allocations per single training batch) goes through MLIR's own bufferization (`memref.alloc`) instead, lowered to a plain `malloc`, later blanket-renamed to `cleave_alloc_rc` by `unify_alloc.rs` — a completely separate code path that has never once consulted `region_analysis`'s own verdict.

**Net effect, real and substantial, not yet fixed**: `net_grad` — literally the single hottest, most-executed piece of code in this whole kernel (`18,750` times over a full run) — is *correctly identified* as arena-safe by an analysis this project already built and tests, and gets **none** of the benefit, because the mechanism that verdict feeds only understands struct construction, not tensor computation. This plausibly explains a real, previously-unattributed share of the disassembly-measured "effective" compute time beyond the two findings already chased above (the accumulator chain, the `l1` register spill) — every one of those dozens of per-batch tensor allocations pays a real `malloc` call plus a written refcount header, each one forcing exactly the kind of register-spill-around-a-call-boundary this entry's own two failed experiments were chasing from the wrong end. Closing this gap for real needs bufferization itself to route through `cleave_alloc_local` inside a region-local function — either a custom allocation-function hook into `--one-shot-bufferize` (if MLIR exposes one), or a region-aware successor to `unify_alloc.rs`'s own blanket rename (redirecting a `malloc`/`free` pair to `cleave_alloc_local`/a no-op specifically when it sits inside a function `region_analysis` already marked safe) — a real, cross-cutting architecture change, not attempted yet, but now precisely scoped: the analysis and the arena both already exist and are already proven correct in isolation; only the bridge between "which functions are safe" (CPS-level) and "how a tensor's own bufferized storage gets allocated" (MLIR-level, downstream) is missing.

**A first real attempt at that bridge — built, found unsound before landing, cleanly reverted (`git checkout` on the three touched files; confirmed via `git status`/`git diff` that this left zero trace, and via a full rebuild + `mnist-interop` re-run, `test accuracy: 0.9342` unchanged).** The attempt: a `cleave_alloc_local_void(size) -> ptr` malloc-shaped wrapper (`cleave-rt`, mirroring `cleave_release_void`'s own existing precedent for the same "same-signature pure rename" trick), plus a region-*depth*-aware rewrite of `unify_alloc.rs`'s own `llvm.call @malloc` retargeting: walk each `llvm.func`'s basic blocks in order, track a counter incremented on `cleave_region_enter`/decremented on `cleave_region_exit`, redirect any `@malloc` found at depth > 0 to the arena instead of `cleave_alloc_rc`. Compiled and looked plausible on a real dump (169 real call sites redirected on `mnist-interop`) — but crashed `--emit-object` on the real kernel with an LLVM assertion (`dyn_cast on a non-existent value`) on the very first real test, before any correctness check beyond "does it compile."

**Root-caused precisely why, and found the fix would have been unsound even without the crash — a real design error, not a bug to patch.** `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`'s own doc comment states directly what this attempt missed: `cleave_region_enter`/`cleave_region_exit` bracket *one whole loop iteration*, unconditionally, for *every* loop — not the span of one region-local call. They're opened wide on purpose, specifically because a region-local call's own result (`net_grad`'s `g`) must stay valid until whatever *later* code in the *same iteration* finishes reading it (`Optimizer::step`, right after) — closing the region right after the call returns, which a narrower per-call marker would do, is exactly the bug this design avoids. The direct consequence: `Optimizer::step`'s own internal tensor arithmetic — genuinely escaping (`w`/`b` become next iteration's carried `net`/`state`) — executes *inside the identical* `cleave_region_enter`/`cleave_region_exit` span as `net_grad`'s own internal allocations, with nothing in the IR at that point distinguishing which callee an allocation actually came from. A depth-only check cannot tell these apart; it would have silently arena-backed a real escaping tensor, corrupting `net`/`state` the moment the next `cleave_region_exit` rewound the arena cursor out from under it — a data-corruption bug, strictly worse than the crash that actually happened first and caught it.

**Found a second, independent, deeper obstacle to the "tag the decision early, let it survive to the rewrite" alternative — real pipeline ordering, checked directly in `pipeline.rs`, not assumed.** The natural fix for the depth-ambiguity above is to decide `cleave_alloc_rc` vs `cleave_alloc_local` using real *function identity* (`region_analysis`'s own actual granularity) rather than textual region depth, tagging the decision once, early, and carrying it through to the eventual `llvm.call @malloc`. But `--inline` (`pipeline.rs`, right after the transform-dialect matmul schedule) runs *before* `--one-shot-bufferize` — confirmed directly, by line number, not inferred from behavior — so `net_grad` no longer exists as a separate `llvm.func` even at the *bufferization* stage, let alone by the time `unify_alloc.rs` runs after `--convert-to-llvm`. A real fix has to mark the decision at the *original*, initial-lowering stage (`mlir_lower.rs`, exactly where `ctx.currently_region_local` is already known for structs) and carry a real, discardable attribute through every one of `--inline` → `--convert-elementwise-to-linalg` → `--linalg-fuse-elementwise-ops` → the transform-dialect tile/vectorize schedule → `--one-shot-bufferize` → `--scf-forall-to-parallel` → `--convert-scf-to-openmp` → `--convert-to-llvm` — several of which (fusion, tiling, vectorization) rebuild ops from scratch with no established guarantee of preserving an arbitrary attribute across the rewrite. Not yet checked empirically pass-by-pass; a real, bounded next step (dump the IR after each stage, confirm or refute survival directly) rather than a reason to abandon the idea, but genuinely more work than "add a rename" — this is the concrete shape of the "cross-cutting architecture change" the entry above already flagged, now with the exact pass list and the exact failure mode identified.


## `--inline` itself, directly confirmed as the real cause of the `l1` register spill (the entry above's own "32-register spill next to `cleave_alloc_rc`") — `#[no_inline]` built, measured as a net regression once properly benchmarked, and set aside; the real fix was the arena/`region_analysis.rs` bridge this same investigation had already found and scoped — done, ~10-12% real, confirmed win

Asked directly by the user, following up on the register-pressure finding above: does the spill go away without `--inline`? Tested directly (`pipeline.rs`'s own `create_inliner()` call made conditional on a temporary env var, `examples/mnist-interop`'s real kernel recompiled with it off, disassembled — the change reverted immediately after, `git diff` confirmed clean).

**Confirmed, disassembly-direct: without `--inline`, `l1`'s own matmul reduction (`MatMul::matmul<Tensor<f32,32,784>,...>..omp_par.25`, the real per-thread reduction body, 296 instructions) has *zero* allocation calls inside it at all** — its own only 6 `callq` sites are all OpenMP runtime helpers (`__kmpc_global_thread_num`, `__kmpc_for_static_init_8u`/`_fini`, `__kmpc_barrier`) and one `__chkstk` stack probe, never `cleave_alloc_rc`. The FMA mix (15× `vfmadd132ps`, 1× `vfmadd231ps`, 16× `vbroadcastss`, only 2× `vmovups` in the *whole* function) confirms the accumulator itself stays register-resident across the real, genuine loop (confirmed via its own backward jumps, `jb`/`jne` — not unrolled) — the exact "textbook GEMM microkernel" shape the transform-dialect tile/vectorize schedule elsewhere in this file was built to chase, apparently arising here without it, once nothing forces the register allocator to spill around an unrelated call boundary.

**But not a free win — the real, measured cost, confirmed by disassembling the same kernel both ways**: total `cleave_alloc_rc` relocations across the whole compiled object nearly **triple** (75 with `--inline` → 231 without), and the object file's own size nearly triples too (122 KB → 350 KB). `--inline` was added for a real, already-documented, different reason (`pipeline.rs`'s own comment on it, and `doc/backlog-done.md`'s own "destination-passing rewrite" entry): without it, a cross-function tensor chain like the SGD update (`Ring::sub(model, Scale::scale(grad, opt.lr))`, two separate `func.func`s) can't be fused by `--linalg-fuse-elementwise-ops` into one kernel, so *every* such chain materializes its own separate scratch buffer instead of one fused result — exactly what the relocation-count increase reflects. The two problems are real and both confirmed, but pull in opposite directions from the same knob: `--inline` trades a clean, per-function register allocation (no spill around unrelated calls) for far fewer total allocations overall (cross-function fusion) — today's setting (`--inline` on) already wins on raw allocation count by a wide margin, so simply flipping it off is a net regression, not a fix.

**Real next step, well-specified, currently in progress (kept — a first over-hasty full revert via `git checkout --` was corrected; see below).** A *selective* inlining policy: `#[no_inline]`, a real, general attribute (`grammar.pest`'s own pre-existing `fn_decl = { attribute* ~ ... }`, zero grammar changes) threaded structurally through `ConcreteUnit`/`CTopLevelFn` (mirroring `origin`'s own existing "read straight off `f.attrs` where `f: &FnDecl` is already on hand" shape) down to `mlir_lower.rs::lower_top_level_fn`, which sets the real `no_inline` unit attribute `func::FuncOp`/`func::CallOp` already support and MLIR's own generic inliner already consults (`FuncInlinerInterface::isLegalToInline`, confirmed directly in MLIR's own source) — no cleave-compiler-side hardcoding of "matmul" anywhere, `stdlib/linalg/matrix.cleave`'s own `MatMul::matmul` impl declares it, matching this project's own extensibility discipline exactly.

**Measured exactly the hoped-for result in isolation first.** `examples/mnist-interop`'s own real kernel, recompiled with only this attribute added: `l1`'s own matmul reduction disassembled *identically clean* to the "fully disabled `--inline`" case above (zero allocation calls, the same register-resident FMA chain) — but `cleave_alloc_rc` relocations stayed near the *fused* baseline (81, not 231) and object size grew only modestly (168 KB, not 350 KB): the cross-function elementwise fusion `--linalg-fuse-elementwise-ops` needs kept working for everything *except* matmul, exactly as designed.

**But the real, full workspace test suite caught a genuine regression immediately**: `dps_rewrite.rs`'s own `matmuls_own_fill_seed_is_redirected_and_the_copy_still_neuters` test failed — expected and understood (`dps_rewrite.rs`'s own redundant-copy elimination needs the *whole* producer chain, matmul included, inlined into the same function as the struct-field write to recognize its own pattern at all; `#[no_inline]` on matmul specifically prevents that, for every struct-field write whose source traces through a matmul — the SGD weight-update case, concretely). A real, bounded cost, not itself disqualifying.

**A real crash was found running the real kernel, not the test suite**: `examples/mnist-interop`'s own compiled binary, previously accuracy-verified and stable all session, **crashes with a genuine stack overflow** (`thread 'main' has overflowed its stack`, a hard segfault) — confirmed reproducible, confirmed *not* OpenMP-specific (`--no-openmp` crashes identically), confirmed not a direct self-recursion (no function calls itself). Traced part of the way: the lowered IR shows the known-dangerous "Problem B" shape (`doc/backlog.md`'s own earlier entry on the transform-dialect matmul schedule's own pad-retry fallback) reappearing — a real loop (confirmed via its own backward jump) whose body allocates several `llvm.alloca` scratch buffers per iteration, all bracketed by a *single* `stacksave`/`stackrestore` pair spanning the whole loop rather than one per iteration — for `MatMul::matmul<Tensor<f32,128,32>,Tensor<f32,32,10>,Tensor<f32,128,10>>` (an `N=10`, non-16-aligned output shape, the same class Problem B was originally about).

**Two important corrections, both found by testing the mechanism (kept, not reverted) via the AOT path instead of `--run`/JIT, per direct user instruction after a first, over-hasty full revert of this whole entry's own code (`git checkout --` on all five files) triggered by an unrelated JIT-only incident (below):**

1. **The stack overflow is *not* pre-existing — it's a real regression caused by `#[no_inline]` itself, confirmed by direct A/B testing**: `git stash` (not `git checkout --`, kept reversible) of every `#[no_inline]`-related change, `mnist-interop` rebuilt and rerun — the *baseline* (no `#[no_inline]` anywhere) runs cleanly through at least 7 full epochs with no crash, no growth, nothing (stopped only by an external timeout). Restoring the stash and rebuilding reproduces the stack overflow again immediately, in well under 30 seconds, every time. So this is squarely a bug in how `#[no_inline]` on `MatMul::matmul` interacts with the rest of the compiled kernel, not a pre-existing issue this change merely exposed.
2. **The two real hard machine resets earlier attributed to this investigation in general were specifically a JIT (`--run`/`ExecutionEngine::invoke_packed`) phenomenon, not an AOT one** — confirmed by testing the *exact same* `#[no_inline]`-enabled kernel via the AOT path (`cargo build -p mnist-interop --release`, running the linked `.exe` directly) instead of JIT: it crashes fast, cleanly, and boundedly (a genuine stack overflow, process exits, no hang, no runaway memory) every single time — nothing like the JIT path's unbounded memory growth. The AOT path is safe to keep using for this investigation; the JIT path (`--run`) specifically is not, until that separate mechanism is understood.

**Root-caused precisely, via a minimal, one-line reproduction — and it has nothing to do with `N=10`, alignment, `grad()`, or `Optimizer::step` at all.** Side-by-side IR comparison (`--dump-mlir-lowered --openmp`, with vs. without `#[no_inline]`, a temporary debug hook dumping the module right after the `--inline`/elementwise-fusion pass, removed after use) showed the *same* dangerous shape — a real loop allocating several `llvm.alloca` scratch buffers per iteration, all bracketed by a single `stacksave`/`stackrestore` spanning the *whole* loop — present in **both** cases, at every matmul shape, not just `N=10`. For `MatMul::matmul<Tensor<f32,128,32>,Tensor<f32,32,10>,...>` specifically (standalone, `#[no_inline]`'d), the reduction loop bound was traced precisely to `K=32`, and its own `stackrestore` *does* fire correctly right after that loop exits — bounded, and small (~128 allocas' worth), exactly as designed. So a lone `N=10`-shaped call was never going to be the culprit on its own.

**The real variable is `K` (the reduction dimension), not `N`-alignment.** A from-scratch minimal probe — a *single*, bare call, no loop, no `grad()`, no `Optimizer::step` — `let c: Tensor<f32,32,512> = MatMul::matmul(a, b);` with `a: Tensor<f32,32,784>`, `b: Tensor<f32,784,512>` (the real MNIST input width, `K=784`) — **crashes on its own** with `#[no_inline]` active, and runs cleanly (confirmed via `git stash` of just `matrix.cleave`, rebuilt, re-tested) without it. Bisecting a 4-layer forward-only chain (no backward pass, no optimizer at all) the same way confirmed it further: even 10 iterations of the full `784→512→256→128→10` chain crash, and so does a *single* call to just the first (`K=784`) layer alone. Each `#[no_inline]`'d reduction's own per-iteration alloca'd scratch (confirmed structurally: several buffers per `K`-step, released only once the *whole* `K`-loop finishes) costs roughly a small constant per iteration — for `K=32` that's small and harmless (~128 allocas' worth, well under any default stack); for `K=784` (this project's own real MNIST input width) that same per-iteration cost, times 784 iterations, is enough on its own to exhaust a default ~1MB main-thread stack, in a single call, before the function even returns.

**The "K matters, not N-alignment" framing above was a real, useful narrowing step, but the actual root cause sits one layer deeper — and it has nothing to do with the transform-dialect schedule's own pad-retry fallback at all.** Disproved directly: dumping the module right after the transform-dialect schedule runs (a temporary debug hook, removed after use) showed the `K=784` matmul's own `vector.transfer_read`/`write` ops *already* clean (`in_bounds = [true, true]`, no masking, no padding) — the schedule vectorizes this shape perfectly on the very first attempt, exactly as it does when inlined. A separate probe (`Init::xavier` alone, no matmul at all, filling the identical `784x512` tensor) doesn't crash either — ruling out the tensor-initialization loop as the source too.

**The real cause: `one-shot-bufferize`'s own function-boundary handling.** Dumping the module right after `one-shot-bufferize` (also a temporary debug hook) showed it: a `#[no_inline]`'d function's own tensor *parameters* — crossing a genuine `func.func` call boundary — bufferize to `memref<32x784xf32, strided<[?, ?], offset: ?>>`, a **fully dynamic** layout (unknown strides, unknown offset), instead of the plain `memref<32x784xf32>` (static, contiguous, row-major) a same-function-produced tensor gets. `bufferize-function-boundaries=true` alone doesn't pin this down — MLIR's own default for a function boundary is deliberately conservative (`fully-dynamic-layout-map`, since *in general* a caller could hand a function any layout at all). A `vector.transfer_read`/`write` against an unknown-stride memref cannot lower to a plain vector load/store no matter how clean its `in_bounds` attribute is — it falls back to the generic, scalarized lowering (`--convert-vector-to-scf`'s masked-transfer path), allocating real per-iteration scratch, bracketed by the single loop-wide `stacksave`/`stackrestore` `--convert-scf-to-openmp` wraps around the whole per-thread body (`pipeline.rs`'s own comment on that pass, elsewhere in this file) — the actual mechanism behind the crash, present at *every* `K`, just below the crash threshold for small ones.

**Fixed, one line, in `pipeline.rs`**: `one-shot-bufferize`'s own `function-boundary-type-conversion` option (confirmed present in this exact toolchain via `mlir-opt --help-hidden`) defaults to that conservative `fully-dynamic-layout-map`; setting it to `identity-layout-map` instead gives every function-boundary memref the plain, static, contiguous layout a same-function tensor already gets. Sound specifically for this project (not a general-purpose loosening papering over a real risk): cleave's own monomorphization guarantees every `Tensor` value crossing a call boundary is either a fresh allocation or an unchanged view of one, *always* contiguous, row-major, never a non-trivial stride or offset — the conservative default was protecting against a case that structurally cannot occur here. Confirmed fixed: the minimal `K=784` single-call probe, the bisected forward chain, and the real `mnist-interop` kernel (10 full epochs, `#[no_inline]` active) all run clean, no crash, correct accuracy (0.9342), every time. Full workspace suite green (`dps_rewrite.rs`'s own already-understood, already-documented regression aside). Note the fully-lowered MLIR text still *shows* the same alloca/stacksave pattern textually even with the fix applied — the actual win happens downstream, in LLVM's own backend optimizer (SROA/mem2reg, invoked during `--emit-object`/`--emit-exe`, not visible in any `--dump-mlir-*` output), which can now prove the small per-iteration scratch buffer never escapes once its address is derived from a statically-known-contiguous base, and promotes it to registers instead of real stack space.

**The fix holds at any `K`, confirmed, not assumed**: a `K=65536` probe (83× the real MNIST `K=784`) compiles and runs clean — and, checked directly in the emitted object's own disassembly (`llvm-objdump`), every one of the matmul function's own stack-frame `sub rsp, <imm>` sites is a small, fixed, compile-time-constant immediate (largest seen: `0x168`, 360 bytes), completely independent of `K`, no `__chkstk` probe even needed. This confirms the mechanism precisely: LLVM's own SROA/mem2reg promotes the per-iteration scratch to registers because it can prove non-escape from a *single textual instance* of the loop body — a proof that doesn't depend on how many times that instance executes at runtime, only on what it does in one iteration. Growing `K` cannot reintroduce the alloca-in-loop danger through this mechanism.

**Honest, corrected finding on the actual payoff — an earlier reading of this was wrong, from noisy measurement, not from a real result.** The first attempt at full-kernel, end-to-end timing (via this session's own sandboxed shell) found a spread wide enough (±5-8s on a ~40s run) to call the comparison a wash: with `#[no_inline]`, 40.8s/44.2s/43.6s/45.3s; without, 38.4s/46.7s/45.0s. **Redone directly by the user, in a real terminal, same machine, same binary, `cargo run --release` both ways** — far tighter, far more trustworthy numbers: **with `#[no_inline]`: 37.1s/36.3s/36.9s/36.8s (~36.8s avg); without: 34.9s/34.2s (~34.6s avg).** Clean, repeatable, low-noise, and unambiguous: **`#[no_inline]` is currently a real, measurable net regression on the full kernel, roughly 6-8%**, not neutral and not a win — despite the genuine, correctly-attributed, isolated win it produces on `l1` alone.

**Root cause of the discrepancy, found by reading the real call graph, not re-guessed**: `#[no_inline]` is declared on the *whole* generic `MatMul::matmul` impl — every monomorphized shape gets it, not just the one (`K=784`, `l1`'s own forward matmul) the original register-spill measurement was about. The real kernel calls this impl at *eight* distinct shapes (four forward layers, four backward weight-gradient matmuls) — and `stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s own `Dense`-level `Optimizer::step` (`fn step(opt, model, grad, state) { let rw = Optimizer::step(opt, model.w, grad.w, state.w); ...; (Dense(w: rw.0, ...), ...) }`) writes each layer's updated weight into a fresh `Dense` struct field — a real, matmul-sourced struct-field write, once *per layer* (four of them), each one exactly the shape `dps_rewrite.rs`'s own copy-elimination needs the full producer chain inlined to recognize. `#[no_inline]` breaks all four, paying a real `memcpy` (784×512, 512×256, 256×128, and 128×10 floats respectively) on every single training step (18,750 of them over a full run) — while only *one* of the eight call sites (`l1`'s own forward matmul) was ever confirmed to have the register-spill problem this attribute exists to fix in the first place. One real, confirmed win, paying for four real, confirmed losses.

**The obvious next step (scoping `#[no_inline]` to just the one shape that needs it, via a more specific impl) turned out to be a dead end, checked before attempting it rather than after**: `infer.rs`'s own `TypeErrorKind::OverlappingImpls` rejects a concrete `MatMul<Tensor<f32,32,784>,Tensor<f32,784,512>,Tensor<f32,32,512>>` impl coexisting with the generic one — cleave has no "most-specific-impl-wins" resolution rule at all, only a hard, unconditional error the moment two impls could both match the same type. `#[no_inline]` set aside entirely (mechanism kept in `cps.rs`/`mlir_lower.rs`, not applied anywhere) — see below for the real fix.

**The real fix, found by going back to the actual root cause instead of working around its symptom**: this section's own earlier finding (the "allocation angle" paragraph, above) already named it precisely — the `32`-register spill is the x86-64 calling convention forcing a save across *any* call a live accumulator's own caller might make, and `net_grad` (the reduction's real caller) makes plenty of them, because every one of its own internal tensor intermediates still goes through a genuine heap `cleave_alloc_rc` — `region_analysis.rs`'s own arena-safety verdict, already correctly computed for `net_grad` itself, never reached them. `#[no_inline]` only ever isolated the *symptom* (one specific reduction, moved away from whichever call happened to sit next to it); this closes the actual gap. Done as a real, tested extension to `region_analysis.rs` (not a new mechanism — `mlir_lower.rs::tensor_seed`, committed earlier this project alongside this same investigation, already builds a real arena-backed tensor destination whenever `ctx.currently_region_local` is true; it was sound but *permanently inert*, because `net_grad` never constructs a tensor directly — it delegates every real computation to shared algebra functions, `MatMul::matmul`/`Ring::add`/`Scale::scale`, and `region_analysis`'s own single-level scan never looked *inside* an already-confirmed-safe function's own body to see them):

- **The soundness argument**: once a callee's own call site is proven not to reach the enclosing loop's own carried state, *everything* that callee computes internally is safe by the same reasoning, transitively — no separate escaping re-check needed at any recursion depth (this language has no mutable globals or capturing closures a value could escape through besides its own return, and a callee's own return is exactly what the outer check already covers). What still has to hold, at *every* depth, is the original, unchanged precondition: exactly one call site for that specific name, in the *whole* program (`call_counts`, computed once, globally) — a shape genuinely shared between a region-local caller and any other context anywhere is still correctly excluded, which is precisely why `MatMul::matmul`'s own backward-pass weight-gradient instantiations (structurally distinct shapes from any forward-pass call — a transposed operand order) qualify while a shape actually shared between contexts never would.
- Implemented as a worklist-based transitive closure in `find_region_local_functions` (`region_analysis.rs`): once a callee is confirmed region-local, its own body's direct top-level calls are checked against the same single-call-site test and, if they pass, marked and pushed for further descent — multi-hop and mutually-recursive shapes both handled correctly (10 new tests in `cleave/tests/region_analysis.rs`, including the one this whole extension exists to still get right: a function shared between a region-local caller's own body and a completely unrelated call site elsewhere must never be marked, checked directly, not assumed).
- **Measured, cleanly, by the user directly, same low-noise terminal methodology this whole entry's own earlier correction established**: two repeated `cargo run --release -p mnist-interop` runs, **30.58s and 30.78s** (accuracy unchanged, `0.9342`) — against the ~34.6s average baseline this same entry already established as the honest, low-noise reference point. A real, reproducible **~10-12% improvement**, on top of everything already landed this session, with none of `#[no_inline]`'s own costs (`dps_rewrite.rs`'s optimization stays fully intact — nothing in this fix touches inlining at all).


## The `36.7%` accumulator-latency chain (`vfmadd132ps` rotating through `zmm0`-`zmm3`, one dependency each, this same document's own "Real profiler ground truth" entry above) — real fix found and landed, a wider `M`-tile on the *right* matmul specifically, ~36% single-thread win, a new best multi-thread time too

Asked directly by the user, following up once the register-spill/arena work above closed 32% of the original single-thread cycle budget: the one remaining, precisely-quantified, still-open item is this accumulator chain — is it fixable now?

**The theory, confirmed structurally first, on an isolated `mlir-opt` probe, before ever touching cleave's real schedule.** The current schedule's own `M`-tile (the batch/row dimension) is `1` everywhere (`tile_using_forall ... tile_sizes [1, 0, 0]`/`[1, 0]`) — a single output row per accumulator, so the whole `K`-reduction (`784` steps for `l1`) is one unbroken, serially-chained `vfmadd213ps` sequence, each step's own third operand (the running sum) being the *previous* step's own output — confirmed directly in the disassembly (`zmm26 = zmm28*zmm26 + zmm27`, `zmm25 = zmm27*zmm25 + zmm26`, ...), latency-bound by construction (~4 cycles/step, no independent work to overlap it with). Widening the tile to `M=4` on an isolated probe matching `l1`'s exact shape (`32x784 @ 784x512`), pushed all the way through real `llc -mcpu=znver4` codegen (not just MLIR text), produces exactly the hoped-for shape: `vector.outerproduct`'s own `4x16` accumulator lowers to *four* separate physical `zmm` registers, each fed by its own independent `vfmadd213ps` chain (`zmm31`/`zmm6`/`zmm7`/`zmm8` in the first four instructions, no cross-register dependency) — confirmed directly reading the instruction operands, not inferred. `llvm-mca`'s own static analysis on the extracted fragment gave an inconclusive/misleading signal (its own iteration-replay model doesn't represent one genuinely `784`-deep chain correctly) and was abandoned in favor of a real, controlled measurement on the actual kernel instead.

**Two real, over-hasty, wrong-target attempts on the real kernel — found and corrected before landing anything, not guessed past.** The schedule has *three* separate `M`-tiling sites, not one: `@fuse_and_vectorize`'s own `%epi` tile (Stage 1, governs any matmul fused with a real elementwise consumer — `l1`'s own forward matmul, concretely, the one this whole investigation is about), `@tile_and_vectorize`'s own `%m` tile (Stage 2, the standalone fallback — the *backward*-pass weight-gradient matmuls route through here, `K=32` always, a reduction only *2* tiles deep, never the bottleneck), and Stage 0's own `%dx` tile (the backward root-fusion chain, same short-`K` shape). First attempt widened *all three* to `M=4` at once — measured a real *regression* (single-thread `115.0s`, worse than the `75.0s` baseline). Root-caused by reading the schedule's own routing logic, not re-guessed: `l1`'s forward matmul is fused into its own bias-add epilogue and routes through **Stage 1**, whose own `M`-tile is governed entirely by `%epi` (line 66) — **completely unaffected** by Stage 0/Stage 2's own tile sizes. A second attempt, isolating *only* Stage 0/Stage 2 to `M=4` (leaving `%epi` at `1`), measured **even worse** (single-thread `135.8s`) — this widened the tile on the *wrong* matmuls entirely (the short, `K=32` backward-pass ones, which had no latency problem to fix in the first place), paying real overhead (wider slicing/tiling bookkeeping) for zero corresponding benefit.

**The real fix: widen only `@fuse_and_vectorize`'s own `%epi` tile (Stage 1) to `M=4`, leave Stage 0/Stage 2 at `M=1` (their own short-`K` matmuls were never the target).** Full workspace test suite green; `digits-interop` re-verified end to end (`test accuracy: 0.94713414`, unchanged) alongside `mnist-interop` (`0.9342`, unchanged) — confirms the fix generalizes correctly to a different network's own matmul/epilogue shapes, not just this one kernel. **Measured directly by the user, three separate thread counts, same low-noise terminal methodology already established**: `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1`: **48.3s** (down from the `75.0s` established baseline — a real, substantial **~36% single-thread improvement**, matching the theory closely: this accumulator chain was genuinely the dominant remaining single-thread cost). `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8`: **24.56s** — a new best multi-thread time, better than both the previous default-thread-count baseline (`~30.6s`) and this same run's own `16`-thread result (`30.26s`, this fix alone, no change to thread count, essentially a wash — the fix's own benefit compounds much better with fewer, physical-only threads than with the OS's default logical/SMT count). Against PyTorch-CPU's own `7.64s` (`8` threads): **the gap narrows from `~4x` to `~3.2x`.**

**A real, related, already-once-flagged lever, reconfirmed here and still not landed**: `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8` (physical cores) beating `16` (default, logical/SMT) by a wide margin matches this same document's own much earlier "SMT/AVX-512 execution-unit contention" finding (two AVX-512-bound threads sharing one physical core's own vector ALU) — its own "real next step" (`cleave-rt`'s own OpenMP init defaulting to the machine's real physical core count, via `GetLogicalProcessorInformationEx`/`RelationProcessorCore` on Windows) was never implemented. Doubly motivated now — worth doing for real, not just documenting again.

## `linalg.transpose`'s own real cost (gather/scatter) eliminated entirely — the backward pass never materializes a transpose at all any more, via `linalg.matmul_transpose_a`/`_b`'s own `indexing_maps`, worked around melior's missing Properties API by going through MLIR's own textual parser

Follows directly from `doc/backlog.md`'s own former "blocked by a genuine melior API gap" entry (moved here, superseded by this one) — the user's own question ("est-ce qu'il existe une forme mathématique...") and the two real next steps it left open: patch melior's own Properties API, or construct the op via MLIR's own textual parser. **Went with the parser** — no melior/mlir-sys patch needed at all.

**One correction to that earlier entry's own framing, found while implementing this**: `linalg.matmul_transpose_a`/`_b` are **not real MLIR mnemonics** — confirmed directly, `Linalg.h`: `MatmulTransposeAOp`/`BOp` are plain C++ subclasses of `MatmulOp` (`class MatmulTransposeAOp : public MatmulOp`), never registered under their own name in any `.td` file or generated `.inc`. `MatmulTransposeAOp::create(...)` is a C++-only convenience that builds an ordinary `linalg.matmul` with non-default `indexing_maps` and lets `classof` recognize the result after the fact — there is no `linalg.matmul_transpose_a` for MLIR's own parser to ever see. The real, only-ever-real textual form (confirmed directly, `mlir/test/Dialect/Linalg/generalize-named-ops.mlir`'s own `@matmul_bcast_a`) is the plain named op with an explicit `indexing_maps = [...]` clause: `linalg.matmul indexing_maps = [affine_map<...>, affine_map<...>, affine_map<...>] ins(...) outs(...) -> ...` — region elided exactly like an ordinary `linalg.matmul`, MLIR's own custom parser synthesizes the canonical multiply-accumulate body regardless of which indexing maps are attached. `lower_raw_mlir_op`'s own `"linalg.matmul_transpose_a"`/`"_b"` dispatch strings are cleave's own internal names for what it does, not real MLIR ones — the doc comment on `build_matmul_transpose_no_seed` (`mlir_lower.rs`) spells this out precisely so nobody re-discovers it by surprise later.

**The parser workaround, implemented and working**: `mlirOperationCreateParse(context, sourceStr, sourceName) -> MlirOperation` (`mlir-c/IR.h`) is a real MLIR C-API function, confirmed present in this project's own generated `mlir-sys` bindings and now called directly (`mlir-sys` added as a *direct* `cleave/Cargo.toml` dependency, previously only transitive via melior — same pinned fork/branch/commit either way, so still exactly one real copy). Melior itself doesn't wrap this function, but exposes exactly the raw-pointer escape hatches needed: `Context::to_raw`, `StringRef::new`/`to_raw`, `Operation::from_raw`/`from_option_raw`, and (the part that makes this actually usable, not just callable) `OperationLike::region`/`RegionLike::first_block`/`BlockLike::first_operation_mut`/`OperationMutLike::remove_from_parent`/`set_operands`. The one real wrinkle flagged in advance (a standalone parse needs *bound names* for its operands, and the real, already-lowered `a`/`b`/`init` values have none) turned out to have a clean answer: parse a tiny throwaway `func.func` wrapper (its own block arguments stand in for the operands, typed with their real MLIR types via melior's own `Type: Display`), reach inside it with the escape hatches above to detach the one real op (`remove_from_parent`), rewire its operands onto the real values (`set_operands`, same types as the placeholder block args it replaces, so no re-verification surprise), and append it into the real block. The wrapper (and its now-orphaned `func.return`, holding nothing but a dangling *use* of the just-detached op's own result — never a problem, `mlirOperationRemoveFromParent`'s own doc comment: "not destroyed", only unlinked) is simply dropped once its one useful op has been extracted.

**`stdlib/linalg/matrix.cleave`'s own adjoint rule now reads exactly as the math is**: `adjoint matmul(a, b), u: (matmul_transpose_b(u, b), matmul_transpose_a(a, u))` — two new algebras, `MatMulTransposeA`/`MatMulTransposeB`, each a thin one-impl wrapper over `mlir::linalg::matmul_transpose_a`/`_b`, shapes checked by hand and confirmed by the compiler's own inference (`MatMulTransposeB<Tensor<T,P,Q>,Tensor<T,R,Q>,Tensor<T,P,R>>`, `MatMulTransposeA<Tensor<T,P,Q>,Tensor<T,P,R>,Tensor<T,Q,R>>`) — no `transpose(...)` call anywhere in the backward pass any more. An existing unit test (`egraph.rs`'s `adjoint_matmul_rule_produces_the_real_transpose_based_gradient_formula_for_a_non_square_case`) still hard-asserted the *old* `matmul(dC, transpose(b))` formula structurally — updated to assert the new `matmul_transpose_b(dC, b)`/`matmul_transpose_a(a, dC)` shape instead (a real, intended behavior change, not a regression to chase).

**Correctness confirmed, not just "didn't crash" — both cross-checks landed on the *exact same* accuracy already on record from before this change**: `digits-interop`, bounded AOT run, `test accuracy: 0.94713414` — bit-for-bit identical to the value already documented above (this entry's own predecessor fix), a strong signal since this test is fully deterministic: the new construction path produces numerically identical results to the old transpose-based one, not merely "close." `mnist-interop`, full 10-epoch bounded AOT run, `test accuracy: 0.9342` — likewise identical to the value already on record above. Full workspace test suite green (52 unit tests in `cleave`, all integration suites, both doctests) after the one intentional test-expectation update.

**The `NYI: non-trivial layout map` diagnostic, chased down and resolved — a red herring, not a sign the fix isn't working**: found while validating (a minimal isolated probe emitted `error: NYI: non-trivial layout map`, sourced to the new op's own synthetic parse location, from `LoopAnalysis.cpp`'s `isContiguousAccess`, part of the *legacy* `--affine-super-vectorize` cost-model heuristic). Reproduced the *identical* diagnostic on a **plain, untransposed** `linalg.matmul` of the same non-divisible shape (`mlir-opt`, isolated probes, both through the real Stage 2 tile/vectorize/pad-retry sequence) — confirmed this is the project's own already-known, already-tolerated non-divisible-shape case (the `128 -> 10` output layer), completely unrelated to the transpose work; an evenly-divisible transposed shape vectorizes to a clean `vector.contract` exactly like an ordinary matmul, tiling included, M-tile size included (tested `[1,0,0]` and `[4,0,0]` both, plus no M-tile at all).

**Disassembly, old (`d43dba9`) vs new (`d2d75ba`), same toolchain, real `mnist-interop` kernel**: `vgather` **68 → 40** (-41%), and — the real signal — spread across **8 distinct functions before, only 2 after**: most of the backward pass's matmuls now vectorize completely cleanly, no gather at all, including the two largest functions in the whole compiled object (2607 and 1964 disassembled lines — the dominant matmuls). `vscatter` unchanged (`20 → 20`, confirmed *not* a leftover `linalg.transpose` — zero `Transpose`-named symbols anywhere in the compiled object's own symbol table); the remaining 40 gather + 20 scatter sit in two small functions (≤312 lines each), not the hot ones.

**Wall-clock, resolved via a proper back-to-back comparison, not a single lucky number**: the first single-sample comparison (`22.80s` old vs `24.7-28.3s` new, at `OMP_NUM_THREADS=4`) looked like a regression, but `22.80s` was one lone measurement, never itself replicated. Rebuilt the pre-fix commit in a disposable `git worktree` (no history rewrite) and ran both binaries back-to-back, same machine, same session: **old — 26.88s, 26.76s (mean 26.82s); new — 24.74s, 24.82s, 28.33s (mean 25.96s)**. The new code is, if anything, **~3% faster on average**, consistent with the disassembly's real structural win — the apparent regression was measurement noise from comparing against a single unrepresentative old-code sample, not a real effect. `OMP_NUM_THREADS=4` specifically has real run-to-run variance on this machine (~14% spread across 3 same-binary runs) — worth remembering before trusting any single future measurement at this thread count again.

## Inherent impls (`impl Type { ... }`, no algebra) removed entirely as a language concept — dot-call is now pure sugar over top-level generic functions and algebras, closing the three items above (`Dot-call and bare-call are two disjoint resolution paths`, `A zero-parameter ("static"/receiver-less) method...`, `An inherent-impl method can't construct a fresh instance...`) by eliminating the mechanism they were all about, not by patching it

Reached via a real design question, not a feature request: once `v.method(args)` is pure syntactic sugar for `method(v, args)` — resolved through the exact same call-site machinery a bare call already uses — an inherent impl types exactly the same constraint as an ordinary top-level generic `fn` taking the receiver as its own first parameter. The *only* thing an inherent impl provided beyond that (dot-call syntax) becomes free for *any* function once the dot is desugared this way. Algebras remain irreducibly necessary for two structural reasons no function or inherent impl could ever replace: genuine multi-type relational dispatch (`MatMul<A,B,C>`, dispatched on the *pair* `(A,B)`, impossible to attach to a single type's own impl block) and output-type-driven inference with no qualifier anywhere at the call site (`Init::xavier()`, no receiver value exists to dispatch from). Decision: remove `impl Type { ... }` as a language concept (grammar, AST, registry, inference, callgraph, CPS, monomorphization) rather than keep it as redundant sugar.

**Desugar**: `lower.rs`'s `lower_postfix_op` rewrites `v.method(args)` directly to `ExprKind::Call(Path::single(method), [], [v, ...args], [])` at lowering time — an ordinary bare call, the receiver spliced in as an ordinary first argument. No separate `MethodCall` AST node, no separate resolution table; dot-call and bare-call now converge on the exact same `infer_call` path (qualified `Algebra::method`, then `algebras_with_fn(name, arity)`, then `env.get(name)` for a let-bound lambda or top-level `fn`'s own scheme). One real exception preserved: `x.to()` still substitutes the callee name to `convert` (reaching `Convert<From,To>`'s algebra method) — the one case where the call-site name isn't the callee's own name.

**Removed end to end, confirmed by `grep -rn "InherentImpl\|inherent_method\|InherentTemplate\|resolve_method_call" cleave/src/` finding nothing left but historical doc-comment references**: `grammar.pest`'s `inherent_impl` rule; `ast.rs`'s `ItemKind::InherentImpl`/`InherentImplDecl`/`ExprKind::MethodCall`; `registry.rs`'s `inherent_methods`/`InherentMethodEntry`/`inherent_method()`; `infer.rs`'s `resolve_method_call`, `infer_inherent_impl_fn_generic`, `infer_inherent_impl_block`, `in_progress_methods`, the method-call halves of `pending_method_calls`/`check_pending_method_calls`; `callgraph.rs`'s `infer_inherent_impls_early`/`with_inherent_patterns`/`InherentMethodPattern`; `cps.rs`'s `InherentImpl` handling in `collect_units`; `monomorphize.rs`'s `InherentTemplate`/`derive_inherent_instantiation`/the whole `inherent` worklist. `Dims.len()`'s own pack-generic special case (`pack_len_from_method_call`) was re-gated onto the new desugared `ExprKind::Call` shape rather than lost.

**The three real inherent impls anywhere in the repo, migrated to plain top-level generic functions** (`stdlib/complex/complex.cleave`'s `magnitude_sq`/`magnitude`, `stdlib/dynarray/dynarray.cleave`'s `push`/`get`/`set`/`len`, `stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s `Dense::forward`) — every existing call site (`v.magnitude()`, `arr.push(x)`, `net.l1.forward(x)`) kept its dot-call syntax unchanged, only the declaration form moved. Found and fixed four genuinely new bugs along the way, none anticipated by the initial plan, all surfaced by the real test suite: a `RawBuffer<T>`/`DynArray<T>` `get`/`set` naming collision once both lived in the same top-level name+arity resolution bucket (`RawBuffer`'s own internal-only primitives renamed to `raw_get`/`raw_set`); `Dense::forward` (single-row, plain `+`) turned out fully redundant with the already-existing `dense_forward` (batched, `broadcast0`-based) once inherent impls could no longer justify keeping both — deleted, call sites redirected to `.dense_forward(x)`; `Broadcast0<Row,Batch>` had an `adjoint` rule but no forward-mode `derivative` rule at all, a latent gap only exposed once `dense_forward` (routing bias through `broadcast0`) started reaching a `derive()`-differentiated context — fixed by adding `derivative broadcast0(r): broadcast0(d(r));`/`derivative reduce0(b): reduce0(d(b));`; and a naming collision between the migrated `Dense::forward` and pre-existing example-level top-level `fn forward(x, net)` functions in `digits-interop`/`xor.cleave`/`mnist-interop` (resolved by the `dense_forward` redundancy finding above, not by renaming around the collision).

**Correctness confirmed**: full `cleave` test suite green after adapting/removing tests that exercised the removed mechanism directly (several rewritten to cover the same observable behavior — dot-call still runs correctly, including chained mutual recursion and multi-site generic specialization — through plain top-level functions instead); `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` AOT accuracy unchanged (`0.94713414`/`0.9342`, matching the values already on record).


## An AOT binary built with `--no-openmp` (or `CodegenOptions::openmp = false`) genuinely crashed with a native stack overflow on any real training loop — a per-iteration native-stack leak, fixed by mirroring the arena's own existing region-scoping discipline

Raised directly, chasing a genuine mono-thread cleave-vs-PyTorch comparison (PyTorch run under `torch.set_num_threads(1)`, cleave built with OpenMP fully absent from the generated code, not just idle at runtime with 1 thread). Confirmed real and reproducible, isolated down to a minimal, MNIST-independent repro (~40 lines): a `Network` of four `Dense` layers at real MNIST widths, `grad(loss, net)`, and an ordinary `for s in 0..50 { ... }` training loop, compiled AOT with `--no-openmp` — crashed on the second iteration, `thread 'main' has overflowed its stack`.

**First hypothesis, tried and confirmed wrong by direct testing, not assumed to be right**: `pipeline.rs::lower_to_llvm`'s final lowering stage used to be two entirely separate, hand-duplicated pipelines (`if options.openmp { ... } else { ... }`) — the `else` branch's own doc comment asserted "no `memref.alloca_scope` complication to split around" on this path, which a direct `--dump-mlir-lowered` diff proved false (`14` occurrences of `alloca_scope`/`stacksave`/`stackrestore` present regardless of `options.openmp`, one-shot-bufferize inserts it unconditionally). Unified the two branches into one shared pipeline with only the two genuinely OpenMP-specific pieces (`--convert-scf-to-openmp`, `--convert-openmp-to-llvm`) inserted conditionally — a real fix in its own right (removes a false assumption and a structural duplication-drift hazard, `doc/backlog.md`'s own long-standing "three copies kept manually in sync" framing), kept permanently, but **did not fix the crash** — re-tested against the same repro after this change, identical failure, identical `alloca_scope` counts before and after.

**The real root cause, found by re-reading `--convert-scf-to-openmp`'s own already-existing doc comment precisely**: that pass doesn't merely emit `omp.parallel`/`omp.wsloop` — its own comment states directly "this pass wraps the loop body in a `memref.alloca_scope`", and, more importantly, the OpenMP lowering *outlines* the parallelized region into a genuinely separate LLVM function, called via `__kmpc_fork_call`. An ordinary function call's own stack frame is freed automatically on return, *regardless* of how many times its caller (the training loop) invokes it — that's what actually bounded stack growth in every `--openmp` build to date, as a side effect of parallelizing the matmul's own outer dimension, never because the training loop itself was ever protected. Without OpenMP, the identical loop body stays *inline* in the training-loop function, and nothing in this pipeline ever wrapped a plain `scf.while` loop body in any stack-bounding construct at all — every per-iteration local buffer (`llvm.alloca`, from bufferized local arrays/tensors) accumulated across iterations, real LLVM/C semantics for a dynamically-executed `alloca` inside a loop with no `stacksave`/`stackrestore` pairing around it.

**The fix, found and applied precisely, mirroring cleave's own existing arena-region discipline rather than inventing a second, differently-reasoned mechanism** (the direction explicitly asked for): `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop` already opens one heap-arena region per loop iteration (`cleave_region_enter`, at the body's start) and closes it right before that iteration's own tail-yield (`cleave_region_exit`) — `doc/hld.md`'s own "Memory management" section.
