Added the identical pairing for the *native* stack: `llvm.intr.stacksave`/`stackrestore` (real LLVM intrinsics, `LLVMIntrinsicOps.td`'s own `LLVM_StackSaveOp`/`StackRestoreOp`, no melior binding, built via `OperationBuilder` the same way every other unbound op in this codebase is), opened and closed around the same loop-iteration boundary.

**Placement, found the hard way — a real, instructive mistake, not guessed right the first time**: the first attempt built these two ops directly inside `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`, at the exact same point as `cleave_region_enter` — this put real `llvm`-dialect ops into the module *before* one-shot-bufferize's own `buffer-deallocation` stage ever ran, which requires every op it encounters to answer "what are your memory effects" and errored outright ("ops with unknown memory side effects are not supported") the moment it hit them. Fixed by moving the insertion to a post-hoc module walk in `pipeline.rs` (`insert_stack_scopes_in_loops`, structurally identical to the already-existing `stamp_op_and_children` walk used for FMA-fusion attribute-stamping) — found every `scf.while` op (cleave's own `for`/`while`, confirmed via `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`'s own `scf::r#while(..., before_region, after_region, ...)` call — region `1` is always the loop body), inserted `stacksave` as its body's first operation and `stackrestore` right before its terminator. Run once, right after `buffer-deallocation` and before `--affine-parallelize`/`--lower-affine` — early enough that `--convert-scf-to-cf` (later) never gets a chance to flatten the loop into a raw CFG first, though (unlike `memref.alloca_scope`) a flat stacksave/stackrestore pair doesn't structurally *need* this exact ordering to stay correct — no single-block-region constraint applies to either op, so nothing about later pass ordering can silently break this the way it broke the OpenMP-specific mechanism for the same underlying problem.

**Verified**: the original isolated repro, extended to 200 iterations (up from the 50 that first exposed the crash), runs to completion cleanly under `--no-openmp`, `exit: 0`. Full `cargo test -p cleave --release` clean, zero failures, all 25 test binaries. `examples/mnist-interop`, built with `CLEAVE_NO_OPENMP=1` (the toggle added while chasing this), a full 10-epoch run on a 3200-sample debug-capped training set: identical accuracy (`0.69125`) and elapsed time (`~2.6s`) to the equivalent `--openmp` capped run — correctness and performance both unaffected by the fix on the path that already worked, the previously-crashing path now simply also works.

**A second, structurally different crashing loop shape found the same day — via the user's own suggested isolation technique, not more disassembly.** Asked directly, after the fix above: shrink the real network to a single, minimal `Dense<784,10>` layer, same FFI/loop/`grad()`/`Optimizer::step` structure otherwise, to see what fixed/structural cost survives independent of network size. The minimal network's own AOT `--no-openmp` build overflowed the stack immediately — a second, genuinely different bug, not a sign the first fix was wrong (the real 4-layer network, already fixed and verified above, kept working throughout). Isolated via direct A/B on a from-scratch ~40-line repro (no MNIST): the *identical* shape widened from `N=10` to `N=512` (same `K=784`, same everything else) runs 500 training iterations clean; only the narrow output width crashes. Root-caused via `--dump-mlir-lowered`: real `llvm.alloca ... !llvm.array<8 x vector<10xf32>>` sites — `vector<10xf32>`, this shape's own narrow output width exactly — sitting inside a loop with no stacksave/stackrestore of its own. This is `transform.structured.pad`'s own narrow-output-width retry path (`doc/backlog-done.md`'s own "Problem B" entries, elsewhere in this file) — a real `scf.for` loop from the matmul tiling schedule, structurally unrelated to the `scf.while` loops `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop` builds, so the first fix (which only matched `"scf.while"` by name) never had a chance to see it.

**Before shipping a second reactive patch, audited the whole pipeline for every loop shape it can produce, rather than fixing this one crash and waiting for a third** — the user's own explicit direction, after two structurally different crashes in one day: "voir déjà si on a bien une solution générale et pas plusieurs verrues qui risque de se répéter." Traced every loop-producing/loop-consuming pass in `lower_to_llvm` by line order:

- `scf.while` (`mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`, cleave's own `for`/`while`) — present from initial MLIR construction, survives unchanged to any later point.
- `scf.for` (the transform-dialect matmul schedule's own `tile_using_for`, including the pad-retry path above) — created before `one-shot-bufferize` even runs, also survives essentially unchanged in loop-shape through bufferization.
- `scf.parallel` — created from **two independent sources**, both only reaching this exact shape *after* the point the first fix originally ran: `--scf-forall-to-parallel` (converts Stage 1's own `tile_using_forall`, right after `one-shot-bufferize`) and the *second* `--lower-affine` call (converts whatever `affine.parallel` the *old*, non-transform-dialect fallback path — `--convert-linalg-to-affine-loops`, for any matmul shape the schedule's own `vectorize` step declined — produced via `--affine-parallelize`). **Neither of these exists in loop form yet at the original insertion point** (right after `buffer-deallocation`) — a real, found-by-audit blind spot for the *old* fallback path specifically, not yet exposed by any actual crash, but structurally identical to the two that already were.

**The general fix: one insertion point, not one per loop kind.** Moved the whole pass to run right after the *second* `--lower-affine` call instead — the one point in the entire pipeline where `scf.while`, every `scf.for` (both sources), and every `scf.parallel` (both sources) are *simultaneously* present, still fully structured (no multi-block CFG from `--convert-scf-to-cf` yet), and not yet consumed by anything OpenMP-specific (`--convert-scf-to-openmp`, immediately after, only runs `if options.openmp`). Extended the match to `"scf.while" | "scf.for" | "scf.parallel"` (the first two share one region index, `0`; `scf.while`'s own body is region `1`, per its own doc comment). One real risk checked directly rather than assumed safe: inserting `llvm.intr.stacksave`/`stackrestore` *inside* an `scf.parallel` body **before** `--convert-scf-to-openmp` runs (when `options.openmp` is on) could in principle confuse that pass's own region-outlining logic — tested directly on the real, unmodified 4-layer `mnist-interop` kernel with `--openmp` enabled: `test accuracy: 0.9342` (unchanged), `30.17s` (within the already-established range for this thread count) — no regression, the outlining tolerates the extra ops fine.

**Verified**: the narrow-`N` repro (both the original and the exact minimal single-`Dense`-layer network that first exposed it) now runs clean under `--no-openmp`. Full `cargo test -p cleave --release` clean, all 25 binaries. Both `digits-interop` (`0.94713414`) and the real 4-layer `mnist-interop`, tested both ways — `--openmp` (`0.9342`, `30.17s`, confirming the relocation didn't regress the already-working path) and `--no-openmp` (`0.9342`, `38.78s`, matching the already-established no-openmp baseline) — all correctness-identical, zero regressions either direction.
## `mut` carries no real semantic weight for *most* scalar/struct/tuple bindings — likely removable for those, but `DynArray`'s own mutate-in-place `push` turns out to be a genuine, permanent exception, not a stopgap — raised and then corrected while designing the struct-allocation-strategy entry below

Surfaced directly while working out why `DynArray<T>`'s own envelope (`{buf: RawBuf, len: i32, cap: i32}`) seemed to need heap identity despite being exactly as small/pointer-shaped as `Dense` (see the entry below): the real reason wasn't the envelope's own size, it was `push`'s own API convention — mutates its `mut v: DynArray<T>` parameter in place, returns nothing, relies on the caller's binding already being a heap pointer under today's implementation for the mutation to be observed at all.

**A two-category model was proposed, tried, and found genuinely unsound — the attempt and why it fails matter as much as the conclusion.** The idea: **storage types** (`Array`, `Tensor`, `RawBuf` — anything reached through *indexed* access) are mutable by nature, no annotation needed; **value types** (scalars, structs, tuples — everything else) are never mutated in place, so a `mut` binding's own "reassignment" is semantically indistinguishable from plain shadowing (already compiles to pure SSA/phi-carried rebinding under the hood, confirmed via this session's own hand-written MLIR probes). Under this model, `DynArray`'s own envelope looked like an ordinary "value type" (small, pointer-shaped) that just needed `push` migrated to value-return style (`v = v.push(x)`, matching `Optimizer::step`'s own convention) to drop its last mutate-in-place case.

**Implemented directly, then caught by the user before landing, via a precise, concrete counter-example — a real aliasing hazard, not a style objection.** `push`'s own body, even rewritten to return a fresh envelope, still writes the new element *through the existing, shared `buf` pointer* in the common (no-resize) case: `raw_set(v.buf, v.len, x); DynArray(buf: v.buf, len: v.len+1, cap: v.cap)`. Given `let v1 = v0.push(x); let v2 = v0.push(y);` (both reading the *same*, unreassigned `v0`) — both writes land in the *identical* slot (`v0.buf[v0.len]`), and `v1`/`v2` end up as two silently-divergent envelopes sharing corrupted storage, each believing it owns the next free slot. Exactly the classic C "fat pointer copied by value" hazard (`struct { char* data; size_t len, cap; }`, trivially copyable, silently aliased). Cleave has no move/ownership/borrow-checking system (deliberately, per this whole project's own direction) to make `v0.push(x)` *consume* `v0` the way Rust's own `Vec::push(&mut self, ...)`/move semantics prevents this exact bug — so nothing catches a stale-binding reuse at all.

**Root cause, precisely: `DynArray` has no safe value-semantics representation at all, not just an inconvenient one.** The only two implementations of "copy the envelope" are: share the `buf` pointer (cheap, but exactly this aliasing hazard) or deep-copy the backing buffer on every envelope copy (safe, but destroys the entire point of an efficiently-growable, in-place-mutated collection). Unlike `Dense`/`Network`/tuples — which have *no* shared backing storage at all to alias, so two independent copies can never diverge dangerously — `DynArray` is inherently a single-owner, shared-mutable resource. Reverted immediately, cleanly (`git checkout --` on all five touched files, confirmed via `git status`/`git diff` — zero trace left): `push` stays exactly as it was, mutating its `mut v: DynArray<T>` parameter in place, relying on `DynArray` staying a stable, heap-boxed, single-identity value.

**The corrected model**: `mut` remains genuinely load-bearing for *two* structurally different reasons, not one — array/`Tensor`/`RawBuf` indexed-element assignment (as originally reasoned), **and** any struct type built around a single-owner, in-place-mutated shared resource (`DynArray`, confirmed the only real instance in the whole codebase, `doc/backlog.md`'s own struct-allocation-strategy entry below has the full survey) — the latter needing `mut`-parameter mutation specifically *because* cleave has no ownership system to make a safe value-return alternative exist at all. `mut` on an *ordinary* scalar/struct/tuple binding (never wrapping a `DynArray`, transitively) still reduces to pure shadowing, that part of the original reasoning holds — but "remove `mut` as a language concept, keep it only for arrays" was wrong as stated; the real boundary is narrower and shaped by this ownership gap, not by storage-vs-value alone. Not attempted further — the removal idea itself is now correctly understood to be more constrained than first thought, not abandoned outright for the cases where it *does* hold.

## A struct's own allocation strategy is hardcoded to "always heap, always refcount" regardless of shape — real hardware-profiled evidence it dominates the remaining single-thread gap, a three-axis design worked out and empirically validated; axis 1 (slot vs. pointer), including field-granularity release tracking, is now implemented, test-verified, and measured on the real kernel; axes 2/3's own embedded-array-threshold optimization is not

Raised chasing the `--no-openmp`-vs-PyTorch single-thread gap (`2.3×`, `doc/backlog.md`'s own thread-count-sweep entry) once the known matmul-codegen levers (FMA fusion, register-resident accumulation, the `128->10` padding fix, `linalg.matmul_transpose_a`/`_b`) were already exhausted — the question became "where do the remaining cycles actually go," answered with real hardware counters (AMD uProf, `collect --config hotspots -g`), not another guess.

**The profiler-confirmed finding, on the real `mnist-interop` kernel, `--no-openmp`, a 32,000-sample capped run**: `train_and_evaluate` itself (the one giant `--inline`d function, real FMA compute included) is only `50.6%` of process CPU time. The rest — `memrefCopy` (`7.2%`), the CRT/NT heap allocator chain (`vcruntime140.dll`'s own internal allocator wrapper, resolved via the DLL's own export table + image base to sit ~2KB past `__NLG_Return2`, i.e. an unexported helper between `std::alloc::alloc` and the real syscall; `ntdll.dll:RtlAllocateHeap`/`RtlFreeHeap`, resolved the identical way, confirmed *directly inside* those two functions, `9.3%`), and the arena's own `cleave_region_enter`/`cleave_region_exit` bookkeeping (`10.7%` combined) — is pure memory/allocation churn, zero floating-point work.

**Confirmed causally, not just correlated, by the user's own suggested experiment**: rebuilt the identical training loop with the network reduced to a single minimal `Dense<784,10>` layer (same FFI data loading, same `grad()`/`Optimizer::step` structure otherwise) — total CPU time barely moved (`11.85s` -> `12.83s`) despite the real compute (`train_and_evaluate`) collapsing from `50.6%` to `6.3%`. `ntdll.dll` (the heap allocator) went from `1.70s` to `6.13s` **in absolute terms**, becoming the single hottest module, ahead of the executable itself. The fixed, per-batch allocation/copy/bookkeeping cost is essentially independent of network size — on the real 4-layer network it was already comparable to the entire matmul workload; shrink the compute and it dominates completely.

**Root-caused, via static disassembly of the real object's own relocations (`llvm-objdump -r`, matching each `cleave_alloc_rc` call site to its own size argument)**: of 70 static `cleave_alloc_rc` (heap) call sites in the compiled kernel, the majority (11×112 bytes, 10×16 bytes, 4×104 bytes, ...) are far too small to be tensor payloads — they're the wrapper structs `Dense`/`Network`/`NetworkState`/`DenseState` and the tuples `Optimizer::step` returns, each just a handful of pointer fields, individually heap-allocated (`malloc` + refcount header) purely because `unify_alloc.rs`'s own policy heap-boxes *every* struct uniformly, regardless of shape.

**Two full days earlier, established while root-causing the AOT `--no-openmp` stack-overflow bug (`doc/backlog-done.md`, same session): `region_analysis.rs`'s own arena promotion (axis B below) structurally can never help this specific allocation class.** The wrapper structs are built inside `Optimizer::step`, which is *by definition* not region-local (its own output becomes `net`/`state` for the *next* iteration — exactly the condition the arena's own "can't free one object out of order" limit (`doc/hld.md`) correctly refuses to promote). Extending `region_analysis.rs`'s own reach further would never reclassify this — the wrapper struct's escaping nature is precisely what that analysis is designed to say no to. A genuinely different mechanism is needed, not a deeper version of the existing one.

**The design, worked out directly with the user and empirically validated at every step, not just reasoned about — three real, orthogonal axes, not one:**

1. **Slot vs. pointer (new axis)** — a struct is "light" (no heap identity at all — a genuine LLVM aggregate SSA value, threaded through a loop's own carried state exactly like a scalar accumulator already is via `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`'s `scf.while`) *iff* every field, after the rule in (2) below, is a scalar or a pointer. Cleave has no aliasing/reference model at all (already established, `stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s own `Init<T>` doc comment) — nothing ever observes two bindings of "the same" struct diverging, so there's no correctness reason every struct must have a stable heap address. `Dense`/`Network`/`NetworkState`/the `Optimizer::step` tuple all qualify unconditionally, at any layer count `N` (a struct of pointers is cheap regardless of how many, `N` is a compile-time constant either way) — validated directly, not assumed: a hand-written MLIR probe threading a 2-pointer struct through a 1,000-iteration `scf.while`, lowered all the way to real x86-64 (`mlir-opt` -> `mlir-translate` -> `llc -O2`), compiles to a *fixed*, one-time `push`/`sub rsp, 0x20` prologue and a pure register swap (`rdi`/`rsi`) inside the loop — zero calls to any allocator. Stress-tested at `N=64` fields (transformer-scale, several attention/FFN weight tensors per layer): still zero heap, a *single* one-time `sub rsp, 0x2c8` (712 bytes) for the fields that spill past available GPRs, a confirmed genuine loop (backward `jle`, not unrolled).

2. **Inline vs. indirected, for arrays specifically (the C-style pitfall, caught directly by the user)** — "bounded" (known size at compile time) is *not* sufficient to justify real stack space, any more than `int buf[1_000_000_000];` is safe in C just because its size is a compile-time constant; a struct field's own *direct* footprint matters, not just whether it's bounded. A pointer field is always cheap regardless of what it references (the pointee's own allocation is a completely separate question — axis 3, below); an *embedded* array field carries its own real `N × sizeof(T)` weight and needs a real, conservative, register/tiny-stack-spill-scale cutoff — past it, the array is converted to a pointer to a separately-allocated buffer (exactly how `Dense`'s own `w`/`b` `Tensor` fields already work today, confirmed directly: `w1`'s own `cleave_alloc_rc` call carries its *own*, separate, full tensor byte size — `0x188000` = `784×512×4` — never folded into `Dense`'s own tiny 16-byte allocation). This is what keeps axis 1 from ever reintroducing the C pitfall through the back door.

   Also validated directly, both directions of the realistic access pattern, not just the trivial swap case: a 64-`f32`-element array embedded alongside 2 pointers, genuinely initialized (not `undef` — ruled out a first, misleading probe where LLVM was silently inventing unconstrained values), updated at a **fixed** index every iteration — LLVM's own standard optimizer recognizes the repeated write as dead until the loop's last iteration and defers materialization entirely to the loop exit, `O(1)` per iteration, no cleave-side mechanism needed. Updated at a **dynamic** index (the realistic case, `arr[i % 64] = ...`) — `llvm.insertvalue` structurally cannot take a non-constant index at all, so LLVM correctly falls back to a real `alloca` + `getelementptr` + indexed `store` — one genuine store per iteration (necessary, not wasted), still against a *single*, one-time, bounded stack allocation (`sub rsp, 0x138`, never growing with iteration count), with the final struct-to-return-value copy itself auto-vectorized (`zmm`/`ymm` bulk moves). Both patterns: zero heap, one fixed allocation regardless of loop trip count.

3. **Bounded-but-large still never reaches the real call stack — arena or heap, never raw `llvm.alloca`, no matter how "bounded" it technically is.** The same C-style pitfall axis 2 closes for *embedded* arrays reappears one level up if a *large-but-bounded* buffer were ever allowed to claim "it's bounded, so `alloca` is fine" directly (exactly the class of bug the `--no-openmp` native-stack-leak fix, `doc/backlog-done.md`, already required real work to close, for per-loop-iteration allocation specifically). The resolution is structural, not a size heuristic to tune: once axis 2 has forced anything past the tiny inline threshold into a pointer, that pointer's own *target* allocation is *always* routed to the pre-existing axis B (`region_analysis.rs`'s arena when the construction site is provably region-local, real heap+refcount otherwise) — it never competes for axis-1 stack space at all. `Init<Tensor<T,In,Out>>::xavier`/`he`'s own `[T;In,Out]` scratch buffer (`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`) already does exactly this today, via `mlir::memref::alloc()`, not `alloca` — consistent with this model already, not a new case to handle.

**Net result originally claimed for the three axes together — corrected below, this turned out wrong for the actual motivating structs**: `Dense`/`Network`/`NetworkState`/tuples move from "always heap+refcount" to "always a value, zero allocation of any kind" — eliminating the majority of the 70 static `cleave_alloc_rc` sites found on the real kernel. The genuinely large, genuinely escaping tensors (`w1..w4`, `net`/`state`'s own real weights) are completely unaffected, correctly staying on the existing refcount path (axis B already says "not region-local" for them, correctly, since they must survive past every iteration).

**Axis 1 is now implemented, in `mlir_lower.rs`, not `unify_alloc.rs`** — `is_light_struct`/`is_light_struct_rec`/`is_light_field_ty` (recursive, whole-program-informed, cycle-guarded via a `visiting` set) classify every struct name at every field-type site; `ty_to_mlir` picks a bare `!llvm.struct<(...)>` value instead of the usual opaque `!llvm.ptr` for a "light" one, and `lower_struct_construct`/`lower_field_access` gained parallel `llvm.mlir.undef`+`insertvalue`/`extractvalue`-based paths that never touch `alloc_llvm_value`/`cleave_alloc_rc` at all. **Five structural disqualifiers found necessary, four of them only by direct testing (real `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` crashes, MLIR-verifier failures, or an outright memory leak on a real training run — not one of them reasoned out in advance)**, each its own whole-program scan mirroring `refcount.rs`'s established `collect_constructed_struct_names` shape, except the last (a local, non-recursive re-check, deliberately *not* a call to `refcount::is_refcounted` itself — see below):
- A field that's array/tensor/vector-shaped (axis 2 isn't implemented yet — see below) — checked structurally, no scan needed.
- `DynArray` itself, by name — the mutate-in-place aliasing hazard the `mut`-removal entry above already covers in full. (No longer needs its own separate transitive check — see the fifth disqualifier below, which subsumes it: `DynArray` is always genuinely refcounted the moment it's constructed anywhere.)
- Any struct ever the target of a real `s.field = v;` (`refcount::collect_field_mutated_struct_names`) — `lower_field_store`'s own GEP-based mutation needs a stable address a light aggregate value structurally doesn't have. Caught by `tests/user_guide.rs::struct_field_mutation`'s own MLIR-verifier failure the moment the classifier stopped being a single-hardcoded-name proof-of-concept.
- Any struct ever named as an `extern fn`'s own parameter or return type (`refcount::collect_extern_boundary_struct_names`) — a real C-ABI symbol (`cleave-rt`) is written assuming cleave's fixed, uniform pointer-shaped struct representation regardless of field shape; `dynarray.cleave`'s own `RawBuf {}` (a zero-field struct, only ever produced by an `extern fn`, hence vacuously "light" under a naive empty-`all()` check) and the generic `RawBuffer<S: HeapStruct>`'s `_ptr`-suffixed impls (any concrete struct `S` used as a `DynArray<S>` element) both hit this directly — caught via a genuine `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` crash (`RawBuf`) and a `'func.call' op operand type mismatch` MLIR-verifier error (`DynArray<Point>` vs. `DynArray<Pair>` in the same program) the first time real stdlib code, not just a hand-written proof-of-concept struct, went through the classifier.
- **A fifth disqualifier — any struct with a genuinely-refcounted nested field — existed temporarily, then was removed and replaced with real field-granularity release tracking.** Found the hard way, against the real `mnist-interop` training loop, not a test or a probe: a severe, unbounded memory leak, every training iteration — `retain_targets` already retains every refcounted field argument of a `PrimOp::Struct` construction unconditionally, keyed on the field's own type, completely independent of whether the *containing* struct is itself refcounted; once the container is "light," nothing ever schedules a matching release for that extra retain, since the CPS-level refcounting pass had no notion of "end of life" for a bare aggregate value, only for a heap pointer with a real, trackable owning scope. First fixed by blanket-excluding any such struct from "light" at all (the fifth disqualifier) — closing the leak, but at the cost of `Network`/`NetworkState`/`Dense`/the `Optimizer::step` tuple, exactly the structs this whole mechanism was built for, never actually becoming light.

**Replaced with the real fix, directly in `refcount.rs`'s own core insertion algorithm (`insert_refcounting_fn`/`rewrite_body`), not a separate optimization layered on top** — following directly from the same "CPS already reifies every future use as an explicit argument, so ownership is a local question" discussion that produced the Tier 1 pair-cancellation pass above. `mlir_lower.rs` gained `LightLeafPath`/`light_struct_release_leaves` — a recursive walk (mirroring `is_light_struct_rec`'s own shape) finding every genuinely-refcounted field reachable from a light struct's own top level, transitively through further light fields, each as a `[(struct type, field name), ...]` hop sequence ending in the leaf's own type. `refcount.rs` threads this through three places `ctx.is_rc(ty)` used to gate alone, now `ctx.is_rc(ty) || !ctx.light_release_leaves(ty).is_empty()`: the ordinary owned-seeding condition, and — found only by hitting real, further bugs, not anticipated up front — the `Fix`-local continuation's own seeding (a loop's own carried `net`/`state` is exactly this shape) and `retain_targets` itself (embedding an *existing* light-with-leaves value into a fresh container, e.g. `Optimizer::step`'s own real `(Network, NetworkState)` return tuple, needs each of its own leaves retained, not the whole value). Release/retain emission for a light-with-leaves value (`wrap_releases`, `field_read_retain`, `retain_targets`'s own wrapping) now builds a real `PrimOp::Field` chain down to each leaf, terminating in an ordinary `Retain`/`Release` on it, via a small shared `build_leaf_chain` helper — reusing the exact same `PrimOp::Field`/`PrimOp::Retain`/`PrimOp::Release` primitives and their existing, already-correct MLIR lowering, no new primitive or lowering code needed at all.

**Two further real bugs found only by running the actual `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` binaries, not caught by the full test suite** (a `STATUS_HEAP_CORRUPTION` on the very first run after removing the fifth disqualifier): (1) the `Fix`-local seeding gate above, missed on the first pass — a loop's own carried light-with-leaves state was silently never tracked, leaking its leaves every iteration; (2) `retain_targets`'s own gate, also missed initially — embedding an existing light-with-leaves value (not a fresh field-read) into a new container retained nothing, so the tuple case above corrupted memory the moment the caller's own `net`/`state` bindings were later (correctly) released, since nothing had protected the tuple's own copy of their leaves. Both are the exact same class of oversight as the original fifth-disqualifier leak — every `ctx.is_rc(ty)` gate in this file needed its own light-with-leaves counterpart, found one at a time by testing against the real, full-scale programs, not derivable by inspection alone.

**Consequence, corrected and now precisely measured, not estimated**: `Network`/`NetworkState`/the `Optimizer::step` tuple **do become light now** (each is built entirely from light/heavy-pointer fields once `Dense`/`DenseState` are excluded only for their own direct `Tensor` fields — axis 2, still not implemented). `Dense`/`DenseState` themselves still don't (their own fields are directly `Tensor`-tagged, the separate, still-real axis-2 gap documented below) — matching the honest scope this design always had once axis 2 was factored out.

**Measured directly, `--dump-mlir-lowered` on the real `mnist-interop` kernel, textual `call @cleave_alloc_rc` occurrences before any inlining collapses duplicate call sites (the same controlled comparison established earlier in this entry) — three points on the same axis, not just two**: `208` (pre-axis-1 baseline, commit `8924672`) `-> 207` (axis-1 with the fifth disqualifier — only `Sgd`) `-> 202` (axis-1 with field-granularity release tracking replacing it). Five more construction sites disappear once `Network`/`NetworkState`/the `Optimizer::step` tuple actually qualify as light — a real, if still modest relative to the original "majority of 70 sites" ambition, measured win, not the zero this entry's own fifth disqualifier had left it at. `call @cleave_retain`/`call @cleave_release` counts stay at `8`/`16` either way — the same logical retains/releases still happen, now distributed across individual leaves via `PrimOp::Field` chains instead of whole-struct pointers for the newly-light containers, not eliminated (that's Tier 1's own job, above, and it already found nothing further to cancel here).

Verified: full `cargo test -p cleave --release` suite green throughout, including two dedicated Tier 1 tests (above) and the pre-existing struct-allocation tests updated for the light-struct behavior change (not weakened — each got a genuinely heavy variant added alongside a new light-specific test). `digits-interop` re-run end to end after every step in this whole entry, accuracy unchanged at `0.94713414` — including the run that first caught the two field-granularity bugs above via a real `STATUS_HEAP_CORRUPTION` crash, and the clean run after fixing them. `mnist-interop`'s own real training loop re-run in full (10 epochs) after the field-granularity fix specifically: `test accuracy: 0.9342`, bit-identical to the established baseline, no crash — the real, full-scale confirmation this whole mechanism needed, not just the small-scale `digits-interop` one.

**A tensor field has the *exact same* unsolved release-tracking gap as a refcounted struct field — currently avoided only by accident, not by design, and axis 2 (below) must not be attempted without closing it first.** Raised directly by the user, verified precisely, not assumed: a `#[mlir_type(tensor)]` field's own payload *is* allocated via `cleave_alloc_rc` (`store_native_shape_field`), a real `RcHeader` exactly like any struct — `is_refcounted(Tensor)` only says `false` because a tensor's own aliasing story is simpler (copied fresh on every store, never independently shared), so the general CPS-level Retain/Release pass doesn't need to track a *bare* tensor variable. But its actual liberation has exactly one path in the entire codebase: `lower_release_cascade`'s own tensor-field branch, reached *only* when the tensor's containing struct's own `cleave_release` call reports `freed` (`mlir_lower.rs:3400-3414`) — piggybacked entirely on the container being a real, heap-allocated, `cleave_release`-calling struct. A "light" struct never makes that call (no pointer of its own to release) — so a tensor field embedded directly into one, the way axis 2 below casually proposed, would leak its payload unconditionally, on every single construction, with *nothing* in the current classifier (`field_struct_is_ever_refcounted` deliberately excludes tensor/vector-tagged names, matching `is_refcounted` itself) positioned to catch it. Today's separate "tensor/vector-tagged field — not yet implemented" exclusion in `is_light_field_ty` accidentally prevents this by blocking construction support entirely, not because anyone designed it as the safety mechanism — any future work on axis 2 must extend the *release*-tracking exclusion (the fifth disqualifier above), not just build the `insertvalue` machinery, or it reopens the identical `mnist-interop` leak on the single most expensive class of payload in the whole system.

**The tensor-leaf case (`Dense`/`DenseState` themselves) is now also implemented — see the dedicated entry below for the full design, a real bug found and fixed, and an empirical measurement.** Only the *embedded untagged array field* half of axis 2 (`[T;N]` declared straight on a struct, not a `Tensor`) remains genuinely not implemented — still correctly a hard disqualifier in `is_light_field_ty`, for the original, narrower reason (no `insertvalue`-based inline-array flattening built).

## Tier 3: `Dense`/`DenseState` themselves now become light — a tensor field embeds its own memref descriptor inline; a real extern-declaration bug found and fixed on the actual `mnist-interop` kernel; the design risk (losing `dps_rewrite`'s own optimization) was real, root-caused precisely, and fully recovered by two targeted `dps_rewrite` fixes, not by reverting `Dense`/`DenseState` back to heavy

Direct follow-on to the struct-allocation-strategy entry above, closing exactly the gap its own "a tensor field has the *exact same* unsolved release-tracking gap" paragraph named. Confirmed empirically before writing any code: `store_native_shape_field`/`load_native_shape_field` already build/consume a tensor field's own memref descriptor as a bare SSA aggregate *value* (`undef`+`insertvalue`, exactly the light-struct shape) — only their own final step (a real `llvm.store`/`llvm.load` against a heavy struct's own field pointer) is heavy-specific, refactored out into `build_tensor_descriptor_value`/`descriptor_value_to_tensor` and reused directly by `lower_light_struct_construct`/`lower_field_access`'s own new light-struct tensor branches. `struct_llvm_type`/`ty_to_llvm_field_type` needed **no changes at all** — they already size a tensor field's own position as the real descriptor type, for a light struct exactly as for a heavy one (confirmed by reading, not assumed).

**One real design fork, resolved by direct testing before committing to an implementation, not guessed**: releasing/retaining a tensor *leaf* reached through a light struct's own field-chain (`refcount.rs::build_leaf_chain`) could either (a) reuse the exact same `PrimOp::Field`+`Retain`/`Release` primitives the struct-leaf case already uses, materializing a real `tensor<...>` value along the way, or (b) build a dedicated, materialization-free path mirroring `lower_release_cascade`'s own existing tensor-field branch directly. Option (a)'s own apparent cost — a `to_tensor` then immediately `to_buffer` round-trip just to get a pointer back — was tested directly on this exact toolchain (`mlir-opt --canonicalize --cse` on a hand-written probe) before choosing: `bufferization.to_buffer(bufferization.to_tensor(x, restrict))` folds to exactly `x`, a real, standard canonicalization, not a hoped-for one. Went with (a) — CPS stays uniform, no new primitive.

**A real bug found only by running the actual `mnist-interop` kernel through the real AOT pipeline, not caught by the full test suite or by hand-written probes**: `'func.call' op operand type mismatch: expected operand type 'tensor<1x10xf32>', but provided '!llvm.ptr' for operand number 0`. Root cause: `emit_cleave_retain`/`emit_cleave_release` declare `cleave_retain`/`cleave_release`'s own extern signature via `ensure_extern_declared(ctx, symbol, &[rc_ty], ...)`, which computes the declared parameter type via `ty_to_mlir(rc_ty)` — correct for an ordinary struct (any declared name maps to `!llvm.ptr` via `ty_to_mlir`'s generic fallback, so *which* struct is irrelevant to the C signature, `emit_cleave_release`'s own original doc comment already established this) but **wrong** for a tensor-tagged `rc_ty`, since `ty_to_mlir` intercepts a `#[mlir_type(tensor)]` name *before* that generic fallback ever runs, giving the native `tensor<...>` type instead. The actual call site already passed a real `!llvm.ptr` (correctly derived via `tensor_value_to_ptr`) — declaration and call site disagreed. Fixed with `declared_ptr_sig_ty`: substitutes a deliberately unregistered name (`"__cleave_opaque_ptr"`) for the declaration specifically when `rc_ty` is tensor-tagged, since `ty_to_mlir`'s own struct-schema miss already falls through to the identical `!llvm.ptr` fallback an ordinary struct gets — no special-casing needed beyond picking a name nothing else declares.

**A real design risk, raised directly by the user and investigated before writing more code, not dismissed**: `Boxed`/`Dense`-shaped structs (a single/couple of direct `Tensor` fields, nothing else disqualifying) now build via `insertvalue`, never the `llvm.getelementptr`-based field store `dps_rewrite::eliminate_redundant_field_store_copies` requires to fire (confirmed directly: that pass's own `op_name_is(size_gep, "llvm.getelementptr")` check) — `dps_rewrite` exists specifically because this exact memcpy was measured (VTune, that module's own doc comment) at **~38% of `mnist-interop`'s own real wall time**, so losing its own optimization for a newly-light `Dense` was a real, not hypothetical, regression risk, surfaced by a genuinely failing structural test (`dps_rewrite.rs::the_passthrough_case_retains_the_shared_pointer_and_neuters_its_own_copy`) before any wall-clock measurement was even attempted. Considered and set aside: eliminating the struct's own refcount header entirely ("Tier 4") does *not* solve this — the copy `dps_rewrite` eliminates exists to satisfy `--buffer-deallocation-pipeline`'s own ownership rules for the *tensor payload's* own buffer, not because of anything about the *container's* own header; a header-less `Dense` would still need the identical copy-or-destination-passing decision for `w`/`b`'s own buffers.

**Correctness measured directly**: real end-to-end runs of both `digits-interop` (`test accuracy: 0.94713414`, unchanged) and the full `mnist-interop` training loop (10 real epochs, `test accuracy: 0.9342`, bit-identical to the established baseline, no crash).

**Wall-clock: the assistant's own first `cargo run -p mnist-interop --release` timing (34.5s, against ~70.1s immediately after Tier 1/2) was wrong — not a measurement to trust, an apples-to-oranges comparison (thread-count/OpenMP environment not actually held constant between the two runs, per this project's own established "the assistant's own timings are noisier than the user's own" finding).** The user's own controlled re-run, `OMP_NUM_THREADS=4`, `--openmp` on, matching this backlog's own already-established "4 threads is the real optimum" reference point (the per-shape thread-count entry elsewhere in this file, `22.80s`): **26.2s — a real, ~15% regression**, not an improvement. The `dps_rewrite` conflict raised as a design risk before writing any code was real, not a false alarm.

**Root-caused precisely on the user's own request ("analyse exactement ce qui se passe pour éviter les surprises"), not left as a plausible-sounding guess**: a temporary counter inside `dps_rewrite::eliminate_redundant_field_store_copies` itself, comparing candidate counts on the identical real kernel, baseline vs. this entry — **16 candidates found and rewritten with `Dense` heavy, only 8 with `Dense` light — exactly half of `dps_rewrite`'s own optimization opportunities lost**, confirmed directly rather than inferred from struct shapes alone. Cross-checked structurally too: tracing one of the surviving, un-rewritten `Dense`-field `llvm.intr.memcpy`'s own size operand back to its defining op shows a real `llvm.ptrtoint` (an actual, non-zero byte count computed via the standard sizeof-via-GEP idiom) — not the `llvm.mlir.zero`-seeded chain `dps_rewrite`'s own neutering leaves behind. Real object-level relocation counts (`llvm-objdump -r` on the actual compiled `kernel.o`, not the pre-inline `--dump-mlir-lowered` count `insert_refcounting`-unaware and therefore untrustworthy for this specific comparison — see the measurement-methodology note below) confirm the trade precisely: `cleave_alloc_rc` sites *did* drop (`58 -> 49`, real wrapper-struct savings), but total instruction count and object size both grew slightly (`24382 -> 24588` instructions, `203749 -> 206081` bytes) — far too small a *static* change to explain a 15% *wall-clock* regression on its own, exactly as expected once the real cost is understood to be **dynamic**: `w1` alone is `784×512×4 = 1,605,632` bytes, now copied in full on every `Optimizer::step` construction of a fresh `Dense`, once per training sample, for every epoch — a real, recurring, per-iteration cost invisible in a static size count, not a one-time compile-time overhead.

**Conclusion (superseded by the fix below, kept for the record)**: the design risk raised before implementation was correct, the "2× faster" measurement that briefly suggested otherwise was an assistant-side measurement error (now corrected), and the real, user-confirmed, precisely-root-caused regression stood — `Dense`/`DenseState` needed a real decision (exclude from "light" again, or extend `dps_rewrite` to recognize the `insertvalue`-based light-struct construction shape too) before this entry could be considered a net win rather than a net loss on the one real workload this project measures itself against.

**The premise behind "extend `dps_rewrite`" turned out to be wrong in an important, narrower way, found by reading `dps_rewrite.rs::match_candidate` directly rather than re-deriving from the earlier "insertvalue has no address" framing**: the `llvm.getelementptr` `dps_rewrite` requires is *not* the destination struct field's own address — re-checked directly, `Candidate::dest_ptr` (`match_candidate`, `memcpy.operand(0)`) is always the bare result of `call @cleave_alloc_rc(...)`, never a GEP into any outer container. The GEP it actually requires (`size_gep`) is purely the classic sizeof-via-null-GEP idiom feeding the *allocation size*, identical in shape whether the destination is a heavy struct's own field storage or a light struct's freshly `alloc_llvm_value`'d tensor-descriptor buffer (`build_tensor_descriptor_value` reuses the exact same helper `store_native_shape_field`'s heavy path always used). The tensor *payload* was never insertvalue-built or address-free at all — only the small 5-word descriptor *around* it is; the payload buffer was already exactly as "in place" as the heavy case's field storage. No second, `insertvalue`-aware mechanism was needed.

**The real, narrow, mechanical cause, found the same way (reading the code, then confirming by instrumentation) rather than guessed**: `alloc_llvm_value` (`mlir_lower.rs`) picks between two allocators per construction site — `cleave_alloc_rc` (heap, refcounted) or `cleave_alloc_local` (a region/bump allocator, used whenever `region_analysis.rs` proves the enclosing function has exactly one call site, inside a loop, with a non-escaping result — a pre-existing, unrelated optimization). `match_candidate`'s own callee check accepted `@cleave_alloc_rc` only. A temporary per-candidate debug counter (`CLEAVE_DEBUG_DPS_CALLEE`, removed after use) confirmed this precisely on the real `mnist-interop` kernel: all 8 of the candidates lost between the pre-Tier-3 baseline (16) and Tier 3 as first landed (8) were rejected for exactly this one reason (`callee == @cleave_alloc_local`), zero for any other.

**Fixed**: `Candidate` gained an `is_local: bool` field; `match_candidate` accepts both callees (`cleave_alloc_local`'s own extra leading `handle` argument shifts the size operand to index 1, not 0); `build_fresh_alloc` reproduces the *same* allocator on the CSE-shared/slow path (not `cleave_alloc_rc` unconditionally, which would have silently defeated the region allocator for the rebuilt destination); the neuter-old-call tail zeros the correct operand index for the local case and, critically, **skips the `cleave_release` call entirely for a region-local allocation** — a region has no per-object refcount header at all (reclaimed in bulk when it closes), so releasing one would have been a real correctness bug (bump-allocator corruption), not a missed optimization. `cleave/src/dps_rewrite.rs`'s own doc comment on `Candidate::is_local` has the full reasoning.

**Re-measured the same way, with a second, more precise temporary probe (per-rejection-line debug tags, all removed after use)**: of the original 8 `cleave_alloc_local` candidates, **4 are now correctly rewritten** (previously 0). The remaining 4 initially looked like a real, structurally *different*, and *correct* reason to reject: their `linalg.generic`'s own `outs` argument is genuinely *read* inside the region body and is *not* seeded by `tensor.empty()` — read at the time as a real in-place accumulate (plausibly `DenseState`'s own momentum/gradient update), where redirecting the destination to freshly allocated, uninitialized struct storage would silently corrupt the computation with garbage instead of the prior accumulated value. **This turned out to be wrong, caught directly by the user pushing back on "definitively irrecoverable" ("j'ai du mal à comprendre... j'ai l'impression que c'est juste un pb de lowering") rather than accepted at face value** — a second, more targeted debug probe (dumping the enclosing function and the rejected `outs`'s own seed op) showed all 4 rejections are `train_and_evaluate`, seeded by **`linalg.fill`, not some other struct's own live data**. `linalg.fill`'s own `outs` is exactly as don't-care as `tensor.empty` (unconditionally overwritten with a constant — whatever was there before is irrelevant either way) — this is *exactly* the shape the `linalg.matmul`+`linalg.fill` branch right below already treats as safe, just never recognized when a `linalg.generic` (not the named `linalg.matmul` op) is the one reading it. Not a structural, permanent limit of the technique at all — a real, narrow matcher-coverage gap, fixed by adding the identical `linalg.fill`-redirect handling already used for `linalg.matmul` to the `linalg.generic` branch too. **Net, re-measured after this second fix: `16` of the original `16` candidates now match — full recovery, not `12`.**

**Verified after the first (`is_local`) fix alone (12/16)**: full `cargo test -p cleave --release --no-fail-fast` suite green; `cleave/tests/dps_rewrite.rs`'s own 8 tests green unchanged; `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` both correct. Wall-clock at that point (`OMP_NUM_THREADS=4`): assistant's own check `25.16s`, roughly flat against the confirmed-regressed `26.2s`.

**The user's own follow-up re-run (still against the 12/16 state) showed a regression across every mode tested** (default/16-thread `38.75s`, 8-thread `26.79s`, 4-thread `28.82s`, `--no-openmp` `42.29s`) — prompting a direct request to root-cause precisely before doing anything further. Investigated via the same `git stash`-isolated A/B technique already established this session, this time on wall-clock rather than candidate count: no-openmp and 4-thread both showed *no* regression attributable to the `is_local` fix itself (before/after runs statistically indistinguishable), a real hypothesis about `cleave-rt`'s own region allocator (`ARENA_BASE`/`ARENA_CURSOR`/`REGION_DEPTH`, documented **single-threaded by design**) being newly contended by a redirected *parallel* write was raised and checked, and ruled out as the dominant effect by that same A/B. The assistant's own 8-thread/default numbers landed close to the user's; only 4-thread diverged, tentatively attributed to run-to-run noise (four heavy training runs launched back to back, no cooldown) rather than a further code regression — flagged as not independently confirmed.

**That whole investigation is superseded by the second fix above (`linalg.fill`, 16/16)**, found directly from the user pushing back on "definitively irrecoverable" rather than accepting it — the 4 candidates the first round of investigation treated as a real, permanent residual cost were in fact a plain matcher gap, now closed. Re-verified after the second fix: full suite green again, `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` both still correct (`0.94713414`/`0.9342`, bit-identical, no crash) across every rebuild during this second round.

**Wall-clock for the 16/16 state is not yet cleanly measured — explicitly flagged, not glossed over**: repeated timing attempts on the assistant's own machine during this second round swung wildly (`~25s` one run, `~40-51s` shortly after on *identical, unchanged* builds, including a direct back-to-back A/B where a rebuild with the `linalg.fill` branch literally disabled measured the *same* elevated time as the branch enabled) — traced to an unrelated interfering process on the assistant's own machine during this session (the user's own words: "je joue à un jeu, qui interfère en perf"), not a code effect. **No wall-clock conclusion is drawn from any of this session's assistant-side timing for the final, 16/16 state** — per this project's own established practice, that number needs a clean re-run, on a quiet machine, to mean anything.

**Wall-clock, measured cleanly by the user directly (their own machine, their own timing — the authoritative measurement this project has always deferred to)**: `--no-openmp` (serial) `40.18s`/`38.93s` — close to, though not identical to, the pre-Tier-3 `37.86s` reference (a small, `~3-6%` residual, within the same order as the ~3% spread between the user's own two back-to-back runs, not chased further here). **`OMP_NUM_THREADS=4`: `22.04s`** — not merely a recovery of the pre-Tier-3 reference points (`22.80s`/`22.49s`), *better than both*.

**Conclusion (current)**: `Dense`/`DenseState` stay light — no revert was needed, and this is now a confirmed, measured net win, not just a neutralized regression. The `dps_rewrite` conflict was real, and fully closed by two small, well-scoped fixes (`cleave_alloc_local` support, then `linalg.fill`-seeded `linalg.generic` support) rather than a second, `insertvalue`-aware mechanism — **16 of 16 original candidates now match**, not 12 — and the user's own clean 4-thread measurement confirms the fix closes the wall-clock gap this whole entry's own design risk raised, with room to spare. The small residual serial-mode gap (`~3-6%` against `37.86s`) is not yet explained and not yet worth chasing further given its size relative to run-to-run noise already observed on the very same measurement.

**Fixed alongside**: `dps_rewrite.rs::the_passthrough_case_retains_the_shared_pointer_and_neuters_its_own_copy` — `Boxed` forced back onto the heavy path via a real field mutation on a separate, throwaway binding (the same pattern used repeatedly elsewhere this session), so the rewrite's own optimization stays covered by a real test for the case it actually targets, without blocking `Dense` from taking the (measured faster) light path.

**A real measurement-methodology correction, found while investigating the `dps_rewrite` conflict, worth recording precisely**: `--dump-mlir-lowered` (used for every `cleave_alloc_rc`/`cleave_retain`/`cleave_release` count in this whole struct-allocation-strategy entry and Tier 1's own entry below) **never calls `refcount::insert_refcounting` at all** (confirmed by reading `main.rs`'s own `args.dump_mlir_lowered` block directly — unlike `args.dump_cps_optimized`/`args.run`, both of which do). Every retain/release *count* reported via that flag this session was therefore near-meaningless for judging refcounting behavior specifically (though allocation-site counts, driven by `PrimOp::Struct` construction alone, independent of refcounting, stayed valid) — real refcounting behavior needs `--run`/`--dump-cps-optimized`, or, most authoritatively, the real compiled object from `cleave-build`, as used for every correctness check in this entry.


Follow-on to the struct-allocation-strategy entry above, from a long discussion with the user working through the CPS≡SSA correspondence directly: `refcount.rs`'s own module doc comment already says its algorithm is deliberately *not* a liveness analysis — it releases a value at the latest safe point (a function's own return, or the last point it's still passed forward), not the earliest. Since CPS already reifies "everything that happens after this point" as an explicit continuation with an explicit argument list, computing whether a specific retained value is used again is a *local*, syntactic question (does it reappear in an argument list reachable from here) rather than a global dataflow problem — explored at length with the user, including an explicitly rejected alternative (representing structs as MLIR-bufferized `memref`s to reuse `OwnershipBasedBufferDeallocationPass`/`PromoteBuffersToStackPass` — rejected because `bufferization.clone`'s own documented contract, "may alias or copy, mutating either afterward is UB," is incompatible with `DynArray`/`mut`'s real shared-mutation semantics, confirmed against `BufferizationOps.td` directly, not assumed).

**Built: `cleave/src/rc_opt.rs`, `eliminate_redundant_retain_release`, run unconditionally as the final step inside `refcount::insert_refcounting` itself** (not at its three separate call sites, so `--dump-cps-optimized`/`--run`/the real AOT pipeline all benefit automatically) — exactly the Swift/Objective-C ARC-optimizer shape: the naive baseline stays the untouched, always-correct source of truth; this pass only *removes* a `Retain`/`Release` pair it can *prove* is a no-op (the retained value is never read again on *any* reachable path, and an actual, textually-present matching `Release` for the same `CVar` is found and removed alongside it — never assumed). Deliberately conservative: the forward scan stops at any `Fix` it can't specifically recognize as an `if`'s own join shape (`Fix { defs: [join], body: If { .. } }` — recursing into both branches independently, matching each branch's own explicit argument list to the join, no dynamic merge needed) — a loop's own back-edge or a real call's own resumption is a hard stop, not attempted in this first version.

**Two real bugs found and fixed while building it, both in the new pass itself, not the baseline**: (1) a `PrimOp::Field` read *through* the retained value (e.g. `o.x.a`, reading `.a` off a field-read result) was wrongly treated as a disqualifying second use — it's transparent (one more dereference of the same about-to-die pointer, no new reference created), fixed by exempting exactly this shape; (2) the dedicated test asserting the pass never breaks the DynArray-push aliasing case failed twice in a row, in a way that led to two real, separate, pre-existing findings (below), not a bug in the new pass at all.

**Verified**: full `cargo test -p cleave --release` suite green; two dedicated tests added (`cleave/tests/refcount.rs`) — one confirms a provably-redundant pair (a struct field embedded and never used again) is fully eliminated (both the retain and its matching release), one confirms a retain protecting a value with no possible matching release (a field read off a borrowed function parameter) is never touched. `digits-interop` re-run end to end: accuracy unchanged (`0.94713414`).

**Measured on the real `mnist-interop` kernel (`--dump-mlir-lowered`, counting `call @cleave_retain`/`call @cleave_release`, `git`-free A/B via a one-line temporary bypass of the new pass's own call site): `8` retains / `16` releases, identical with the pass on or off — zero pairs eliminated.** Root-caused, not just observed: every retain in the real kernel protects `Optimizer::step`'s own returned `(Network, NetworkState)` tuple, unpacked and immediately re-threaded as the training loop's own carried state (`loop$313 v1789 v1787 v1788`) — a genuine, real second use crossing exactly the loop-back-edge boundary this first version's own forward scan refuses to cross by design. Not a wasted exercise — the mechanism is real, tested, and safe — but this specific, conservative scope has no measured payoff on the one real workload profiled so far; extending the scan across a loop's own back-edge (a real, bounded fixpoint — matching classic liveness analysis on a CFG with back-edges) is the concrete next step if this axis is pursued further, not yet attempted.

**Two separate, more serious, pre-existing bugs found while building the DynArray-safety test — neither caused by this pass, both confirmed present with it fully bypassed, both out of this Tier 1 work's own scope, not fixed here**:
1. **`DynArray::push` never gets a protecting retain at all.** `push`'s own body calls `raw_set`, a real `extern fn` (`dynarray_set_ptr`) — `refcount.rs::rewrite_body`'s own `retain_targets` match only recognizes `PrimOp::Struct`/`Array`/`FieldStore`/`Store`, never `PrimOp::Extern`, so embedding an existing struct into a `DynArray` via `push` inserts *no* retain whatsoever. The caller's own local binding for the pushed value still gets released normally once it's no longer used in the caller — freeing memory the `DynArray`'s own buffer still (now silently) points at. Confirmed directly: `--dump-cps-optimized` on a minimal `arr.push(p)` shows `release p` in the caller with zero `Retain` anywhere in the whole program, reproduced identically with the new Tier 1 pass completely bypassed.
2. **A top-level function's own *parameters* are invisible to `retain_targets` entirely.** `refcount::collect_var_info` only walks a function's own *body* (`walk_var_info(&top.def.body, ...)`) — `top.def.params`/`top.param_types` are never consulted, so `var_types` has no entry for a bare parameter at all. `retain_targets`'s own `if let Some(rty) = ctx.var_types.get(cv)` check silently no-ops whenever the value being embedded (via `Struct`/`Array`/`FieldStore`/`Store`) is a bare parameter, not a `let`-bound local — a real, general, currently-silent memory-corruption risk for *any* "setter"-shaped function that embeds one of its own struct-typed parameters directly into a container it's handed, not specific to `DynArray` at all. (A value read *off* a borrowed parameter via `PrimOp::Field`, by contrast, is a `let`-bound local with a real `var_types` entry — unaffected, confirmed via the dedicated test that replaced the original, confounded `DynArray`-based one.)

Neither is fixed here — both need the user's own call on priority (a real, general fix for #2 likely also fixes #1's own real-world instances, since `push`'s pattern is exactly "borrowed parameter embedded into a container," modulo the `extern`-vs-`Store` gap layered on top).

**P1, not P0 — confirmed by direct measurement against the real `mnist-interop` kernel, not inferred from "the tests still pass."** Temporary instrumentation in `retain_targets`'s own loop (logging every retain-target `CVar` absent from `var_types`) found exactly **one** occurrence in the entire real, compiled kernel: `Sum::broadcast<Tensor<f32,32,10>,f32>`'s own `u: f32` parameter, embedded into an array literal — a plain scalar, `is_rc(f32)` is `false` regardless of whether its type were known, so this specific occurrence is harmless by construction, not just by luck. Neither `#1` (`DynArray`-of-structs) nor a struct-typed "setter"-shaped parameter (`#2`'s own general case) occurs anywhere in the real kernel today. Downgraded from an immediate-fix candidate to P1 backlog on this basis — real, worth fixing generally (any future stdlib/user code with either shape is still exposed), but not blocking or currently silently corrupting the one real workload this project measures itself against.

## Every loop iteration, at every nesting level, unconditionally opened and closed a region — VTune-confirmed real cost (`~4.7%` CPU time, `486` million calls on one real 10-epoch training run), separate from and pre-existing the Tier-3/`dps_rewrite` work above — fixed, call count down `~4,050×`

Raised while chasing the small (`~3-6%`) serial-mode residual gap left after Tier 3's own `dps_rewrite` fixes closed the main regression (entry above). An initial hypothesis (the `(Network, NetworkState)` tuple returned by `Optimizer::step` gets silently re-materialized via heap-alloc+memcpy) was investigated and **directly disproved**: a structural search of `--dump-mlir-lowered --opt-level 0` for any `getelementptr` typed against a *whole struct* (the unambiguous signature of a heavy-struct allocation) found exactly one match in the entire program — an unrelated `([i8;6], i32)` label tuple, not `Network`/`NetworkState`/`Dense`/`DenseState`. Retracted rather than left standing.

**Real profiling (`vtune -collect hotspots`, single-thread/`--no-openmp` build, machine load from the user's own concurrent, unrelated use explicitly accepted as fine for this — hotspot *attribution* is far more robust to background noise than a wall-clock stopwatch)** found the real breakdown: `train_and_evaluate` (actual compute) `55.2%`; an unresolved `VCRUNTIME140.dll` frame `14.9%` (almost certainly `memcpy`, matching this project's own established pattern for reading such frames); an unresolved `ntdll.dll` frame `6.9%` (plausibly the underlying Windows heap allocator); `memrefCopy` (a real, named MLIR bufferization runtime symbol) `5.1%`; and, surprisingly for a function that should be near-free (one atomic `fetch_add` plus two relaxed loads), **`cleave_region_enter` at `4.7%`**.

**Root-caused directly, not left as "that's odd"**: `cleave_region_enter` is called **unconditionally at the start of every `for`/`while`/`loop` iteration cleave ever lowers, at every nesting level** (`mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop`, right before the iteration's own body) — regardless of whether anything inside that specific iteration ever actually calls `cleave_alloc_local` at all. The function's own doc comment already states this is deliberate ("safe to open unconditionally... the decision was already made once, per allocation site, at compile time"). A temporary call counter (`cleave-rt/src/lib.rs`, gated by `CLEAVE_DEBUG_REGION_COUNT`, removed after use) confirmed the real scale directly: **`486` million calls over one real 10-epoch `mnist-interop` run** — far beyond anything explained by Dense/DenseState construction alone (a few million at most), pointing to much finer-grained, more deeply nested cleave-level loops somewhere in the real kernel/stdlib paying this cost on every single iteration, whether or not region-local allocation is ever used inside.

**Explicitly not attributed to Tier 3 or to today's `dps_rewrite` fixes** — this mechanism (`lower_loop`'s own unconditional region open/close) predates both. Tier 3 likely *increased* the real cost somewhat (more constructions now qualify as region-local candidates than before Dense/DenseState were light), but the mechanism itself, and its unconditional-regardless-of-need cost, is a separate, general, pre-existing property of every loop in the language.

**Fixed**: `cleave_region_enter`/`cleave_region_exit` emission is now *conditional*, per loop — `mlir_lower.rs::lower_loop` computes, once per loop right where its own `then_branch` is already extracted, `needs_region` (this loop's own direct top-level callees, intersected against `ctx.region_local_fns` — the same whole-program set `lower_top_level_fn` already consults). `region_analysis.rs::collect_direct_callees` (already built for `find_region_local_functions`'s own transitive descent) is reused directly rather than reimplemented — made `pub(crate)`, no new call-shape parsing logic duplicated. When `needs_region` is `false` (the overwhelming common case, per the `486`M-call finding), `cleave_region_enter` is never emitted at all, and `region_handle` becomes `None` — the `App`-to-`yield_targets` arm (`lower_cexpr`) already handled a `None` handle correctly (skipping `cleave_region_exit` too), so no change needed on the exit side at all. A nested loop's own direct calls are picked up by `collect_direct_callees`'s own unrestricted recursion into nested `Fix` defs too — slightly over-conservative for an *outer* loop wrapping an inner one that's the only real region user (the outer loop still wraps unnecessarily in that case), but harmless: an outer loop's own iteration count is always far smaller than whichever loop actually dominates the real cost, so this doesn't reintroduce anything close to the `486`M-call problem.

**Verified**: full `cargo test -p cleave --release --no-fail-fast` suite green; real AOT builds (`digits-interop`, `mnist-interop`, both `--no-openmp` and `--openmp`) all succeed; `digits-interop` — `0.94713414`, unchanged; `mnist-interop` — 10 real epochs, `0.9342`, bit-identical, no crash, on every rebuild during this fix.

**Re-measured the call count directly, same temporary counter technique (`CLEAVE_DEBUG_REGION_COUNT`, `cleave-rt/src/lib.rs`, removed after use)**: **`~120,000` calls, down from `486` million — a `~4,050×` reduction**, on the identical real 10-epoch `mnist-interop` run. `cleave-rt/src/lib.rs` itself carries no diff after cleanup (`git status`/`git diff` confirmed) — the whole fix lives in `mlir_lower.rs`/`region_analysis.rs`.

Wall-clock impact not chased further in this same sitting — the user's own machine was under unrelated, explicitly-accepted concurrent load for the whole VTune/region investigation (a game, per the user's own words), making any further assistant-side stopwatch comparison pointless per this project's own established practice; the `~120,000`-call figure and the unchanged accuracy are what this entry stands on. A clean wall-clock re-run, by the user, on a quiet machine, is the natural next data point if this axis is revisited.

## `--cse` was missing from the post-tiling/pre-`--expand-strided-metadata` pipeline stage — added, alongside a real (but empirically no-op on this kernel) redundant-`memref.copy`-elimination pass built while chasing the same lead — real, substantial, user-confirmed wall-clock win, correctly attributed after an initial misattribution

Direct follow-on to the VTune investigation above (the `memcpy`/`memrefCopy`/`ntdll` slice of the single-thread hotspot breakdown, raised as a lower-priority lead after the region fix, then explicitly reprioritized by the user: **"non ce n'est pas openmp, il est prévu de changer le parallelisme, donc c'est bien monothread qui nous intéresse"** — single-thread is the real target, OpenMP is a throwaway mechanism about to be replaced).

**Root-caused via a minimal, isolated `mlir-opt` probe reproducing the exact real schedule** (`cleave/mlir/matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`'s own `tile_using_forall %epi tile_sizes [8, 0]` on the bias-add epilogue, `fuse_into_containing_op`, `tile_using_for [0,16,0]`/`[0,0,16]` + `vectorize {create_named_contraction}` on the fused matmul) — matching this project's own established "verify empirically before writing a fix" discipline, not guessed from the real kernel's own far harder to read, fully-inlined disassembly alone. After `--loop-invariant-subset-hoisting` + `one-shot-bufferize`, the probe showed a real, necessary write-back copy (a tile's own private accumulator into the shared output's own subview) *immediately followed by a second, entirely redundant `memref.copy %x, %x`* — copying that exact same subview into *itself*. Confirmed directly that neither `--canonicalize` nor `--cse` alone folds this away; confirmed directly that `--cse` is a *precondition* for even seeing it as a textual self-copy at all (before CSE, two structurally identical `memref.subview` computations are two different SSA values).

**A real, separate bug found and fixed getting the elimination pass built at all, not specific to this pass**: `melior`'s own `remove_from_parent` (`mlirOperationRemoveFromParent`) only *detaches* an op from its parent block — its own documented contract, "not destroyed, only unlinked" — it does **not** free it. Calling it and leaving the result an orphaned, never-reclaimed, never-reinserted op crashed for real (`STATUS_STACK_BUFFER_OVERRUN`, a genuine MLIR-internal assertion, `"expected that op has no uses"`, hit *later*, in a subsequent pass — not at the call site itself), confirmed directly building the real `examples/digits-interop` kernel. This is not new or specific to `memref.copy`: `dps_rewrite.rs`'s own doc comment already documents the identical failure mode for `llvm.intr.memcpy`, and `unify_alloc.rs`'s own doc comment independently reconfirms it for `memref.alloc`/`memref.dealloc` — a general melior/MLIR-C-API hazard across every op kind tried so far in this codebase, now confirmed a third time. Fixed by finding the one place in this codebase where `remove_from_parent` *is* used safely (`mlir_lower.rs::build_matmul_transpose_no_seed`) and matching its exact pattern: detach, then *immediately* reclaim real ownership via `Operation::from_raw` and `drop` it explicitly (`Operation`'s own `Drop` impl is what actually calls `mlirOperationDestroy` — `remove_from_parent` alone never does).

**Built**: `cleave/src/redundant_copy_elim.rs` (new module, `pub fn eliminate_self_copies`), plus a `--cse` pass added right before it in `pipeline.rs` (previously absent at this specific pipeline location — confirmed directly by reading the surrounding stages, not assumed). A structural pass-manager bug found and fixed in the same edit, unrelated to correctness but worth recording: the new `--cse` stage's own `pass_manager` was accidentally the *same* `let`-bound one the *previous* stage (`--canonicalize`+`lower-vector-multi-reduction`) had already `.run()` once — `PassManager::add_pass` accumulates, so re-running it silently re-ran those two passes a second time too (harmless, both idempotent, but wasteful and inconsistent with every other stage's own fresh-`PassManager`-per-stage convention in this file) — fixed with a proper fresh `let pass_manager = pass::PassManager::new(context)`.

**Measured precisely, and initially misattributed — corrected here, not left standing**: a temporary call counter confirmed `eliminate_self_copies` genuinely finds and removes **11 real self-copies** on the real `mnist-interop` kernel. But a careful, *forced-clean* (`cargo clean -p mnist-interop -p cleave-build`, not just a `pipeline.rs` edit + rebuild — cargo's own `rerun-if-changed=src/kernel.cleave` means touching `pipeline.rs` alone does **not** force the build script to recompile `kernel.cleave`, a real methodology trap hit and corrected mid-investigation) object-level A/B (`llvm-objdump`, size/instruction-count/`memcpy`-relocation-count) showed **`eliminate_self_copies` has zero measured effect on the final compiled object** — identical size, instruction count, and `memcpy` relocation count with it enabled or disabled. Whatever those 11 ops would have become downstream is evidently already cleaned up by something later in the pipeline regardless (`--expand-strided-metadata`/`--finalize-memref-to-llvm`/LLVM's own final instruction selection) — the pass is safe and correct, but a no-op on this specific kernel's own real memcpy cost. Kept anyway (real, tested, harmless, might matter on a shape where the downstream cleanup doesn't happen to catch it) — not the reason for the win below.

**`--cse` alone, by contrast, is the real source of the win — small in static object size (`148146 -> 147065` bytes, `17550 -> 17319` instructions, the same forced-clean methodology, `~1.3%` smaller), but the user's own real, controlled wall-clock measurement shows something far larger**, explained by *where* the redundant computation CSE removes actually sits: duplicate `memref.subview`/address-arithmetic computations inside the hot, per-tile/per-k-step loop body, executed potentially tens of thousands of times over a real training run — a small *static* code shrink can still mean a large *dynamic* instruction-count reduction when what's eliminated was being needlessly recomputed on every one of those iterations, invisible to a purely static size/instruction-count diff.

**User's own measurement, on a now-quiet machine, both modes**: `OMP_NUM_THREADS` default (16 logical) — `21.28s`; `--no-openmp` (serial, the config this whole investigation was reprioritized around) — `29.59s`, down from the region-fix-era `~40-42s` this backlog's own previous entry left off at — **a real, roughly `~30%` serial-mode improvement**, user's own words: "gros progrès". The default-thread run beating the previous session's best is confirmed real as well.

**The user's own sharp, correct observation, worth recording precisely**: *"je pense que la partie openmp est de pire en pire à mesure qu'on améliore le code mono thread"* — as single-thread code keeps getting faster, OpenMP's own fixed fork/join cost becomes a *larger* fraction of a *smaller* per-region compute time, so its own relative speedup ratio mechanically shrinks even as *both* configurations' absolute times keep dropping. The numbers here confirm it directly: `29.59s / 21.28s ≈ 1.39×` at default (16-logical) thread count — a materially weaker speedup ratio than this backlog's own earlier-recorded `22.80s`-serial-era ratios, exactly the same "fixed cost against a shrinking base" mechanism this backlog's own "Fork/join fusion" entry already identified in the opposite direction (a *bigger* per-region cost lowering the *fraction* fork/join eats) — now observed from the other side, as the per-region cost keeps shrinking instead.

**Verified**: full `cargo test -p cleave --release --no-fail-fast` suite green; real AOT builds (`digits-interop`, `mnist-interop`) both succeed; `digits-interop` — `0.94713414`, unchanged; `mnist-interop` — `0.9342`, bit-identical, no crash, confirmed on every forced-clean rebuild during this investigation (both the buggy-crash state and the fixed state were caught this way, not glossed over).

## Fork/join fusion across `l1->l2->l3`'s own forward chain — built, worked correctly, measured as a real regression, reverted — the wrong axis was fused

Direct follow-up to the still-open "OpenMP parallel efficiency" lever (below): since every real gain to the matmul microkernel itself mechanically *shrinks* parallel efficiency (a fixed fork/join cost becomes a *bigger* fraction of a *smaller* per-region compute time — confirmed empirically this same session, M=4→M=8 tiling: efficiency dropped `24.6%→18.9%` even though both the serial and 8-thread absolute times improved), the natural next lever is fewer, bigger regions. Stage 0 (`doc/backlog-done.md`) already does exactly this for the *backward* chain (one `scf.forall` covering eight real ops). Built the direct forward-pass analogue: walk `l1`'s epilogue -> `l2`'s epilogue -> `l3`'s epilogue backward via `transform.get_producer_of_operand` (mirroring Stage 0's own technique exactly), fusing all three layers' matmul+bias-add+relu into *one* `scf.forall` per batch-chunk instead of three separate ones (`l4`'s own non-divisible `128 -> 10` shape deliberately left to the existing, unmodified per-matmul fallback).

**Two real bugs found and fixed getting it to actually match the real kernel** (both are real, general lessons, not one-offs): (1) an isolated, non-differentiated probe fuses each layer's bias-add with its own `relu` into one `linalg.generic`, but the real kernel's own backward pass reuses every layer's pre-`relu` value, so `--linalg-fuse-elementwise-ops` correctly keeps them as two separate ops — the real chain is 8 hops, not 5; (2) `relu` never changes shape, so bias-add and `relu` share an identical result type, and `filter_result_type` alone can't tell them apart (`num_associations` reports 2, the whole stage silently no-ops via `failures(suppress)` with zero trace) — fixed by anchoring on `l4`'s own uniquely-shaped matmul (`32x10`) and walking back one hop, the same "use a neighbor's unique shape as a landmark" trick Stage 0 itself already needed once.

**Structurally, it worked exactly as designed**: one function, `394` real `vfmadd`, zero `gather`/`scatter`, confirmed via disassembly. Full test suite green, `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` both bit-identical to the established baseline accuracy (confirms the fusion mechanism itself, and its correct no-op on a non-matching shape, are both sound).

**Measured anyway, per this session's own hard-won discipline of not trusting "structurally cleaner must be faster": a real, reproducible regression.** The cleanest signal is single-thread (lowest noise, and — the key diagnostic — *no cross-core interaction is even possible* at `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1`): `36.2s` (M=8 alone) `-> 42.1s` (+ forward fusion), a consistent `~16%` regression across two repeated runs each side. 8-thread showed the same direction, noisier.

**Root cause, reasoned through rather than left as "fusion is just bad"**: this is a loop-*interchange* cost, not a fork/join one — and the single-thread result is what proves it (ruling out any cross-core cache-contention or scheduling-imbalance story outright, since none is possible with one thread). Before: the outer loop is *layer*, the inner is *batch-chunk* — each weight matrix (`W1` 1.57MB, `W2` 512KB, `W3` 128KB) loads once and is reused across all four batch-chunks before moving to the next layer, a natural, sequential, cache-friendly access pattern. After: fusing per batch-chunk makes the outer loop *batch-chunk*, inner *layer* — each of the four chunks walks `W1->W2->W3` in turn, evicting the previous weight matrix from L1/L2 before the *next* chunk needs `W1` again, forcing up to `4x` the effective weight-matrix reload traffic. The workload was already known to be far from compute-bound (`doc/backlog-done.md`'s own roofline entry: cleave at `~0.16%` of theoretical peak) — exactly the regime where this kind of reuse loss dominates a fork-count win.

**Reverted cleanly** (the M=8 tiling fix itself, unrelated and independently validated, kept — `git diff` after revert shows only that one-line change, confirmed). **Real implication for the still-open fork/join lever below**: reducing region *count* by merging *along the batch dimension* trades away weight reuse and loses on this workload. The next real attempt, if made, needs to preserve the old *layer-outer* memory-access order while still forking only once — e.g., one thread team spawned per training step with real `omp.barrier`s *between* layers inside it, not a transform-dialect fusion that collapses the loop nest itself. Not attempted; a materially different, more delicate mechanism than `fuse_into_containing_op`.

## Per-shape, compile-time OpenMP thread-count selection — a real, cleave-specific lever MKL/PyTorch can only approximate at runtime, not yet attempted

Raised directly by the user, following up on the accumulator-latency-chain fix (`doc/backlog-done.md`'s own "the `36.7%` accumulator-latency chain... real fix found and landed" entry): PyTorch/MKL choose their own thread count *dynamically*, per call, based on problem size — a small matmul often runs with fewer threads than a big one, since fork-join overhead can exceed the parallelism benefit for small work.
