Measured directly on the real kernel, by the user, across five thread counts (`1`/`2`/`4`/`8`/`16`): **`4` threads is the real optimum (`22.80s`), not `8` (physical cores, `24.56s`) or `16` (default, `30.26s`)** — a real, non-obvious, U-shaped curve, not "more threads always better" up to the physical core count.

**`OMP_DYNAMIC=true` (the zero-code, standard-OpenMP way to ask libomp itself to pick fewer threads when it judges the work too small) does not reproduce this** — tested directly: `29.82s`, essentially unchanged from the plain `16`-thread default, alongside a real runtime warning (`OMP: Warning #227: Cannot determine machine load balance - Using KMP_DYNAMIC_MODE=thread limit`) — this build's own libomp can't do the load-balance detection its own dynamic mode needs on this platform. Not a viable path as-is.

**The real, cleave-specific opportunity, not yet built**: every matmul shape in a cleave program is fully static (every dimension known at compile time, unlike MKL, which only learns shapes at runtime) — in principle, `mlir_lower.rs`/the transform-dialect schedule could compute a real, shape-appropriate `num_threads` per matmul (its own FLOP count, `M*N*K`, all compile-time constants) and bake it directly into that *specific* region's own `omp.parallel`, doing *better* than MKL's own runtime heuristic since the decision is made with perfect, static information, not an educated guess. **Two real, unresolved technical questions, found by checking before attempting, not assumed away**:
1. `--convert-scf-to-openmp`'s own `--num-threads=<uint>` option (confirmed real, `mlir-opt --help-hidden`) is **pass-wide**, applying uniformly to every `scf.parallel` region a single invocation converts — not a per-region override. Getting a genuinely different thread count per matmul shape would need either running the conversion multiple times, each scoped to a different subset of regions (partitioned by shape — real, fiddly schedule engineering, not attempted), or a transform-dialect mechanism that computes a thread count from a matched op's own static shape and attaches it as a real, per-op attribute `--convert-scf-to-openmp`'s own conversion pattern would need to consult instead of (or ahead of) the pass-wide option — not confirmed whether that pattern reads any such override at all.
2. Even if a per-region thread count can be set, the *aggregate* effect isn't obviously a pure win: `net_grad`/`Optimizer::step` run `18,750` times over a full training run, each doing 8 real matmuls — if several of those get their *own* independent thread-team fork/join per call (rather than sharing one already-open team), the *number* of fork/join events could go up even as *some* individual regions get cheaper, a real tradeoff not yet measured either way.

**Scoped as its own, separate chantier — not blocking the rest of the parallelization work**: a real, bounded next step (not yet started) would be confirming point 1 directly (read `SCFToOpenMP.cpp`'s own conversion pattern for whether it already supports a per-op override, before designing schedule-side arithmetic to produce one), then, if real, prototyping a two-tier split (e.g., "big" shapes at one thread count, "small" ones at another, chosen via a compile-time FLOP-count threshold) rather than a fully general per-shape scheme up front. **User's own explicit call**: find further *single-thread* wins first (a fixed thread count is trivial to change later and doesn't gate that work at all), then come back to find the real optimal thread count — dynamic or fixed — once the single-thread baseline stops moving.

**Re-measured after the native-stack-leak fix (`doc/backlog-done.md`'s own "AOT binary built with `--no-openmp`... genuinely crashed" entry), against a real PyTorch-CPU baseline for the first time on this exact sweep, by the user directly, on the same machine, same session — the U-shape holds, and a sharper, more important finding sits alongside it**:

| config | elapsed | vs PyTorch (1 thread) |
|---|---|---|
| PyTorch (`torch.set_num_threads(1)`, `mnist_bench.py --threads 1`) | **16.46s** (0.9303 acc) | reference |
| cleave, `OMP_NUM_THREADS=4` | **22.49s** | 1.37× slower |
| cleave, `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8` (physical core count) | 25.00s | 1.52× |
| cleave, `OMP_NUM_THREADS=2` | 27.38s | 1.66× |
| cleave, `--no-openmp` (genuinely no OpenMP compiled in) | 37.86s | 2.30× |
| cleave, `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` (OpenMP build, forced to 1 thread) | 42.95s | 2.61× |
| cleave, `OMP_NUM_THREADS=16` (default today — logical/SMT count) | 45.60s | 2.77× |

(all cleave rows: `0.9342` accuracy, identical across every config, confirming none of this is a correctness difference.)

Two separate findings, not one:
1. **The U-shape is real and reproducible** across two independent measurement sessions (`22.80s`/`24.56s`/`30.26s` then vs `22.49s`/`25.00s`/`45.60s` now, at `4`/`8`/`16` threads respectively) — 4 threads beats both fewer and more, on an 8-physical-core/16-logical-thread machine. The `16`-thread number moved a fair amount between sessions (`30.26s` -> `45.60s`) — not chased further here, plausibly ordinary machine-load variance at this specific oversubscribed/SMT-contended setting rather than a regression from any change in between (every other row's own accuracy and rough magnitude stayed consistent).
2. **New this round, and the more consequential one**: `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` on the OpenMP-compiled binary (`42.95s`) is measurably *slower* than `--no-openmp` (`37.86s`) — real, non-zero OpenMP fork/join machinery overhead exists even at one worker thread, on top of whatever the actual compute costs. And **even cleave's own best-tuned configuration (`22.49s` at 4 threads) is still `1.37×` slower than a single PyTorch thread (`16.46s`)** — the gap this project's whole parallelization effort is meant to close isn't purely a "not enough threads" question; it's already present at the 1-thread-vs-1-thread level, underneath whatever the thread-count tuning can still buy back. Closing *that* gap (single-thread codegen quality, `doc/backlog-done.md`'s own several already-fixed mechanisms — FMA fusion, vectorization, the accumulator-latency chain, `Broadcast0`/`reduce0` vectorization) remains the more fundamental lever than thread-count tuning alone, exactly the ordering the user's own earlier explicit call (just above) already anticipated.

**Re-measured again, much later, after the region-open-conditional fix and the `--cse` addition (both entries below) — the single-thread-codegen-quality lever named above just paid off directly, not a separate axis**: cleave's own `--no-openmp` serial time dropped to **`29.59s`** (user's own measurement, quiet machine) — against the same `16.46s` PyTorch-1-thread reference, that's **`1.80×`**, down from `2.30×` at the same config just above. Raised directly by the user, cross-checking the table here rather than trusting a vague impression: **"si tu regardes les baselines, je crois qu'en monothread PyTorch est vers 15, ça veut dire qu'on est plus qu'à x2 à peu près"** — confirmed precisely against this table's own stored `16.46s` reference, `1.80×`, close to but a bit better than the user's own quick "~2×" estimate. A real, quantified narrowing of exactly the gap this section's own finding #2 called "the more fundamental lever" — achieved with zero new threads, on the single-thread axis alone, ahead of the user's own planned change to the parallelization strategy itself (which is why single-thread, not the OpenMP rows above, is the reference this project now measures itself against — see the `--cse` entry below, raised directly by the user: *"non ce n'est pas openmp, il est prévu de changer le parallélisme, donc c'est bien monothread qui nous intéresse"*).

**Re-measured a third time, after the `dps_rewrite` `Strategy::Passthrough` fix (own entry below, `~8.4×` fewer bytes copied per sample) — user's own real measurement, four independent serial runs plus one 4-thread run, same quiet machine**: `--no-openmp` — `31.17s`, `26.87s`, `29.68s`, `28.10s` (average `~28.96s`) — against `16.46s`, **`~1.76×`**, a further real narrowing from `1.80×`, on the single-thread axis alone, exactly as this fix's own object-level measurement predicted (a real, if partial, reduction — the copy eliminated was a real cost, but compute already dominates `~69%` of total time per the VTune breakdown above, so a memcpy-only fix was never going to close the whole remaining gap by itself). **The 4-thread number is the real headline**: `OMP_NUM_THREADS=4` — **`16.59s`** — against PyTorch's own `16.46s` (1 thread), that's **`~1.01×`** — cleave's own current best configuration has, for the first time this whole project, essentially **matched** a single PyTorch thread on the one real workload this project measures itself against. Not yet a win, but the gap this project's own "parallelization is the viability test" framing exists to close is, right now, close to zero on this specific measurement.

## `examples/mnist-interop`'s own real training loop is ~12.7x slower wall-clock than an equivalent PyTorch-CPU/MKL baseline — several real causes ruled out with direct evidence, narrowed to one specific, disassembly-confirmed mechanism (the reduction accumulator round-trips through memory every `k`-step instead of staying register-resident), real fix not yet attempted

Raised directly, high priority — a real, existential concern for the project if this doesn't close: does cleave's own compiled training loop stand a chance against a mainstream, MKL-backed CPU baseline at all? Investigated with real rigor rather than a single top-line number, because several plausible-sounding hypotheses turned out to be wrong when actually tested, and it matters which ones are ruled out versus still open.

**The baseline, both sides measured under real, comparable conditions, not assumed.** A PyTorch-CPU reference (`bench/mnist-pytorch/mnist_bench.py`, poetry-isolated, CPU-only wheel) built to match `examples/mnist-interop/src/kernel.cleave` term for term — identical architecture (784→512→256→128→10, `He`/`Xavier` init formulas copied exactly from `nn.cleave`, zero-initialized biases matching `Ring::zero()` rather than `nn.Linear`'s own default), identical loss shape (`sum(err*err)` over the whole batch, not a mean, with `lr` scaled by the batch size at the same point `optim.cleave` does it), identical optimizer (hand-rolled plain SGD, not `torch.optim.SGD`'s own defaults, to guarantee no extra momentum/decay), identical hyperparameters (`epochs=10, lr=0.001, seed=1, batch=32`), identical unshuffled sequential batching, same dataset/mirror/cache as `data.rs` itself. Both measured with each framework's own real default multi-threading (PyTorch: 8 threads, its own default; cleave: `--openmp`, real and already the `cleave-build`/AOT default) — deliberately not restricted to one thread on either side, the fairest "best each side can do" comparison.

**Result**: PyTorch — 7.65s total (0.77s/epoch), 93.03% test accuracy. cleave (`--openmp`, real AOT `--emit-exe` build) — 96.89s total (9.69s/epoch), 93.42% test accuracy. **~12.7x slower**, with accuracy essentially identical (cleave slightly *higher*, within normal run-to-run PRNG noise) — confirms the gap is a real throughput difference on equivalent work, not cleave doing less computation or converging to a degenerate optimum.

**Hypothesis 1, tested directly, ruled out: scalar-per-element FFI data loading.** `load_train_batch_input`/`_target` (`kernel.cleave`) called `train_pixel`/`train_target` once per pixel/target-class — ~476M individual `extern fn` crossings over the full 10-epoch run, and (worse) an opaque call inside the loading loop's own body blocked MLIR from ever vectorizing the copy. Fixed with bulk, one-call-per-sample `extern fn`s (`data.rs::train_sample_pixels`/`train_sample_targets`, a single `copy_nonoverlapping` each, matching `format_f32`'s own `out: *mut u8` precedent) collapsing ~476M crossings to ~1.2M. **Measured result: 105.55s — no improvement, slightly worse.** Reverted (`git checkout` on `data.rs`/`kernel.cleave`) rather than keeping a proven-net-negative change. A VTune profile taken right after this test showed the compiled kernel's own main-thread CPU time (`train_and_evaluate`, 1142.65s of 2323.81s total CPU-seconds) is almost entirely *self* time in the real compute, not data loading (`data::init`: 0.013s, negligible) — data loading was never the bottleneck.

**The real per-core-vs-parallel-scaling breakdown, corrected after an intermediate arithmetic mistake.** A serial (`--no-openmp` build) run of the exact same kernel measured **333.1s** — cleave's own OpenMP gives a real **3.44x** speedup on this 8-physical-core machine (matching the user's own earlier VTune observation of "~3x scaling, with or without OpenMP" almost exactly), not the ~8x that would be ideal. Naively dividing the overall 12.7x gap by only cleave's own 3.44x parallel-scaling shortfall (as if PyTorch's own reported number were a serial-equivalent baseline) gives a misleading ~43-58x apparent single-core gap — wrong, because PyTorch's 0.77s/epoch is *itself* already an aggregate across its own 8 threads, not a serial number. Done properly (comparing real measured single-core cleave throughput, below, against PyTorch's own aggregate divided by its thread count), the true per-core gap is **~4.5-6x** — a real, modest, explicable shortfall (consistent with "vectorized but not register/cache-blocked" versus a hand-tuned MKL microkernel), not a mysterious one. The observed 12.7x overall gap is this ~4.5-6x per-core shortfall *compounding* with the ~43%-efficient (3.44 of 8x) OpenMP scaling — plausibly the same root cause manifesting twice (an unblocked kernel is both slower per core, spilling past whatever cache actually matters at this size, *and* scales worse across cores, contending over shared memory bandwidth), though this compounding-cause link itself isn't independently confirmed, just consistent with the numbers.

**Isolated single-matmul throughput, measured directly** (`bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_matmul_n1000.cleave`/`_n3000.cleave`, the exact slope between two iteration counts to cancel out fixed JIT/startup cost): **3.49ms/call, 7.37 GFLOP/s single-core**, for the kernel's own largest layer shape (`32x784 @ 784x512`).

**Hypothesis 2, cache/register tiling (`--affine-loop-tile`) — three real, distinct attempts on the real AOT pipeline, all regressed, each root-caused precisely rather than re-guessed blind:**
1. `cache-size=512` (MLIR's own automatic heuristic): confirmed via a minimal `mlir-opt` probe (`bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_matmul.mlir`, deleted after use) that it tiles *only* the innermost (`k`, reduction) dimension, to a tile size of 4 — `i`/`j` stay completely untiled, so the working `C` tile per `k`-step is still the entire `32x512` output — not real cache blocking, just three extra nested loops and per-iteration tile-bound `affine map` evaluation, pure overhead. **96.9s → 121.5s.**
2. `tile-sizes=8,64,128` (explicit, all three dimensions genuinely blocked, confirmed on the probe): tiling `i` (the batch dimension) shrinks the *trip count* `affine-parallelize{max-nested=1}` sees on its own outermost loop from 32 to 4 — confirmed directly on the probe (`affine.parallel (0) to (32) step (8)`) — at most 4 of this machine's 8 cores ever busy at once. **121.5s → 125.5s** (still worse).
3. `tile-sizes=1,64,128` (tile size *1* for `i`, a deliberately degenerate no-op tile preserving the full 32-iteration parallel loop, confirmed on the probe): preserves all 32-way parallelism, still genuinely blocks `j`/`k`. **124.5s — no better than attempt 2, still far worse than untiled.**

**Retested on a clean, isolated, single-core JIT probe with zero parallelization confound at all** (`--run` defaults OpenMP off) to settle whether tiling has *any* real per-core benefit independent of the parallelism interactions above: untiled baseline 3.488ms/call vs. tiled (`tile-sizes=1,32,64`, deliberately smaller/more L1-conservative than attempt 3) 3.555ms/call — **neutral to slightly negative even in the cleanest possible test.** Conclusion: `affine-loop-tile`, as tested across four real, distinct, reasoned parameter choices, does not help at this problem size on this machine — plausibly because `784x512` (1.5MB) already sits reasonably within L3 without blocking, and the tile-boundary bookkeeping (runtime `min`-clamped `affine map` evaluation per iteration, visible directly in the tiled loop dumps) outweighs whatever bandwidth saving remains. Not shipped — `pipeline.rs` reverted to its pre-investigation state (confirmed via `git diff`, clean).

**A real, controlled, confirmed compiler limitation found along the way — the `export fn`-Tensor-parameter case — but confirmed *not* to be what's hurting the real kernel.** Root-caused via direct MLIR source reading, not guessed: `affine-super-vectorize`'s own dependence analysis (`mlir/lib/Dialect/Affine/Analysis/LoopAnalysis.cpp`) explicitly rejects any memref with a non-identity layout map (`"NYI: non-trivial layout map"`). **Confirmed via a controlled A/B test, dimensions held identical and already register-width-clean (`32x512x512`, all multiples of 16) on both sides**: a `Tensor` arriving as an `export fn` parameter (crossing the ABI boundary) compiles to **100% scalar FMA, zero packed**; the *identical* matmul on a locally-constructed tensor (`Init::xavier()`, never crossing a function boundary) compiles **mostly packed, zmm**. Real, size-independent, structural. A real, existing MLIR pass built for exactly this (`--normalize-memrefs`, confirmed via its own doc comment — `mlir/lib/Dialect/MemRef/Transforms/NormalizeMemRefs.cpp`: "All memrefs passed across functions with non-trivial layout maps are converted to ones with trivial identity layout ones") **partially** helps the `export fn`-parameter case (layout-map error count 3→1, still not fully packed — plausibly because the pass can't safely change an *externally-visible* function's own parameter type without breaking its ABI, only what's reachable through it). **But has zero measurable effect on the real mnist kernel — byte-identical FMA instruction counts with or without it, no layout-map error at all even without the pass.** `train_and_evaluate` never passes a `Tensor` as an `export fn` parameter (only `epochs: i32, lr: f32, seed: i64`; every tensor is constructed locally) — this specific pathology, while real and now precisely understood, was never the real kernel's own problem. Not added to the default pipeline (zero benefit on the case that matters); worth remembering if a future `export fn` taking `Tensor` parameters directly becomes a real, exercised pattern.

**A real methodology error in the vectorization-mix reasoning above, found and corrected before it misled anything further.** The "32 packed / 13 scalar" FMA count on the real kernel, and an earlier "128→10 is ~85% scalar" reading on an isolated probe of that specific layer, were both **misattributed** — confirmed directly by locating exactly where each `vfmadd*ss` instruction lives in the disassembly, not by re-counting blind. Every scalar FMA found sits in its own small, separate, address-contiguous loop *before* the real matmul body (`probe..omp_par`, the actual OpenMP-outlined hot region) — matching `Init<Tensor<T,In,Out>>::xavier`/`he`'s own nested per-element draw loop (`nn.cleave`: `buf[i,j] = uniform(...)`, genuinely, correctly scalar — an RNG draw can't trivially vectorize), called once per tensor constructed, not once per matmul iteration. **Looking specifically at the real hot loop (the `omp_par`-outlined region) instead of the whole function: every locally-constructed matmul tested — including the previously-"suspect" `128→10` layer — compiles to 100% packed `vfmadd231ps`/zmm, zero scalar, a real broadcast-multiply-accumulate microkernel pattern (`vfmadd231ps zmm3, zmm2, [mem]{1to16}`).** Vectorization itself was never the real kernel's own bottleneck; the earlier "29% scalar" framing is retracted.

**The real, disassembly-confirmed finding: the reduction's own accumulator round-trips through memory on every step of `k`, instead of staying resident in a register for the whole reduction.** The real hot loop's own inner body: `vmovups zmm3 {k1}{z}, [rdi]` (reload the output tile) → `vfmadd231ps zmm3, zmm2, [...]{1to16}` (one contribution) → `vmovups [rdi]{k1}, zmm3` (store it straight back) — repeated once per step of `k`, not accumulated in a register across the whole reduction and spilled once at the end. For this kernel's own largest layer (`k=784`), that's 784 redundant memory round-trips per output tile instead of one — real, `k`-proportional memory traffic, entirely independent of the (already-confirmed-correct) vectorization.

**Tested the obvious, precisely-named fix directly — confirmed, not assumed, that it doesn't apply here.** `--affine-scalrep` ("Replace affine memref accesses by scalars by forwarding stores to loads and eliminating redundant loads", confirmed via `mlir-opt --help-hidden`) is MLIR's own store-to-load forwarding pass — exactly the shape of this problem on paper. Added to the pipeline (right after `convert-linalg-to-affine-loops`, before parallelization/vectorization) and tested two ways: on the real, well-vectorized local-construction probe (`--emit-object`, full disassembly) and, to rule out a placement/ordering mistake, on a minimal standalone `mlir-opt` probe (`--convert-linalg-to-affine-loops --affine-scalrep`, nothing else). **Both byte-for-byte identical to the untreated baseline — confirmed the pass's own `affine.load`/`affine.store` ops are exactly the ones present (right op type, so not a mismatch), it simply doesn't fire on this pattern.** Reverted (`pipeline.rs` confirmed clean via `git diff`). Most likely explanation, not independently confirmed: `affine-scalrep` is a narrow peephole (a store immediately visible to a *later* load of the same address), not a full promotion of a loop-*carried* memory accumulator into a proper `iter_args` SSA value across iterations — the harder transformation this actually needs. The more likely real fix is architectural, not a pass to insert: vectorizing `linalg.matmul` at the `linalg` level itself (which has real reduction-dimension semantics, and can lower a reduction into `vector.contract`/a genuinely register-resident accumulator) *before* ever converting to naive `affine.for` loops — rather than the current `convert-linalg-to-affine-loops` → `affine-super-vectorize` combination, which apparently never had a way to know the load/store pair was a loop-carried reduction at all. Not attempted — a real, structural change to the vectorization strategy, not a quick pipeline addition.

**That structural fix, actually prototyped via `mlir-opt`'s transform dialect (not `mlir::linalg::vectorize`'s C++ API directly — no plain pass wraps it; `transform.structured.vectorize`, MLIR's real, current entry point, confirmed via `--help-hidden` listing `transform` as an available dialect and `--transform-interpreter` as its driver) — real, incremental progress, not yet a complete fix:**

1. **Plain `transform.structured.vectorize` on the untiled `linalg.matmul`** (`bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_matmul_transform.mlir`) produces exactly the hoped-for shape at the abstract level — a single `vector.multi_reduction <add>` op, real reduction semantics, no memory involved in the reduction itself. But the vectors involved are the size of the *entire* matrix (`vector<32x512x784xf32>`) — an abstraction, not hardware-sized. Pushing this through `--lower-vector-multi-reduction` (which does exist and does work — confirmed it expands the reduction into a real SSA-chained sequence of `vector.extract`+`arith.addf`, genuinely register-resident, no memory round-trip) fully *unrolls* the reduction (784 static steps for this kernel's largest layer) rather than leaving it as a loop — pushing the resulting IR through the rest of the lowering pipeline (`--convert-vector-to-scf --convert-vector-to-llvm --convert-to-llvm`) hung for minutes on this one function alone and was killed rather than let run further — a real, separate, self-inflicted problem (full unrolling before tiling is simply the wrong order), not evidence against the approach itself.
2. **Tiling first, then vectorizing each tile** (`transform.structured.tile_using_for %matmul tile_sizes [1, 16, 16]`, then `vectorize` the tiled op) fixes the unrolling-explosion problem — produces a clean 3-level `scf.for` nest (`i`×1, `j`×16, `k`×16) with a *bounded*, hardware-sized `vector.multi_reduction` per tile. Real progress: **49 memory round-trips per output tile instead of 784** (one per 16-wide `k`-tile rather than one per single `k`-step) — but still not zero, because `transform.structured.vectorize` vectorizes each tile independently; it doesn't on its own hoist the accumulator's own `vector.transfer_read`/`write` pair out of the surrounding `k`-tile loop into a real loop-carried value.
3. **Tried the obvious next fix, `--loop-invariant-subset-hoisting`** (a real MLIR pass — "Hoist loop invariant subset ops outside of the loop", confirmed via `--help-hidden` — built for exactly "this memory access doesn't depend on the loop variable, hoist it") on the tiled+vectorized memref-level IR. **Byte-for-byte identical, no effect**, despite the accumulator's own `memref.subview` genuinely not depending on the `k`-tile loop variable at all (confirmed by inspection — only `%arg3`/`%arg4`, i.e. `i`/`j`, appear in its indices). Most likely explanation, tested directly rather than assumed: hoisting a *memref* read/write pair safely requires proving no aliasing, which this general-purpose pass declines to attempt for raw memrefs (memrefs can alias in ways pure SSA values structurally cannot) — confirmed by re-running the *identical* tile+vectorize sequence on a **tensor**-typed `linalg.matmul` instead of a memref-typed one: the tensor version already produces real `scf.for ... iter_args` carrying the accumulator as a genuine SSA value (`tensor.extract_slice`/`insert_slice` around a loop-carried tensor argument) — the safe, no-aliasing tensor form doing automatically what the hoisting pass wouldn't attempt on memrefs.
4. **Bufferizing that tensor-level result** (`--one-shot-bufferize="bufferize-function-boundaries"`, cleave's own existing bufferization pass) was the natural next test — but the accumulator's own `iter_arg` bufferizes to the *whole* `memref<32x512xf32>` (the same backing memory throughout, nothing promoted), because the loop's own `iter_arg` type at the point of tiling is the whole output tensor, sliced via `extract_slice`/`insert_slice` *inside* the loop body — not a small `vector<1x16xf32>`-typed `iter_arg` directly. Bufferization has nothing to promote because the loop was never structured to carry just the small accumulator tile as its own value.

**The two-level tiling structure, actually tried** (`bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_matmul_2level.mlir`): two *separate*, sequential `transform.structured.tile_using_for` calls — first `[1, 16, 0]` (tile `i`/`j` only, leaving a smaller `linalg.matmul` that still reduces the *full* `k=784`), then `[0, 0, 16]` *on that smaller op* (tiling only its own reduction). This works exactly as hoped at the tensor level: the inner `k`-loop's own `iter_args` is now genuinely the small `tensor<1x16xf32>` output tile itself (confirmed directly in the dumped IR), not the whole `32x512` tensor threaded through with `extract_slice`/`insert_slice` at every step the way the single-combined-tiling attempt produced. (MLIR does also have a purpose-built op for reduction-specific tiling, `transform.structured.tile_reduction_using_for`, confirmed via `LinalgTransformOps.td` — but its own documented shape is a *different* strategy, split-K-style: independent partial sums into a small buffer, combined in a separate step afterward, not "keep one running accumulator resident" — the two-*separate*-`tile_using_for`-calls approach above is the more directly applicable one here, not that op.)

**But bufferizing this correct tensor-level structure still doesn't survive as register-resident — a real, deeper finding, not yet resolved.** Running `--one-shot-bufferize="bufferize-function-boundaries"` (cleave's own existing bufferization pass) on this IR: the small `tensor<1x16xf32>` `iter_arg` bufferizes back down to a `memref.subview` *aliased directly into the final output buffer's own memory* — a reasonable bufferization choice in isolation (avoids an extra allocation/copy, writes happen "in place") — but it means the `k`-loop's own body still does a `vector.transfer_read`/`vector.transfer_write` pair against that subview on every iteration; `--canonicalize` afterward even drops the loop's own `iter_args` entirely, since the loop-carried tensor value resolved to "the same fixed memory address" throughout, nothing left to carry. The *tensor*-level structure was exactly right; *bufferization's own default in-place policy* undoes the benefit by choosing to represent the small per-iteration tile as a memory reference rather than keeping it unbuffered (a real vector value) until the loop exits and only the final result needs to reach memory.

**Pushed further the same evening: found a real fix for the *tensor*-level case, then confirmed empirically (not just theorized) that it doesn't survive cleave's own current pipeline ordering.** `--loop-invariant-subset-hoisting`, re-tried on the two-level-tiled result *before* bufferizing (i.e. on the genuinely-safe, no-aliasing tensor form, not the memref form it had already failed on) — this time it worked exactly as hoped: the `k`-loop's own `iter_args` becomes the loop-carried tensor/vector value directly (`vector.transfer_read`/`write` against the loop's own `%argN` in place, not a freshly `extract_slice`d value each iteration). Bufferizing *that* result (still `--one-shot-bufferize`) produced the real target shape: `%alloc = memref.alloc() {alignment = 64} : memref<1x16xf32>` — a genuinely **local**, freshly-allocated per-tile buffer (not aliased into the big output matrix), with the `k`-loop reading/writing *this* local buffer and only one copy into the real output after the loop exits. Pushed all the way through `--lower-vector-multi-reduction --convert-vector-to-scf --convert-vector-to-llvm --convert-to-llvm`: compiled quickly this time (unlike the untiled full-unroll attempt, which hung and was killed) and produced genuine `vector<16xf32>`-typed `llvm.fadd` chains — real hardware-width vector arithmetic, not the giant `vector<32x512x784xf32>` abstraction nor a scalar remainder loop.

**Wired this exact validated sequence into the real pipeline (`pipeline.rs`, right before `convert-linalg-to-affine-loops`, via `transform-preload-library`+`transform-interpreter` reading an external `.mlir` transform-library file, plus `loop-invariant-subset-hoisting`) and measured on the real isolated single-matmul probe.** First attempt failed outright — a real, separate mistake, not a design problem: forgot to call the matching `register_*` functions (`pass::transform_dialect::register_preload_library_pass`/`register_interpreter_pass`, `pass::transform::register_loop_invariant_subset_hoisting`) before referencing these passes by name in the textual pipeline, the same registration step every other textual pass addition in this file already needed — fixed, confirmed compiling and running once added. **Measured result: 3.599ms/call — slightly worse than the untiled baseline (3.488ms), not better.** This confirms, empirically now rather than just as a theoretical risk flagged before attempting it: cleave's own pipeline already bufferizes *before* the point this transform sequence runs, so `linalg.matmul` is already memref-typed by the time it gets here — the exact condition under which `--loop-invariant-subset-hoisting` was already confirmed to no-op (aliasing conservatism on raw memrefs). The real fix validated at the tensor level does not survive being inserted at cleave's *current* bufferization-before-vectorization pipeline position. Reverted (`pipeline.rs` confirmed clean via `git diff`).

**Real next step, well-specified, not yet attempted**: this whole transform sequence needs to run *before* cleave's own bufferization, not after — a genuine pipeline reordering (moving `linalg.matmul`-specific tiling/vectorization to operate on tensor-typed IR, ahead of the existing `one-shot-bufferize` call), not another pass insertion at the current position. This is a larger, real architectural change to `pipeline.rs`'s own stage ordering, needing care about what else currently depends on bufferization happening where it does today, before attempting it — not a same-session follow-up guess.

**Wired the two-level-tiling fix into the real pipeline that same night, right before `one-shot-bufferize` (so `linalg.matmul`-shaped ops are still tensor-typed when the transform runs) — three real, distinct blockers found and worked through in sequence, the first two fixed and confirmed, the third genuinely still open:**

1. **First attempt: silently matched nothing at all — not an error, a real gap in test methodology.** Measured performance was statistically unchanged (~3.6ms/call either side of bufferization, both slightly worse than the 3.488ms untiled baseline) — indistinguishable from "did nothing." Root-caused by dumping the module's own text immediately before the transform pass ran (`module.as_operation().to_string()`, a temporary debug `eprintln!`, removed after): **cleave's own matmul lowering never emits `linalg.matmul` (the named op) at all — it builds `linalg.generic` directly**, with hand-written `indexing_maps`/`iterator_types` (`mlir_lower.rs::build_matmul_no_seed`). `transform.structured.match ops{["linalg.matmul"]}` matched zero ops, so every downstream tile/vectorize step silently operated on nothing — not a bug in the transform interpreter, a correct response to a genuinely empty match set. Fixed by matching `"linalg.generic"` instead (`matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`) — safe for this probe (its only `linalg.generic` is the matmul itself), though a real production version would need a tighter match (e.g. by iterator-type shape) to avoid also catching genuinely-elementwise `linalg.generic` ops from `--convert-elementwise-to-linalg` elsewhere in a real program.
2. **Second, real blocker, once matching worked: `transform.structured.vectorize` genuinely fails on cleave's own matmul body — "Attempted to vectorize, but failed", reproduced on a minimal standalone `mlir-opt` probe too, not a cleave-specific bug.** Root-caused by reading the full generic body cleave actually emits (same debug-dump technique): the payload uses `linalg.index 2`/`arith.cmpi`/`arith.select` to avoid a physical zero-fill of the accumulator — a real, deliberate, already-once-hard-won optimization, precisely documented in `mlir_lower.rs::build_matmul_no_seed`'s own doc comment (avoids a correctness bug from *skipping* initialization entirely, hit once before; also tuned so the multiply's result has exactly one real use, letting LLVM's own backend fuse a real FMA — confirmed there via the *old* `affine-super-vectorize` path, which this doc comment states handles the `select` fine). **`transform.structured.vectorize` is a different vectorizer with a different limitation**: confirmed directly, on an isolated hand-written probe, that removing the `linalg.index`/`select` and using a genuine `linalg.fill`-zeroed seed with a plain `out + a*b` body vectorizes cleanly (real `vector.multi_reduction`) — most likely because the vectorizer's own reduction-detection heuristic wants a combiner that consumes `outs` *directly*, and the `select` breaks that direct-consumption pattern even though it's mathematically equivalent. **Tested whether removing the trick is actually safe before touching real cleave codegen**: rewrote `build_matmul_no_seed` to use a real `linalg.fill` seed and a plain body, confirmed via cleave's own compiler end to end — output bit-identical to the known-good baseline (`2.0327845`, `probe_matmul_n1000.cleave`), and, critically, **real FMA fusion survives on the existing default pipeline** (`llvm-objdump` on the real compiled object: `2 vfmadd231ps`, zero separate `vmulps`/`vaddps` pairs) — the multiply's result still has exactly one real use in this simpler form, so the property the original trick was tuned for holds regardless. A second real gap found immediately after fixing the match, fixed the same way: `vector.multi_reduction` (from `transform.structured.vectorize`) needs its own lowering (`--lower-vector-multi-reduction`) that the existing pipeline never needed before (the *old* `affine-super-vectorize` path never produces this op at all) — present in every standalone probe that validated this sequence earlier, forgotten on the first real-pipeline wiring attempt; added back (`pass::vector::register_lower_vector_multi_reduction`), confirmed via dump that `vector.multi_reduction` genuinely disappears afterward.
3. **Third blocker, still open, genuinely deeper than a missing pass: `error: LLVM Translation failed for operation: builtin.unrealized_conversion_cast`, a hard crash (segfault) at final translation, both fixes above applied.** Confirmed via `--dump-mlir-lowered` that **16 `unrealized_conversion_cast` ops survive all the way to the end** — real memref/vector-dialect values (from the new transform-produced code) sitting adjacent to, and needing to interoperate with, `!llvm.struct<(ptr,ptr,i64,...)>`-shaped values (the *old* pipeline's own already-lowered descriptor representation for the same logical memrefs) — inside the *same* function body (everything inlined into one, as established earlier in this same investigation). `--reconcile-unrealized-casts` (already run at the very end of cleave's existing pipeline) can only fold away a cast immediately canceled by its own converse — it has no way to unify two genuinely different representations of the same logical memref/vector concept across a real, extended live range or control-flow join, which is what's happening here: this new transform-dialect path and the rest of cleave's own pipeline lower the *same kind of value* two structurally different ways, and nothing currently reconciles them where they meet mid-function.

**Fourth attempt, same night: tested the "give it its own immediate, local, complete lowering" idea directly, rather than leaving it as an untested hypothesis.** Added the exact same four-pass sequence the pipeline's own end already uses (`create_vector_to_llvm`/`create_scf_to_control_flow`/`create_to_llvm`/`create_reconcile_unrealized_casts`) *immediately* after bufferization + `lower-vector-multi-reduction`, before any of the rest of the pipeline's own later stages ever run. **Byte-for-byte identical result — the same 16 `unrealized_conversion_cast` ops, the same exact types, the same crash.** This directly rules out "coexistence/timing with the rest of the pipeline" as the cause: running this exact conversion sequence immediately, in isolation from everything downstream, doesn't reconcile these casts either. Which means the earlier standalone `mlir-opt` probes that appeared to "succeed" (real `vector<16xf32>`-typed `llvm.fadd` chains, confirmed via text inspection) were an **incomplete check** — confirming the *output shape* looked right without ever confirming *zero unreconciled casts remained* or pushing all the way through a real LLVM translation/object-emission step. The real, precise culprit, found by direct inspection of exactly which casts survive: `vector<1x16x16xf32>` (a 3-D vector with a leading size-1 dimension, from vectorizing the `[1,16,0]`-tiled `i`/`j` loop) to `!llvm.array<1 x array<16 x vector<16xf32>>>` — genuinely N-D vector types, which `--convert-vector-to-llvm`/`--reconcile-unrealized-casts` apparently can't fully self-resolve in this shape, independent of where in the pipeline they run.

**Fifth attempt, same night: found and fixed the real, complete, correct pass sequence — confirmed via real LLVM translation, then confirmed running correctly end to end in cleave itself — and the core hypothesis still didn't pay off.** Root-caused the N-D-vector-to-LLVM gap precisely, on an isolated `mlir-opt` probe first (not cleave) this time, pushing all the way through `mlir-translate --mlir-to-llvmir` (the real check the previous round's own false positive had skipped): two genuinely missing passes, `--expand-strided-metadata` (turns `memref.subview`'s own dynamic offset/stride metadata into plain arithmetic — without it, `finalize-memref-to-llvm` has nothing it can convert) and `--lower-affine` right after (`expand-strided-metadata` itself emits `affine.apply`), plus `--convert-vector-to-scf` before `--convert-vector-to-llvm` (handles the genuinely N-D `vector<1x16x16xf32>` *load* shapes directly — the piece `canonicalize` alone, which only ever simplified the *reduction's own* combiner chain down to 1-D, was never going to touch). **Confirmed zero `unrealized_conversion_cast` survive, and a real, clean `mlir-translate` translation succeeds — the actual missing-pass list, complete.**

Wiring this exact sequence into cleave's real pipeline hit a long chain of small, real integration bugs, each found and fixed in turn rather than guessed around: two passes needed their own `register_*` call before being referencable by name (`canonicalize`, textually invisible until registered — melior's `PassManager::run` returns a real `Result<(), Error>` that had been silently discarded via `.is_err()` all night, hiding every one of these as an opaque, misleading "failed" with no message; fixed by actually propagating `Error`'s own text, which immediately turned several "mysterious" failures into a one-line, obvious diagnosis each), one registration accidentally dropped during a mid-session refactor. **Once all of it compiled and ran: a real, clean, zero-crash, zero-cast execution, correct output** (`2.0328503` vs the known-good `2.0327845` — a real, expected, small floating-point reassociation difference from the reduction now genuinely summing in tiled-block order rather than the original order, not a correctness bug).

**Measured: 3.69ms/call — still slightly worse than the untiled baseline (3.488ms), even with a fully correct, working implementation of the register-residency fix.** This is the real, sobering, important finding of the night: the reduction-accumulator memory round-trip was real and disassembly-confirmed, and is now genuinely fixed — but fixing it, on its own, does not close the gap. Whatever overhead this whole apparatus adds (extra loop levels, subview/stride bookkeeping, the `vector-to-scf` lowering path for the N-D load shapes) is roughly canceling out whatever memory-traffic saving it provides, at this exact problem size, on this exact machine. Reverted (`pipeline.rs`/`mlir_lower.rs` confirmed clean via `git diff`) — not shipped, since it's not a proven net win and forcibly routes every `linalg.generic` (not just matmul-shaped ones) through an under-tested path (the match criteria used tonight, `ops{["linalg.generic"]}`, would also incorrectly catch genuinely-elementwise generics in a real multi-op program — flagged earlier, never actually hit tonight only because every probe used was matmul-only).

**Sixth attempt, next session: reapplied the exact fifth-attempt pipeline and, this time, actually disassembled the real emitted object before reaching for `llvm-mca` — found the real explanation directly, no micro-architectural analysis needed.** `bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_matmul_local.cleave`, `--emit-object`, `llvm-objdump -d -r --x86-asm-syntax=intel`. Two real, distinct regressions, both confirmed directly from the object file's own instructions and relocations, not inferred:

1. **The matmul's own reduction loop lost all OpenMP parallelism.** The object contains exactly one `__kmpc_fork_call`/`omp_par` region (confirmed via `-r`'s relocation listing, since an unlinked `.o`'s indirect `call rax`/`call rbx` sites show no symbol name without it) — and its body is a `vxorps`+16×`vmovups zmm` unrolled zero-fill loop (the `linalg.fill` seed this same attempt introduced, `mlir_lower.rs`), not the matmul reduction at all. The real matmul body (`vmulps`/`vaddps` pairs on `zmm`, ~500 instructions, found further up in `<probe>` itself) is entirely inline in the main, single-threaded function — zero `__kmpc_*` calls anywhere near it. Root cause, precise: `--affine-parallelize{max-nested=1}` (the pass that marks a linalg-derived loop's outermost dimension `omp.parallel`-eligible) only ever sees `affine.for`, produced by `--convert-linalg-to-affine-loops` — but this attempt's own transform-dialect block consumes the matmul's `linalg.generic` *before* that pass ever runs (right before `one-shot-bufferize`, tiling+vectorizing it directly into `scf.for`), so by the time `--affine-parallelize` runs, there is no `linalg.generic`/`affine.for` left for it to mark. The `linalg.fill` seed is the only linalg-derived op left going through the *old* path, so it's the only thing that stayed parallel.
2. **The matmul's own FMA fusion, the thing the very first round of this investigation fixed, is gone too.** Zero packed `vfmadd*ps` anywhere in the object (`grep -c vfmadd`: 2 total, both scalar `vfmadd213ss`, both inside `Init::xavier`'s own RNG-fill loop, the same pre-existing, already-understood pattern from earlier in this investigation — not the matmul). The matmul body is 100% separate `vmulps`+`vaddps` pairs — exactly the un-fused shape the whole `mark_mulf_addf_contract` mechanism exists to prevent. Root cause, by pipeline position: `mark_mulf_addf_contract` (a hand-written module walk stamping `fastmath<contract>` on `arith.mulf`/`arith.addf` pairs) runs in its original position, late — right before `--affine-super-vectorize`, long after this attempt's transform-dialect `vectorize` step has already consumed and restructured the matmul's own `arith.mulf`/`arith.addf` pair into `vector.multi_reduction` — so by the time it walks the module, either the pair no longer exists in the shape it matches, or it's no longer positioned to matter; either way it never stamps what tonight's new lowering path actually produces.

**Both regressions have the same shape: two module-wide passes tuned for the pipeline's original stage ordering (`convert-linalg-to-affine-loops` → `affine-parallelize` → `mark_mulf_addf_contract` → `affine-super-vectorize`) silently do nothing for an op that this attempt moved out of that stage entirely, and neither pass fails loudly when it finds nothing to do — both just silently no-op.** This fully accounts for "3.69ms/call, not faster" without needing any micro-architectural explanation: the transform-dialect path fixed the one mechanism it targeted (the memory-round-trip) while *silently* giving up both 8-way parallelism and FMA fusion on the exact same op — regressions large enough on their own to swallow the fix's entire benefit, no port-pressure/latency analysis required to explain the flat result. `llvm-mca` was never the right next tool here; direct disassembly (what the user asked for directly, having noticed it hadn't been done yet) answered it in minutes.

Reverted again (`pipeline.rs`/`mlir_lower.rs`, `git checkout --`) — not shipped, same reasons as attempt five plus these two new ones. **Real next step, well-specified, not attempted**: either (a) make the transform-dialect path itself responsible for both properties it now silently drops — mark the outer (`i`) tiled loop `omp.parallel` directly as part of (or right after) the transform schedule, and stamp `fastmath<contract>` on the vectorized body's own arithmetic before or during vectorization rather than relying on the later module-wide walk to still find it — or (b) keep the *old* `affine-parallelize`/`mark_mulf_addf_contract`/`affine-super-vectorize` stage running exactly as today, and let the transform-dialect path only replace the final register-allocation-of-the-accumulator step within that stage, rather than removing the op from the stage entirely. Neither attempted this session.

**Same morning, option (a) done properly — both regressions fixed at the real root, confirmed via MLIR's own source and test suite, not guessed at:**

1. **Parallelism**: `transform.structured.tile_using_forall` (not `tile_using_for`) on the outer `i` dimension produces a real `scf.forall` directly — a construct whose own semantics *assert* iteration independence, no dependence analysis needed at all, sidestepping `--affine-parallelize`'s `affine.for`-only blind spot entirely. `--scf-forall-to-parallel` converts it to `scf.parallel`, feeding the *existing* `--convert-scf-to-openmp` machinery unchanged. One real subtlety, found the hard way: this pass only accepts a `scf.forall` with *zero results* (direct in-place memory writes) — running it *before* bufferize, while the `forall` still carries `shared_outs`/`tensor.parallel_insert_slice`, fails silently (exit 1, no diagnostic at all). Moved to run right *after* `one-shot-bufferize` instead — confirmed via real `mlir-translate`, then via the real compiled object (`__kmpc_fork_call`/`__kmpc_for_static_init_8u`/`__kmpc_barrier`, present).
2. **FMA**: root-caused precisely in MLIR's own source, not by more disassembly — `Vectorization.cpp::vectorizeAsLinalgContraction`: real `vector.contract` (the op with its own dedicated, FMA-friendly lowering) is only ever produced for a *named* structured op implementing `ContractionOpInterface` ("Generic op is ignored as not every arbitrary contraction body can be expressed by a vector.contract") — confirmed further by MLIR's own test suite, `contraction-interface.mlir`'s `@negative_generic`, `CHECK-NOT: vector.contract`, matching this project's exact indexing-map/body shape. `linalg.generic`, no matter how its body is written, can never qualify — only the real named `linalg.matmul` op can. `build_matmul_no_seed` switched from the old `linalg.index`/`arith.select` no-seed trick to a real `linalg.fill` seed + the real named `linalg.matmul` op (the no-seed trick's whole reason to exist — avoiding the fill's cost — was moot anyway, since `linalg.generic` was never going to reach `vector.contract` regardless of its body). `transform.structured.vectorize` needs `{create_named_contraction}` passed explicitly too (silently falls back to the generic path otherwise, confirmed on a real test case matching this exact shape) — then `transform.apply_patterns.vector.lower_contraction lowering_strategy = "outerproduct"` (real MLIR idiom, `vector-contract-to-outerproduct-matmul-transforms.mlir`) turns the resulting `vector.contract` into a `vector.outerproduct` chain. Confirmed via real disassembly: `vfmadd132ps`/`vbroadcastss`, the textbook GEMM microkernel — zero `vpermt2ps`/`vinsertps` shuffle pile.

**Real kernel has four differently-shaped layers (`784→512→256→128→10`) — the fixed-tile-size schedule above breaks outright on the `128→10` layer (`j=10 < 16`, not evenly tileable): `error: Attempted to vectorize, but failed`, a hard pipeline-wide failure, not a partial result.** Fixed via `transform.foreach_match` (matches each `linalg.matmul` independently) wrapping the tile+vectorize action, the whole thing wrapped in `transform.sequence ... failures(suppress)` — confirmed directly on a two-shape probe (`bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_two_shapes_*.mlir`): the divisible shape vectorizes fully (real `vector.outerproduct`), the non-divisible one is left exactly where tiling got it (still a real, named `linalg.matmul`, partially tiled with a dynamic remainder dim, never corrupted) — falling through cleanly to the *old* `affine-super-vectorize` path for that one shape, same as before this transform-dialect stage existed. `foreach_match` itself still reports an overall silenceable failure once any one action fails (postponed to the end of its own walk, not per-op) — `failures(suppress)` on the wrapping `transform.sequence` is what keeps that from aborting the rest of `pipeline.rs`'s own `pass_manager.run().is_err()` check.

**Wired into the real pipeline (`pipeline.rs`, `mlir_lower.rs::build_matmul_no_seed`) — real end-to-end build of `examples/mnist-interop` succeeds, correct output, both fixes confirmed live in the real compiled kernel** (`llvm-objdump` on the real `kernel.o`: 32 `vfmadd132ps` + `__kmpc_fork_call`/`__kmpc_for_static_init_8u`/`__kmpc_for_static_fini`/`__kmpc_barrier`, all present). One real integration bug found along the way: `dps_rewrite.rs`'s own `match_candidate`/`rewrite_one` only recognized the *old* no-seed `linalg.generic` shape for redirecting a matmul's own destination directly into a struct field's storage — the real `linalg.matmul`+`linalg.fill` shape needs a *different* redirect target (the `linalg.fill`'s own `outs`, not the matmul's — a real named-op reduction genuinely reads its own accumulator, so redirecting *its* `outs` straight to uninitialized struct storage would silently corrupt the reduction with garbage, exactly the bug this rewrite's own history already hit once before). `Strategy::Overwrite` generalized to carry an explicit `redirect_op` (the matmul itself for the `linalg.generic` case, the feeding `linalg.fill` for the new `linalg.matmul` case) — every insertion anchor in `rewrite_one` moved from `candidate.producer` to `redirect_op` accordingly (dominance-critical: `redirect_op` can now sit *before* `producer` in program order). Full workspace test suite green after.

**Isolated single-matmul throughput: 1.644ms/call vs the 3.488ms/call baseline — ~2.12× faster, ~15.6 GFLOP/s (up from 7.37).** The first real, unambiguous net win of the whole investigation, on the representative shape.

## A second, completely independent bottleneck found and fixed the same morning — a per-element scalar copy dominating half the real kernel's own compiled code — but net wall-clock still doesn't move

Disassembling the *real*, full `kernel.o` for the first time all investigation (previously only ever isolated single-matmul probes) turned up something the matmul work never touched: **216,040 static `vmovss` instructions — very close to half the entire kernel's own disassembly (223,318 lines total) — a fully compile-time-unrolled scalar element-by-element copy, no loop at all.** Traced precisely to `mlir_lower.rs::lower_tagged_struct_construct` (`Tensor::<T,Dims...>(data: expr)` construction — every call, e.g. `load_train_batch_input`'s own `Tensor::<f32,32,784>(data: pixels)`, called ~18,750 times over a full 10-epoch run) and `copy_nested_array` (nested array literal/repeat construction) — both walked every scalar element by hand (`memref.load`+`memref.store`/`tensor.from_elements`, one op per element, fully unrolled at Rust-construction time — cleave has no dynamically-sized arrays, so nothing stopped this from scaling with total element count).

Fixed by replacing the per-element walk with real, structured bulk ops MLIR can actually reason about and optimize, instead of throwing that structure away up front:
- `lower_tagged_struct_construct`: a defensive `memref.alloc`+`memref.copy` (safe regardless of aliasing, unlike casting `src` directly with `restrict`'s own "exclusively owned" promise — the exact danger `load_native_shape_field`'s own doc comment already found once, for a different call site) followed by one `bufferization.to_tensor ... restrict` — one real op instead of N scalar loads, letting One-Shot Bufferize's own copy-elision remove the copy later if it can prove it was unnecessary.
- `copy_nested_array`: one `memref.subview` (fully static offset/strides — every real call site fixes exactly one compile-time-constant leading index) + one `memref.copy` instead of a recursive per-element walk. `memref.subview`'s own verifier rejects a plain identity-layout result type here (a rank-reduced subview's own physical offset into the bigger buffer needs a real `strided<[...], offset: N>` layout spelled out by hand — confirmed directly, `cargo test`'s own real suite caught the mismatch immediately).

A real, separate integration cost: every hand-rolled test-file pipeline that lowers all the way to the `llvm` dialect (`dps_rewrite.rs`, `mlir_lower.rs`, `egraph.rs`, `unify_alloc.rs`, `user_guide.rs`) needed `--expand-strided-metadata`/`--lower-affine` added (`pipeline.rs`'s own real final-lowering stage already had them, from the matmul work above) — and, found isolated to a completely unrelated, pre-existing case (`user_guide.rs`'s own `array_literal_index_and_mutation`, a plain `a[0] = 10`, nothing to do with either fix): `--finalize-memref-to-llvm` run once, up front, before any `--convert-to-llvm` at all, leaves a genuinely unreconcilable `i64`-to-`index`-to-`i64` round trip behind on ordinary, non-subview `index`-typed constants — fixed by running `--convert-to-llvm`, *then* `--finalize-memref-to-llvm`, *then* `--convert-to-llvm` again, confirmed by testing the ordering both ways on the exact failing case. Full workspace test suite green after (all of the above, plus the matmul/`dps_rewrite.rs` fix together).

**Real object-file confirmation: `examples/mnist-interop`'s own `kernel.o` shrank from 1.97MB to 126KB** (~15.6×) — the unrolled copy genuinely gone from the compiled kernel, not just from an isolated probe.

**Real end-to-end result, both fixes active together: 83.0s** (`cargo run --release -p mnist-interop`, identical `test accuracy: 0.9342` confirming the identical kernel; an earlier same-session measurement read 90.1s — run right after a heavy background rebuild+full-test-suite pass on the same machine, most likely ordinary system-load noise, not a real difference: `kernel.o` itself is byte-identical between the two measurements) **against the 96.89s baseline — a real, if modest, ~14% end-to-end improvement**, despite two real, independently-validated architectural fixes that each measured far larger *in isolation* (a measured 2.12× per-call matmul speedup, and a measured 15.6× reduction in a different function's own compiled size). The honest conclusion, not spun: ~14% is real progress, but nowhere near what either fix's own isolated measurement would predict if it were the dominant cost — something else, still unidentified, accounts for most of the remaining gap. Something else — the backward pass (different matmul shapes, transposed, never isolated or measured), the three smaller layers (never measured independently; the `128→10` layer specifically still takes the *old*, unfixed `affine-super-vectorize` path via the `foreach_match`/`suppress` fallback above), `cleave_alloc_rc` malloc/free churn, OpenMP synchronization overhead, or something not yet hypothesized at all — dominates the remaining ~83 seconds. Guessing which, again, is exactly the trap this whole investigation has already fallen into and climbed back out of more than once.

**Real next step, not yet attempted, user-directed**: stop reasoning from isolated probes and hypotheses entirely — get real, whole-kernel profiling data. `llvm-mca` (present in this toolchain, `I:/Dev/llvm-mlir-22/bin/llvm-mca.exe`, flagged once before and skipped in favor of direct disassembly, which *did* find real things that time — but a static micro-architectural port/latency model is a different question than "where do the cycles actually go" across the *whole* kernel) alongside a real hardware-counter profile (VTune or AMD uProf — noted directly: AMD's own tooling has a real, poor reputation, worth confirming VTune still works reasonably on non-Intel hardware or accepting uProf's rougher edges rather than assuming either tool's output is trustworthy at face value).

**Update, same morning: a real, large, independent lever found — SMT/AVX-512 execution-unit contention, not anything about the kernel's own IR at all.** This machine (`AMD Ryzen 7 9700X`, confirmed via `Get-CimInstance Win32_Processor`) has 8 real physical cores, 16 logical (SMT) — libomp's own default thread count, with `OMP_NUM_THREADS` unset, follows the OS's *logical* processor count, spawning 16 OpenMP worker threads across only 8 physical cores' worth of execution ports. Two AVX-512-bound threads sharing one physical core's own vector ALU is a well-known anti-pattern for pure vector/FP-bound code — confirmed directly, not just theorized, and independently reproduced on two machines (two ~71.0s runs, essentially identical): `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8` (matching physical cores, not logical) measured **71.0s**, against **83–86s** with the default (16) — a real ~15-17% improvement on top of everything else this session already found.

**Correction, caught directly by the user, not self-noticed — a comparison drafted here at one point wrongly called `96.89s` "the PyTorch-CPU baseline": it is not.** `96.89s` is *cleave's own* original, pre-this-session baseline (this entry's very first paragraph, `--openmp` AOT build, before any of this session's fixes) — the real PyTorch-CPU number is **~7.6-7.9s** (`bench/mnist-pytorch/mnist_bench.py`, ~0.77-0.79s/epoch, 93.0% accuracy — measured three separate times across this session, on two different machines: 7.65s, 7.81s, 7.94s, genuinely stable, not cherry-picked). So, the real, correct comparison: **`96.89s → 71.0s` is a real ~27% improvement in cleave's own throughput, against *itself* — but cleave, even now, is still `71.0s / 7.8s` ≈ **~9× slower than PyTorch-CPU**, not faster.** The `12.7×` gap this entry opened with narrows to `~9×` — real, measured progress, but the gap this whole entry exists to close is not closed.

**Real next step, well-specified, not yet attempted**: `cleave-rt`'s own OpenMP runtime initialization should default to the machine's real *physical* core count, not whatever the OS/libomp's own default reports (logical/SMT count) — on Windows, `GetLogicalProcessorInformationEx` with `RelationProcessorCore` gives exactly this distinction; call `omp_set_num_threads()` with that count at startup rather than relying on libomp's own default. Needs a real, portable equivalent for whatever other platforms `cleave-rt` targets (Linux: `/sys/devices/system/cpu/cpu*/topology/thread_siblings`-style parsing or `sched_getaffinity`+`/proc/cpuinfo`'s own `core id`/`physical id` fields, not just `nproc`) — not attempted here, Windows-only confirmed so far. A cruder, zero-code fallback in the meantime: document that `OMP_NUM_THREADS` should be set explicitly to the real physical core count on any SMT machine, since libomp already respects it correctly once set.

**Real VTune data, same morning, confirms the deeper "too many small parallel regions" theory directly, not just from `kernel.cleave`'s own old doc-comment prediction.** `result.csv` (VTune, real hardware counters, not a guess): OpenMP worker threads spend **966.184 of 966.184 CPU-seconds inside `_kmp_launch_worker`'s own internal wait/spin function — essentially 100% of their entire existence, doing zero identifiable real work.** The *master* thread fares only somewhat better: of `_kmp_fork_call`'s own 734.037 CPU-seconds, `_kmpc_barrier` alone accounts for 462.409 (63%) — real compute (`train_and_evaluate`'s own self time inside the fork) is only 268.958. The signature is unambiguous: many short parallel regions, workers spending nearly all their time waiting to be given work or waiting at the join barrier, not computing.

**Went looking for a real, sanctioned MLIR mechanism to fix this the same way `vector.contract`/`ContractionOpInterface` was found for the FMA problem — found one, tested it directly, and it turned out to be a real dead end for a precise, now-understood reason.** `--scf-parallel-loop-fusion` (`mlir::scf::naivelyFuseParallelOps`, real existing MLIR pass, confirmed by reading its own source directly, `mlir/lib/Dialect/SCF/Transforms/ParallelLoopFusion.cpp`): fuses *adjacent* `scf.parallel` ops sharing the exact same iteration space (real SSA-value equality on lower/upper bound/step, not just equal numerically) into one, which — once `--convert-scf-to-openmp` runs afterward — would fold what were N separate `omp.parallel` fork/join regions into one. Added right after `--scf-forall-to-parallel` (preceded by `--cse`, to give every matmul's own identical `32`-wide batch bound a real chance to already be the same SSA value), and tested directly on the real kernel: **zero effect — `kernel.o` came out byte-identical (125,907 bytes), fork-call count unchanged (46 → 47, noise).** Root cause, confirmed by dumping the pre-fusion IR directly rather than guessing: the pass's own adjacency requirement (*zero* real side-effecting ops between two `scf.parallel` loops in the same block) is never satisfied here — **`Broadcast0::broadcast0` (`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`, `dense_forward`'s own per-layer bias broadcast) sits between every consecutive pair of matmuls, and it's a real, hand-written, genuinely scalar `for`-loop** (`for i in 0..B { for _k in 0..M { data[i,j] = r[0,j]; ... } }`, confirmed live in the pre-fusion dump as a real `scf.while`/`memref.load`+`memref.store` nest, not unrolled at compile time the way tonight's earlier fix targeted, but still O(B×M) real scalar memory traffic at runtime, zero vectorization) — a real side effect (`memref.store`) standing directly between every layer's own matmul and the next, breaking the fusion chain at every single boundary, every layer, every batch.

**This is the same family of bug as tonight's two earlier fixes (`lower_tagged_struct_construct`/`copy_nested_array`), one level up: a genuinely structured operation (a row broadcast along a new leading dimension — exactly `linalg.generic`'s own standard idiom, an indexing map that drops a dimension on the input side) hand-written instead as a scalar loop, this time at the *stdlib* level (`nn.cleave`), not inside the compiler.** `Broadcast0::reduce0` (the adjoint — summing back down across the batch dimension for the bias gradient) is the same shape, a real reduction expressible as a `linalg.generic` with a `"reduction"` iterator, currently a hand-rolled scalar accumulator loop instead. `Sum::sum` (`nn.cleave`, rank-2 total-reduction) already has an identical hand-rolled scalar loop, for an unrelated, already-documented reason (`doc/backlog.md`'s own const-generic-conflation bug forcing a manual counter) — worth checking whether it has the exact same fusion-blocking effect anywhere it's used adjacent to a matmul.

**Done, same morning — real, dedicated primitives, not a hardcoded compiler special case: `mlir_lower.rs::build_broadcast0`/`build_reduce0`, dispatched off `mlir::linalg::broadcast(r)`/`mlir::linalg::reduce(b)` the exact same way `matmul`/`transpose` already dispatch off `mlir::linalg::matmul`/`mlir::linalg::transpose` — `Extensibility discipline` followed, not bypassed.** The real *named* `linalg.broadcast`/`linalg.reduce` ops (confirmed real and current in `LinalgStructuredOps.td`) were tried first and abandoned: `Tensor<T,1,M>` is rank-2 (a literal size-1 leading dim), so reaching either named op's own expected rank needs a real `tensor.collapse_shape`/`tensor.expand_shape` first — confirmed directly, on an isolated probe, that the reshaped memref gets a genuinely non-trivial `strided<...>` layout after bufferization (even though the collapse here is always statically contiguous — MLIR's own bufferization doesn't prove that on its own), and `--affine-super-vectorize` refuses to touch it ("NYI: non-trivial layout map", the identical diagnostic the already-documented `export fn`-Tensor-parameter case hits, from a different real cause this time). Fixed by hand-building a `linalg.generic` instead — reading `r[0,j]` via a constant-`0` affine map on the batch axis for broadcast, reducing via a real `"reduction"` iterator for `reduce0` — sidestepping the reshape (and its layout problem) entirely. Confirmed on an isolated, *locally-constructed* probe (matching how the real kernel actually presents these values, not as raw exported-function parameters, which would hit the unrelated, already-known limitation): a clean, real, 16-wide masked-vector store loop for broadcast and a genuine `vector<16xf32>` `llvm.fadd` chain for the reduction, zero scalar code, zero unrealized casts, confirmed all the way through to real LLVM-dialect output. `stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`'s own `Broadcast0::broadcast0`/`reduce0` are now one-line calls to these primitives. Full workspace test suite green after, including the existing `broadcast0_replicates_a_row_and_reduce0_sums_it_back_down` correctness test.

**Real end-to-end result, `examples/mnist-interop`, this fix alone (on top of everything else already landed this session): 50.0s** — down from ~83-86s, confirmed independently on two machines. **Combined with the `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8` SMT fix (`doc/backlog.md`'s own entry above): 41.2s, confirmed independently on two machines a second time.** Against the `96.89s` original baseline: **~2.35× faster.** Against the real PyTorch-CPU baseline (~7.6-7.9s): **~5.3× slower — down from the original 12.7×, the gap more than halved.** `Sum::sum`/`Sum::broadcast` (flagged above as worth auditing for the identical shape) not yet revisited — real next step, not yet attempted.

**Real llvm-mca analysis, same morning, on the real compiled hot loop (`train_and_evaluate..omp_par.10`, extracted directly from the real `kernel.o` via `llvm-objdump`, run through `llvm-mca -mcpu=znver5` — this machine's own real CPU model, `Host CPU: znver5` confirmed) — quantifies a real, separate, still fully open bottleneck, distinct from vectorization quality and from `Broadcast0`.** `Block RThroughput: 16.0` cycles/iteration (what the execution ports alone would allow) vs `Total Cycles / Iterations` measured at **64 cycles/iteration — 4× worse**. `llvm-mca`'s own bottleneck analysis attributes this precisely: `Resource Pressure 0.29%` (the FP ports are barely contended at all) against `Data Dependencies 75.57%` (`Register Dependencies`, specifically) — the real accumulator-register dependency chain (`zmm0→zmm1→zmm2→zmm0→zmm1→zmm3→...`, the critical-path trace shows it explicitly) only rotates through **4 independent registers**, so each `vfmadd132ps` (11-cycle latency) must wait on its own predecessor almost every step — a genuine latency-bound loop, not a throughput-bound one. Real next step, well-specified, not yet attempted: widen the reduction's own independent-accumulator count in `cleave/mlir/matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`'s own schedule (more parallel partial sums per `k`-tile, combined only at the end — "unroll and jam" the reduction) to give the CPU more independent work to overlap while any one FMA's own latency is still in flight.

**Real VTune re-check, same morning, after the broadcast0/reduce0 fix: OpenMP workers are still spinning hard — confirmed directly, not assumed to have improved just because wall-clock did.** Quantified precisely with a clean serial-vs-parallel A/B on the identical, fully-fixed binary: `OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` measures **94.45s**; `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8` measures **41.2s** — a real **2.29× speedup from 8× the cores, ~28.6% parallel efficiency**. Ideal 8-core scaling would land near `94.45 / 8 ≈ 11.8s` — **a further ~3.5× still on the table**, entirely independent of everything fixed this session (which made the *work itself* faster, on both the serial and parallel runs alike, without touching how *efficiently* it's spread across cores at all).

**Re-ran `--scf-parallel-loop-fusion` directly (not just theorized about it) now that `Broadcast0` no longer blocks it — still a complete no-op, `kernel.o` byte-identical, fork-call count still 46 — and this time the real cause is structural, not another one-off bug to fix.** Dumped the pre-fusion IR again: between every consecutive matmul pair now sit exactly two ops — the bias-add (`Ring::add`, `dense_forward`'s own `matmul + broadcast(bias)`) and `Activation::relu` — both real, legitimate, *already-structured* `linalg.generic` ops (not a hand-rolled scalar loop this time, nothing "wrong" with them the way `Broadcast0` was). The real reason fusion still can't see across them: they are **not yet `scf.parallel`-shaped at the point this pass runs** — the matmul's own outer dimension becomes `scf.parallel` early, via the transform-dialect schedule (`cleave/mlir/matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`), while the elementwise bias-add/relu only become thread-parallel much later, via the *old* `--convert-linalg-to-affine-loops`+`--affine-parallelize` path — two different parallelization strategies, running at two different pipeline stages, never simultaneously `scf.parallel` at any one point for the naive adjacency-based fusion to catch.

**Tried the well-specified next step directly, same morning: extended `cleave/mlir/matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`'s own schedule to also tile the elementwise `linalg.generic` ops (bias-add, `relu`) the same way as matmul's own outer dimension — real, correct, and confirmed safe, but still not the fusion win it was meant to unlock.** `transform.structured.match ops{["linalg.generic"]} attributes{iterator_types = [parallel, parallel]} in %new_root` (filtered specifically to exclude `Broadcast0::reduce0`'s own `[reduction, parallel]` shape — tiling a genuine reduction dimension as an independent `forall` would race concurrent writes to the same accumulator with no combining, a real correctness bug, not just a missed optimization; confirmed the filter correctly discriminates on an isolated three-shape probe, `bench/mnist-pytorch/probe_ew_*.mlir`, before touching the real kernel at all) followed by `transform.structured.tile_using_forall %ew tile_sizes [1, 0]` — both ops now genuinely `scf.forall`-shaped at the matmul's own pipeline stage, confirmed directly in the pre-fusion dump.

**`--scf-parallel-loop-fusion` still doesn't fire — and this time it's a real, structural limit of the pass itself, not something a schedule change can route around.** Dumped the pre-fusion IR a third time: between the matmul's own `scf.parallel` and the bias-add's, there is now genuinely *nothing* extraneous except an `llvm.load` (reading the bias tensor's own struct-field descriptor) and a `memref.alloc()` (the bias-add's own fresh destination buffer) — both real, necessary, unavoidable side effects, not leftover cruft. `ParallelLoopFusion.cpp`'s own source has an honest `// TODO: Handle region side effects properly` on exactly this limitation — the pass's adjacency check is a blunt "zero side effects, full stop", with no concept of a "safe" side effect (an allocation of a fresh, non-aliasing buffer; a load of an already-computed, immutable value) that could soundly be hoisted across or tolerated. Measured directly rather than assumed to be neutral: real end-to-end wall-clock **42.15s — statistically identical to the 41.1-41.2s already landed, no regression but no win either**, despite the static fork-call count rising from 46 to 61 (bias-add/relu now fork *separately*, with no compensating fusion). **Reverted** (`pipeline.rs`/`cleave/mlir/matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`, confirmed clean via `git diff`) — not a proven improvement, real added complexity and binary size for no measured benefit.

**Status: the `~3.5×` still-open gap (`OMP_NUM_THREADS=1` vs `=8`, entry above) is real and unclosed by this attempt.** Closing it for real needs one of two genuinely bigger efforts, neither attempted: (a) a custom fusion pass (or a real MLIR upstream contribution) that tolerates the specific *safe* side-effect shapes this kernel's own IR actually produces — fresh, provably non-aliasing allocations and loads of values with no intervening writes — rather than patching `naivelyFuseParallelOps`'s own blanket conservatism; or (b) abandon post-hoc fusion entirely and restructure the transform schedule to build *one* `scf.forall` around the *whole* per-layer chain (matmul → bias-add → relu → next matmul) from the start, with the allocation/load steps happening *inside* that one region's own body (each thread doing its own row's worth of the whole chain), rather than tiling each op independently and hoping a later pass can stitch the pieces back together. (b) is the more natural fit for how a hand-tuned kernel (PyTorch's own, the standard this session keeps measuring against) is actually structured — worth real design time before another quick pass-insertion attempt.

## Option (b) above, actually built: `transform.structured.fuse_into_containing_op`-based region fusion — done, three real bugs found and fixed along the way, net result a wash on this machine (matches baseline, doesn't beat it)

User's own explicit direction, next session: "plutot b pour commencer" — rejecting (a) (patching/extending `--scf-parallel-loop-fusion`) as too fragile, building (b) instead.

**Prerequisite, done first, following the extensibility discipline exactly (same pattern as `Broadcast0::broadcast0`/`reduce0` above): `Ring<Tensor<T,Dims...>>::add/sub/mul/div` (`stdlib/linalg/tensor.cleave`) and `Activation<Tensor<T,Dims...>>::relu` (`stdlib/nn/nn.cleave`) now build a real, rank-generic `linalg.generic` (`mlir_lower.rs::build_elementwise_binop`/`build_relu_elemwise`, dispatched off new `mlir::linalg::elementwise::{addf,subf,mulf,divf}`/`mlir::linalg::relu` primitives) instead of a plain `arith.addf`/`subf`/`mulf`/`divf`/`maximumf` applied directly to the whole `tensor<...>` operand.** Necessary, not cosmetic: a plain `arith.*` op on a tensor never implements `TilingInterface` in this LLVM build (confirmed directly — no external model registered anywhere under `mlir/lib/Dialect/Arith/`), so neither `fuse_into_containing_op` nor any other tile/fuse transform can ever touch it. `linalg.generic` does. `neg` deliberately left as plain `mlir::arith::negf` — not part of any fusion target, no reason to touch it yet. Confirmed neutral on its own, isolated from the fusion schedule change: `OMP_NUM_THREADS=8` with this stdlib change alone (old, matmul-only schedule still in place) measured **41.8s** — statistically identical to the `41.2s` already landed. Full workspace test suite green after, including every `Tensor`-arithmetic/`relu`/`dense_forward`/`grad_through_dense_forward` test.

**The schedule itself (`cleave/mlir/matmul_vectorize.transform.mlir`), real design, validated on isolated `mlir-opt` probes before ever touching the real kernel (the same discipline as every other change this session): a new `@match_matmul`/`@fuse_and_vectorize` stage runs *before* the existing, unchanged, matmul-only `@tile_and_vectorize` (now "Stage 2," a genuine fallback, not removed).** For each `linalg.matmul` whose result has *exactly one* real consumer and that consumer is a plain `linalg.generic` (the shape a whole forward-pass layer chain — `matmul -> bias-add -> [relu]` — collapses down to by the time this stage runs, confirmed directly by dumping this project's own real lowered IR: `--linalg-fuse-elementwise-ops`, already running earlier in `pipeline.rs`, on `linalg.generic`-shaped `Ring::add`/`Activation::relu` now that they're real structured ops, already fuses the *whole* elementwise epilogue chain into one op on its own, no new mechanism needed for that half): tile the epilogue on its own outer (batch) dimension via `tile_using_forall`, `fuse_into_containing_op` the matmul into the resulting `scf.forall` as a producer, then apply the matmul's own existing J/K tile+vectorize-to-`vector.contract` recipe to the *fused* handle. Net effect where it fires: the epilogue shares the matmul's own `scf.forall` (one OpenMP region per layer chain) instead of getting its own, separate one from the *old* `--affine-super-vectorize` fallback path.

**Three real bugs found chasing this from "works on an isolated probe" to "works, and doesn't regress, on the real kernel" — each found by tracing the real kernel directly, never guessed:**
