Tier 3's own light structs read a field via `llvm.extractvalue` instead (no memory access, a bare SSA aggregate field) — structurally equivalent for this branch's own purpose (an unmodified read, safe to redirect to a `cleave_retain`), just never taught to recognize it. **The actual fix is a two-line change to an existing check** (`if !op_name_is(read_descriptor, "llvm.load") && !op_name_is(read_descriptor, "llvm.extractvalue")`) — not a new, separate mechanism at all; the earlier, non-working attempt had built a whole second top-level branch assuming the wrong (final-stage) chain shape, now removed.

**Measured directly, before and after, same real kernel, same forced-clean rebuild methodology**: `dps_rewrite`'s own candidate count went from `16` to `24` (`8` new `Strategy::Passthrough` matches, one per layer's own weight/bias shape, `elems=401408` — exactly `w1` — among them). Real object-level bytes-copied-per-iteration (the same `llvm-objdump -dr` size-tracing method, summing every in-loop `memcpy`'s own real size): **`1,827,392 -> 218,624` bytes per training sample, a `~8.4×` reduction** — the dominant `1.6MB` in-loop copy is gone from the list entirely (the *one-time*, pre-loop network-initialization occurrence of the identical size remains, correctly — that one is a genuine one-time cost, not a per-iteration one).

**Verified**: full `cargo test -p cleave --release --no-fail-fast` suite green (`dps_rewrite.rs`'s own 8 tests included, unchanged); real, forced-clean AOT rebuilds — `digits-interop` — `0.94713414`, unchanged; `mnist-interop` — 10 real epochs, `0.9342`, bit-identical, no crash. Every temporary probe/dump/counter used to reach this conclusion (three `melior`-only block-walk tests, two separate `pipeline.rs`-side module dumps, several `dps_rewrite.rs`-side debug counters) removed after use — `git status`/`git diff` confirmed clean of anything but the real two-line `Strategy::Passthrough` fix.

Wall-clock impact not yet measured by the user on a quiet machine — the natural next data point, matching this project's own established practice of treating the user's own timing as authoritative.

## A real `aliases_ret_param` false positive fixed (any struct-returning function computing a genuinely new value of a type matching one of its own arguments) — a second, deeper "Scale::scale" bare-tensor leak found alongside it, root-caused precisely, and set aside rather than fixed

Raised as one of two prioritized bugs (`doc/backlog.md`'s own "net_grad bridge + Scale::scale leak" entry at the time) from the parallelism/VTune investigation, alternating codegen-quality and language-robustness work by agreement — deliberately started with "the simpler one."

**The fix, real and landed**: `rewrite_body`'s own `Fix` arm decides whether a transferred literal call argument is "the exact same allocation the call already hands back as its own return value" (needed to avoid a double-release — the original `println`/`Print::print` double-free crash, `backlog-done.md`'s own pool-allocator entry) by guessing from *type* equality alone (`resumption_ret_tys.iter().any(|rt| *rt == ty)`). Confirmed, by direct testing, to be a real false positive: any ordinary function computing a genuinely *new* value of the same type as one of its own arguments (`fn combine(a: Boxed, b: Boxed) -> Boxed` computing a fresh `Boxed` from both) gets wrongly treated as if it handed `a`/`b` straight back, silently dropping the seed and leaking both.

**Replaced with a real, decidable fact instead of a type coincidence**: `collect_identity_param_fns` (`refcount.rs`) computes, once per whole program via a small fixpoint, which top-level functions unconditionally return one of their own parameters *unchanged* on every path — `Print<(A,B)>::print`/`println` (`stdlib/io/io.cleave`) are the real instances, `println` only identity *transitively* through its own call to `print` (hence the fixpoint, not one linear pass: `println<T>(x) { let r = print(x); print(['\n']); r }`). The walk (`identity_param_index`/`walk_identity_return`) recurses through every real CPS control-flow shape (`LetPrim`'s own continuation, both `If` branches, a `Fix`'s own body and every one of its local defs), and — the one genuinely new piece — grows its own "known alias" set as it descends past a call to an *already-proven* identity function, so a resumption's own parameter correctly inherits its caller's identity fact. `rewrite_body`'s own `aliases_ret_param` now resolves the callee's name from the call site and looks up this real fact instead of comparing types.

**Verified**: two dedicated regression tests added (`tests/refcount.rs`) — one confirms the original `println` double-release crash stays fixed (`a_struct_passed_to_a_genuinely_identity_shaped_function_is_released_only_once`), one confirms the newly-found struct leak is closed (`a_transferred_call_result_matching_the_final_calls_own_return_type_is_still_released`). Both verified meaningful by temporarily reverting the fix (`git stash`) and confirming the leak test fails as expected (`1` release instead of `3`) while the crash test still passes even against the old code (the old, coarser heuristic happens to also cover the identity case correctly — expected, and itself worth keeping the dedicated test for, against a future "just delete the check" regression). Both tests needed a struct with an embedded array field (`tag: [i32; 1]`) to avoid the unrelated "light struct" optimization eliding all `Retain`/`Release` calls for an all-scalar struct — and needed a helper that skips `optimize_program` entirely (`refcounted_cps_unoptimized`), found necessary after discovering the e-graph's own "construct-then-read-the-same-field" transparency (`backlog-done.md`'s own "Multi-level call transparency" entry) folds a plain scalar `struct`-returning helper's own call away *before* `insert_refcounting` ever sees it — a real, separate, correctly-intentional optimization that would otherwise silently defeat a test aimed at the un-inlined call itself. Full `cargo test -p cleave --release --no-fail-fast` suite green; `digits-interop`/`mnist-interop` both re-run end to end, `0.94713414`/`0.9342`, unchanged.

**The second, deeper bug found investigating this — real, precisely traced on the actual kernel, but set aside, not fixed.** Checking the fix's effect on `mnist-interop`'s own real `cleave_release` count (`--dump-cps-optimized`, before/after) showed **zero difference** — surprising, since `Scale::scale(lr, grad)` fed into `Ring::sub` inside `Sgd`'s own `Optimizer::step` (`stdlib/optim/optim.cleave:57`) is exactly the shape this whole investigation started from. Traced precisely: the intermediate scaled tensor (`v2208` in a real dump) appears exactly twice in the whole function — bound as `Scale::scale`'s own resumption parameter, then passed to `Ring::sub` — no `release` anywhere. Root cause is **not** the aliasing heuristic above (a bare tensor never even reaches it): `rewrite_body`'s own `Fix` arm gates seeding into `owned` at all on `needs_seed = ctx.is_rc(ty) || !ctx.light_release_leaves(ty).is_empty()`, and `is_rc(Tensor<...>)` is `false` by design — a tensor's own liberation was only ever wired through a *containing struct's* own release cascade (`mlir_lower.rs`), never through a bare, unwrapped tensor variable's own binding.

Real, existing evidence a previous session already got partway toward fixing exactly this, and reverted: a dead `is_bare_tensor_ty` helper and a matching, unreachable branch in `wrap_releases` (both removed as part of this same investigation, per the user's own direction to clean up dead code once a bug is set aside rather than fixed) already correctly emitted a release for a bare tensor *once something seeds it* — but nothing ever called it from `needs_seed` itself, so that code never fired. The missing piece looks small (add the same check to `needs_seed`), but the last attempt at seeding a bare tensor here produced a real, never-root-caused `STATUS_ACCESS_VIOLATION` (`doc/backlog.md`'s own account at the time), and no reliable minimal repro exists for this session's own attempt either — a leak, not a crash, on a value whose whole lifetime is one training step, real but low real-world impact absent an actual OOM. **Set aside on that basis, by explicit decision**: a bug with no reliable repro and no observed crash isn't worth the risk of another blind attempt at a mechanism that's already crashed once for an unknown reason — revisit if a real repro or a concrete impact (measured memory growth, an actual OOM) ever surfaces.
